Medieval India


- Preface
    - Reassessing India’s Medieval Past
        - As a nation progresses, it reviews its history to determine:
            - Relevant legacies for growth
            - Inhibiting factors that may hinder progress
        - Medieval Indian history was traditionally seen as:
            - A period dominated by Turkish and Mughal rule
            - Primarily focused on political events rather than societal changes
        - This perspective has changed as historians now emphasize:
            - The evolution from tribe-based societies to territorial states
            - The gradual formation of classes and castes within this system
    
    - Emergence of a New Social Order
        - With the rise of land ownership and control over cultivators:
            - A feudal mode of society developed in India
        - However, key differences existed between:
            - Indian feudalism
            - European feudalism
    
    - Scope of This Book
        - This book traces key developments in India between the 8th and 17th centuries, covering:
            - Social trends
            - Economic growth
            - Political evolution
            - Cultural advancements
        - Brings together decades of historical research to offer:
            - A new orientation to medieval Indian history
            - Better context for debates on:
                - The nature of medieval Indian states
                - The level of religious freedom
                - Economic trends of the period
    
    - Debunking Historical Misconceptions
        - Challenges the notion that Indian history is simply the rise and fall of empires
        - Emphasizes the continuous institutional and cultural growth despite:
            - The breakdown of large empires into smaller states
            - The resurgence of centralized power later
        - Notes how economic and cultural exchange between regional and central states remained active
    
    - Final Thoughts
        - The Mughal era saw a triumph of growth over disintegration
        - The book does not cover:
            - The 18th-century decline of Mughal power
            - British rule and its consequences, which shaped India’s later history


- India and the World
    - Changes in India and the World (8th–18th Century)
        - Global Transformations
            - The period from the 8th to the 18th century witnessed significant changes worldwide.
            - Both Europe and Asia saw the emergence of new:
                - Social structures
                - Political systems
            - These transformations influenced the thoughts and lifestyles of people.

        - Impact on India
            - India, with its long-standing trade and cultural ties, was affected by these global changes.
            - Key connections existed with regions around the Mediterranean Sea.
            - Various powerful empires, including the Roman and Persian Empires, played a role in shaping India’s interactions and influences.

    - Transformation of Europe (6th–15th Century)
        - Decline of the Roman Empire
            - By the sixth century, the Roman Empire had split into two parts:
                1.	Western Roman Empire (Capital: Rome)
                2.	Eastern Roman Empire (Capital: Byzantium/Constantinople)
        - Western Roman Empire
            - Collapsed under invasions from Slavic and Germanic tribes from Russia and Germany.
            - These tribes plundered the Roman territories but eventually settled, reshaping:
                - The population
                - Languages
                - Government structures
            - This period laid the foundation of many modern European nations through the blending of tribal and local cultures.
        
        - Byzantine Empire (Eastern Roman Empire)
            - Included Eastern Europe, Turkey, Syria, and North Africa (Egypt).
            - Maintained a strong monarchy and centralized administration.
            - Differed from the Catholic Church in Rome, forming the Greek Orthodox Church.
            - Played a crucial role in converting Russia to Christianity.
        
        - Cultural & Economic Influence
            - Acted as a link between Greco-Roman civilization and the Arab world.
            - Continued trade with Asia after the fall of Western Rome.
            - Many governance and cultural traditions were later adopted by the Arabs.
            - Contributed to the revival of Greek learning in the West.
            - Ultimately fell to the Turks in the 15th century.
        
        - Western Europe: The Dark Age & Revival
            - After the fall of the Western Roman Empire:
                - Cities declined due to economic collapse and loss of gold trade with Africa and the Orient.
                - The 6th to 10th centuries were termed the Dark Ages.
                - However, agriculture expanded, laying the groundwork for future recovery.
            - From the 10th century onward:
                - Cities revived, leading to economic and cultural growth.
                - 12th–14th centuries saw renewed prosperity in Western Europe.
                - Advances in science, technology, and trade fueled progress.
                - Universities were established (e.g., Padua, Milan), fostering learning and new ideas.
        
        - Legacy: Renaissance & New Europe
            - The intellectual revival in these universities helped spark the Renaissance.
            - This period reshaped Europe, leading to its modern transformation.

    - Europe
        - Growth of Feudalism in Europe
            - Rise of Feudalism
                - After the fall of the Roman Empire, a new system of society and governance emerged, known as feudalism.
                - The term comes from the Latin word “feudum”, which later became “fief” in English.
                - Power was concentrated in the hands of chiefs and their military followers, who controlled large tracts of land and played a key role in government.
                - The king was initially just one of the more powerful feudal chiefs.
                - To control the constant rivalry and conflict among the chiefs, kings required them to swear loyalty as vassals in exchange for recognition of their fiefs.
                - Chiefs could appoint sub-chiefs, who would receive a portion of land from their fiefs.
                - Though the king could theoretically reclaim land from disloyal vassals, this was rarely enforced.
                - Over time, the landed aristocracy became hereditary, restricting new entrants while still replacing disloyal nobles when necessary.
            - Serfdom & the Manor System
                - Serfdom was a key feature of feudal society:
                    - Serfs were peasants who were tied to the land, unable to change professions, relocate, or marry without permission from their lord.
                - Manor System:
                    - The lord of the manor lived in a house or castle and owned vast tracts of land.
                    - A portion of the land was cultivated by the lord with the help of serfs.
                    - Serfs split their time between tending their own fields and working on the lord’s land.
                    - They also paid dues in cash and kind.
                    - The lord was responsible for law enforcement, justice, and security.
                - Due to widespread lawlessness, even free peasants often accepted vassalage under a lord for protection.
                - Some historians argue that serfdom and the manor system were essential to feudalism.
                    - Since India lacked both, some question whether it had feudalism.
                    - However, samantas (local landed elites) held feudal-like power, and peasants remained dependent on them.
                - The manor system and serfdom began disappearing in Western Europe after the 14th century.
            - Military Organization in Feudalism
                - The Feudal Army:
                    - The armored knight on horseback became the most iconic symbol of feudalism.
                    - The shift to cavalry warfare in Europe began in the 8th century.
                    - In the Roman era, the military was infantry-based, with heavy and light infantry armed with spears and short swords.
                    - Horses were used mainly for drawing chariots for officers.
                - Arab Influence on European Warfare:
                    - The arrival of the Arabs changed European warfare.
                    - Arab armies used fast-moving cavalry and mounted archers, rendering traditional infantry ineffective.
                    - This shift necessitated a new military structure, leading to feudalism’s expansion.
                    - Kings lacked the resources to maintain large cavalry forces, so they delegated military responsibilities to fief-holders.
                - Technological Advancements:
                    - The effectiveness of cavalry warfare was enhanced by two major inventions:
                        1.	Iron Stirrup: Allowed heavily armored knights to remain stable on horseback and withstand the impact of a lance charge.
                        2.	Improved Horse Harness: Enabled horses to pull twice the load, making them more effective in battle and agriculture.
                - These innovations likely originated from East Asia and spread to Europe and India by the 10th century.
            - Political and Social Influence of Feudalism
                - Feudal lords held immense power, making it difficult for kings to reassert centralized control even after stronger monarchies emerged in the 11th century.
                - Feudalism shaped both political structures and military organization, influencing governance for centuries.
        - Role of the Christian Church in Medieval Europe
            - Alongside feudalism, the Christian Church played a dominant role in medieval Europe.
            - In the absence of a powerful empire, the Catholic Church took on governmental functions in the West.
            - The Pope became a religious and political leader, holding immense moral and political authority.
            - Church’s Role in Society:
                - The Medieval Age was a religious age, with clergy wielding significant power.
                - Monasteries were set up with land grants from feudal lords and donations from merchants.
                - Monastic orders, such as the Franciscans, served the poor and needy.
                - Monasteries also provided medical aid, education, and shelter for travelers.
            - Church vs. Feudal Lords:
                - Some monasteries became extremely wealthy, behaving like feudal lords.
                - This led to conflicts with rulers, who opposed the growing political influence of the Church.
                - The tension between secular rulers and the Papacy was reflected in the Renaissance and Reformation movements.
        - Conclusion
            - Feudalism in Europe was shaped by political, economic, and military factors.
            - It created a hierarchical society dominated by landed aristocracy, serfs, and a decentralized military system.
            - Despite the emergence of stronger monarchies after the 11th century, the feudal tradition remained deeply ingrained.
            - The Catholic Church played a key role in shaping medieval European society, providing stability, services, and governance.
            - Over time, conflicts between feudal lords, monarchs, and the Church contributed to major transformations, ultimately leading to the decline of feudalism and the rise of a new Europe.

    - The Arab World
        - Rise and Expansion of Islam
            - The rise of Islam in the seventh century united warring Arab tribes into a powerful empire.
            - The Arab empire, founded by the early caliphs, included Arabia, Syria, Iraq, Iran, Egypt, North Africa, and Spain.
        - The Abbasid Caliphate
            - Internal conflicts led to the fall of the Umayyad Caliphate in Damascus in the mid-eighth century.
            - The Abbasids, claiming descent from the Prophet Muhammad’s tribe, established their capital in Baghdad.
            - The Abbasid Empire flourished for about 150 years, controlling major centers of civilization in North Africa, Egypt, Syria, Iran, and Iraq.
            - The Abbasids secured key trade routes linking the Mediterranean to India, contributing to wealth and prosperity.
            - Arabs became dominant traders and seafarers, establishing major cities with high standards of living.
            - Introduced the gold dinar and silver dirham, facilitating global trade.
            - Developed double-entry bookkeeping, advanced accountancy, and a banking system, including bills of exchange (Aundis).
        - Cultural and Scientific Advancements
            - The most famous Abbasid caliphs were Harun al-Rashid and al-Mamun, known for their patronage of science and learning.
            - The House of Wisdom (Bayt al-Hikma) in Baghdad translated Greek, Byzantine, Egyptian, Iranian, and Indian knowledge into Arabic.
            - The Arabs absorbed and refined scientific knowledge from the civilizations they conquered.
            - Many Chinese inventions, including the compass, paper, printing, gunpowder, and the wheelbarrow, reached Europe through Arab intermediaries.
            - Marco Polo traveled to China to learn about its trade, attempting to bypass the Arab monopoly.
        - India’s Contributions to Arab Knowledge
            - India’s economic and cultural exchanges with the Arab world were significant but not well-documented.
            - Sind, conquered by the Arabs in the eighth century, served as a conduit for Indian scientific and cultural influence.
            - The decimal system, developed in India in the fifth century, spread to the Arab world and was later introduced to Europe as “Arabic numerals” by al-Khwarizmi in the ninth century.
            - Indian texts on astronomy (Surya Siddhanta), mathematics, medicine (works of Charaka and Sushruta), and literature (Panchatantra – Kalila wa Dimna) were translated into Arabic.
            - Indian merchants and craftsmen visited Arab markets, and Indian physicians were received at the Abbasid court.
        - Contributions to Science and Exploration
            - By the tenth century, Arabs were making independent contributions to geometry, algebra, geography, astronomy, optics, chemistry, and medicine.
            - Arab geographers improved world maps and developed new navigation tools, aiding maritime exploration.
            - Their records provide valuable insights into medieval India and its neighboring regions.
        - Libraries and Intellectual Freedom
            - Some of the world’s best-stocked libraries and scientific laboratories were established in the Arab world.
            - Arab science was international, with scholars from Khurasan, Egypt, and Spain contributing.
            - Arabic was the common language of literature and science, allowing intellectual exchange across borders.
            - Scholars enjoyed intellectual freedom and patronage, fostering an era of remarkable progress.
        - Decline of Arab Science
            - After the twelfth century, Arab scientific progress declined due to:
                - Political and economic instability.
                - The rise of orthodoxy, which stifled free thought.
            - However, Arab science continued to thrive in Spain until the fourteenth century.
            - India, which had limited interaction with Arab science, experienced a slowdown in scientific progress during this period.

    - Africa and Arab Influence
        - Integration into Trade Networks
            - The Arabs strengthened Africa’s connections to Indian Ocean and Middle Eastern trade.
            - Arab migration and mercantile activity along Africa’s east coast expanded significantly, reaching Malindi and Zanzibar.
        - Trade and Commerce
            - Arab trade involved the large-scale export of slaves, along with gold and ivory.
        - The Ethiopian Kingdom
            - Ethiopia was a powerful and long-established kingdom with many towns.
            - Ethiopians (Habshis) were Christian and actively participated in Indian Ocean trade via Aden to India.
            - They had close economic ties with the Byzantine Empire, which played a key role in the trade network.
            - Their economic position declined following the fall of the Byzantine Empire.
        
    - East and Southeast Asia
        - China: Cultural and Commercial Flourishing
            - Chinese society and culture peaked during Tang rule (8th–9th centuries).
            - The Tang extended control over Sinkiang in Central Asia, including Kashgar, boosting overland trade via the Silk Road.
            - Exports included silk, fine porcelain, and jade to West Asia, Europe, and India.
            - Foreign traders, including Arabs, Persians, and Indians, were welcomed and settled in Canton.
        - Political Changes in China
            - Tang Decline (9th century) → Song Dynasty (10th century) → Mongol Conquest (13th century).
            - The Mongols:
                - Unified North and South China for the first time.
                - Conquered Korea, Tonkin (North Vietnam), and Annam (South Vietnam).
                - Established one of the largest empires in East Asia.
            - Marco Polo, the Venetian traveler, visited the court of Kublai Khan, leaving a vivid account.
            - His journey back to Italy took him through Malabar (India), reflecting increasing global interactions.
        - Southeast Asia: Regional Powers and Trade
            - Chinese Expansionism and Naval Power
                - China developed a strong navy, occasionally attempting to expand into Southeast Asia.
                - However, most Southeast Asian states remained independent.
                - Two dominant empires of this period:
                    1.	Sailendra (Sri Vijaya) Empire (7th–10th Century)
                        - Included Sumatra, Java, Malaya Peninsula, parts of Thailand, and the Philippines.
                        - Dominated sea trade with China.
                        - A 9th-century Arab account states that the empire was so vast that even the fastest vessel could not complete a round trip in two years.
                        - Replaced by the Majapahit Empire (11th–14th century), which expanded its influence further.
                    2.	Kambuja Empire (Successor to Hinduized Funan)
                        - Controlled Cambodia and Annam (South Vietnam).
                        - Flourished until the 15th century, achieving high cultural prosperity.
                        - Angkor Wat (10th–12th century):
                            - Three kilometers of covered passages with Hindu gods, goddesses, and apsaras.
                            - Intricate panels from Ramayana and Mahabharata.
                            - Over 200 temples built, later rediscovered by a French explorer in 1860.
                        - Its golden age coincided with India’s peak temple-building era.
            - Indian Influence and Naval Expeditions
                - Pallavas of South India had a strong navy, active in the Bay of Bengal.
                - Chola rulers (10th century) launched naval expeditions to Sumatra and Malaya to safeguard trade routes.
                - Trade and cultural exchanges with India date back to early centuries.
            - Buddhism and Cultural Influence
                - Palembang (Sumatra) was a Buddhist and Sanskrit learning center.
                - Temple of Borobudur (East Java):
                    - A mountain carved into nine stone terraces, topped with a stupa.
                    - Depicts scenes from Ramayana and Mahabharata.
                    - These epics influenced literature, folk art, and puppet plays.
        - India-Southeast Asia-China Cultural and Trade Links
            - Indian traders and monks settled in South China and Southeast Asia.
            - Buddhism spread from India to China, Korea, and Japan.
            - Korean script was influenced by Indian writing.
            - Hindu gods were assimilated into Buddhism, unlike in India, where the reverse occurred.
            - Arab Traders and Islamization
                - Arab traders intensified their activity in South India and Southeast Asia after the Abbasid Empire’s rise.
                - Early Arab traders coexisted with Indian traders, with no major push for conversion.
                - Indonesia and Malaya gradually converted to Islam, mainly after Islam’s consolidation in India.
                - Buddhism remained dominant elsewhere.
        - End of Indian Influence and European Colonization
            - Commercial and cultural ties between India and Southeast Asia declined with European colonization:
                - Dutch rule in Indonesia.
                - British rule in India, Burma, and Malaya.
                - French rule in Indo-China.
            - This severed long-standing cultural and economic interactions between India and the region.

- Northern India: Age of the Three Empires (800-1000)
    - Political Landscape After Harsha’s Decline
        - Following the decline of Harsha’s empire in the seventh century, several large states emerged across North India, the Deccan, and South India.
        - Unlike the Gupta and Harsha’s empires, no kingdom in North India managed to control the entire Ganga valley.
        - The Ganga valley had been crucial for the expansion of past empires due to its population and resources. It had allowed rulers like the Guptas and Harsha to extend control over:
            - Gujarat, with its rich seaports and thriving trade.
            - Malwa and Rajasthan, which were essential trade links between the Ganga valley and Gujarat.
        - In South India, the Cholas successfully controlled the Krishna, Godavari, and Kaveri deltas, which became the foundation of their supremacy.

    - Major Regional Empires (c. 750–1000 CE)
        - Between 750 and 1000 CE, three major empires emerged in North India and the Deccan:
            1.	Pala Empire (Eastern India)
                - Dominated Bengal and Bihar until the mid-ninth century.
            2.	Pratihara Empire (Western India & Upper Ganga Valley)
                - Controlled western India and the Upper Ganga valley until the mid-tenth century.
            3.	Rashtrakuta Empire (Deccan & Beyond)
                - The most powerful empire of the period.
                - Held territories in North and South India at different times.
                - Acted as a bridge between North and South India in both economic and cultural aspects.
        - Despite frequent conflicts, these empires contributed to:
            - Stable governance over vast regions.
            - Agricultural expansion, including the construction of ponds and canals.
            - Patronage of arts, literature, and temple architecture.
        - Of the three, the Rashtrakutas lasted the longest and played a crucial role in linking North and South India.

    - The Struggle for Domination in North India: The Palas
        - Political Situation After Harsha’s Death
            - The period following Harsha’s death was marked by political confusion.
            - Lalitaditya of Kashmir temporarily brought Punjab and Kanauj under his control.
                - Kanauj was considered the symbol of North Indian sovereignty since Harsha’s time.
                - Control of Kanauj meant access to the upper Gangetic valley, rich in trade and agriculture.
            - Lalitaditya invaded Bengal (Gaud) and killed its ruling king.
            - His power declined with the rise of the Palas and Gurjara-Pratiharas.
        - Conflicts for Kanauj and the Gangetic Valley
            - The Palas and Pratiharas fought for control over Banaras to South Bihar, a region with imperial traditions and rich resources.
            - The Pratiharas also clashed with the Rashtrakutas over territorial control.
        - Rise of the Pala Empire
            - Foundation of the Pala Dynasty
                - Founded by Gopala in c. 750, possibly through an election by local nobles to end political anarchy.
                - Gopala:
                    - Not from a royal family (his father was likely a soldier).
                    - Unified Bengal and extended control over Magadha (Bihar).
            - Reign of Dharampala (c. 770–810)
                - Engaged in a tripartite struggle with the Pratiharas and Rashtrakutas for Kanauj and North India.
                - Key events:
                    - The Pratiharas advanced upon Bengal, but before a decisive battle, they were defeated by Rashtrakuta ruler Dhruva.
                    - Dhruva’s withdrawal to the Deccan allowed Dharampala to occupy Kanauj and hold a grand darbar attended by vassal rulers from Punjab, eastern Rajasthan, etc.
                    - His rule possibly extended to Malwa and Berar.
                - Setback:
                    - The Pratiharas revived under Nagabhata II, who defeated Dharampala near Monghyr.
                    - Bihar and eastern Uttar Pradesh remained a constant battleground between the Palas and Pratiharas.
        - Expansion and Cultural Achievements of the Palas
            - Devapala’s Reign (c. 810–850)
                - Devapala (son of Dharampala) ruled for 40 years and expanded control over:
                    - Pragjyotishpur (Assam) and parts of Orissa.
                    - Possibly parts of modern Nepal.
                - For about a century (mid-eighth to mid-ninth century), the Palas dominated eastern India, even extending influence up to Varanasi.
            - Arab and Tibetan Accounts
                - Arab merchant Sulaiman (mid-ninth century) described the Pala Kingdom:
                    - Called it “Ruhma” (likely referring to Dharmapala).
                    - Noted its large military force with 50,000 elephants.
                    - Mentioned 10,000–15,000 troops dedicated to fulling and washing clothes.
                - Tibetan sources (written in the 17th century) describe the Palas as great patrons of Buddhism.
            - Support for Buddhist Learning
                - Dharampala revived Nalanda University, setting aside 200 villages for its upkeep.
                - He also founded Vikramasila University, which became second only to Nalanda in prominence.
                - The Palas built many viharas, housing large numbers of Buddhist monks.
            - Relations with Tibet
                - The Palas maintained close cultural ties with Tibet.
                - Buddhist scholars Santarakshita and Dipankara (Atisha) were invited to Tibet, introducing a new form of Buddhism.
                - Many Tibetan Buddhists studied at Nalanda and Vikramasila.
        - Economic and Cultural Influence in Southeast Asia
            - Religious Patronage and Social Impact
                - Though Buddhist supporters, the Palas also patronized Shaivism and Vaishnavism.
                - They granted land to Brahmins from North India, which:
                    - Helped expand cultivation.
                    - Transformed many pastoralists and food-gatherers into settled cultivators.
            - Trade and Influence in Southeast Asia
                - Trade with Southeast Asia (Burma, Malaya, Java, Sumatra) enriched the Pala Empire.
                - Gold and silver flowed into Bengal from these regions.
                - The Sailendra dynasty (rulers of Malaya, Java, Sumatra) maintained diplomatic relations with the Palas:
                    - Sent embassies to the Pala court.
                    - Sought permission to build a monastery at Nalanda.
                    - Requested five villages for its upkeep, which Devapala granted.
        - Summary
            - The Palas, Pratiharas, and Rashtrakutas fought for control of North India.
            - The Palas dominated eastern India and expanded into Assam, Orissa, and Nepal.
            - They were great patrons of Buddhism, reviving Nalanda and founding Vikramasila.
            - Their relations with Tibet and Southeast Asia enhanced cultural and economic ties.
            - Trade with Southeast Asia contributed to their prosperity and influence.

    - The Pratiharas: Rise, Expansion, and Decline
        - Origins and Early Conflicts
            - Gurjara-Pratihara Identity
                - The Pratiharas, also known as Gurjara-Pratiharas, ruled over Kanauj.
                - They likely originated from the Gurjaras, who were pastoralists and fighters, similar to the Jats.
            - Initial Expansion
                - Established principalities in central and eastern Rajasthan.
                - Engaged in conflicts with the Rashtrakutas for control over:
                    - Malwa
                    - Gujarat
                    - Kanauj (a key region for controlling the upper Ganga valley).
            - Resistance Against the Arabs
                - Nagabhatta I emerged as a prominent leader.
                - Strongly resisted the Arab rulers of Sind, who attempted to encroach on:
                    - Rajasthan
                    - Gujarat
                    - Punjab
                - In 738, Arab forces advanced toward Gujarat but were decisively defeated by the Chalukyan ruler of Gujarat.
                - Small Arab incursions continued but never became a major threat.
        - Struggles with the Rashtrakutas
            - The Pratiharas’ early attempts to expand into the upper Ganga valley and Malwa were halted by the Rashtrakutas.
            - Key defeats:
                - 790 – Rashtrakuta ruler Dhruva defeated the Pratiharas.
                - 806-07 – Rashtrakuta ruler Gopal II defeated them again.
            - The Rashtrakutas later withdrew to the Deccan, allowing the Palas to exert influence.
        - Bhoja: The Greatest Pratihara Ruler
            - Empire Consolidation (c. 836 AD)
                - Bhoja rebuilt the Pratihara empire and recovered Kanauj, making it the capital for nearly a century.
                - Initially attempted eastern expansion but was defeated by the Pala ruler Devapala.
                - Shifted focus to central India, the Deccan, and Gujarat, leading to renewed struggles with the Rashtrakutas.
            - Key Battles and Expansion
                - Narmada Battle – Bhoja fought a bloody battle on the banks of the Narmada against the Rashtrakutas.
                    - Retained control over large parts of Malwa and some parts of Gujarat.
                    - Unable to extend further into the Deccan.
                - Territorial Reach
                    - Inscription evidence suggests his empire extended west of the Sutlej River.
            - Reputation and Military Strength
                - Arab Accounts
                    - Arab travelers noted that the Pratiharas had India’s best cavalry.
                    - The import of horses from Central Asia and Arabia was significant for their military.
                - Legends and Legacy
                    - Bhoja’s reconquest of Kanauj captured contemporary imagination.
                    - Devotee of Vishnu, adopting the title ‘Adivaraha’, which appears on some of his coins.
                    - Sometimes called Mihir Bhoja to distinguish him from Bhoja Paramara of Ujjain.
                    - Likely died around 885 AD, succeeded by his son Mahendrapala I.
        - Mahendrapala I and Pratihara Dominance
            - Territorial Expansion (c. 885-908 AD)
                - Mahendrapala I maintained Bhoja’s empire and extended control over:
                    - Magadha
                    - Northern Bengal
                    - His inscriptions have been found in Kathiawar, eastern Punjab, and Awadh.
                - Fought a battle against the king of Kashmir but lost some territories in Punjab.
            - Pratihara Power in the 9th and 10th Centuries
                - Al-Masudi’s Account (915-916 AD)
                    - Arab traveler Al-Masudi visited Gujarat and attested to the power and prestige of the Pratiharas.
                    - Called the Gurjara-Pratihara kingdom “al-Juzr” (corruption of Gurjara).
                    - Referred to the Pratihara king as “Baura”, likely a mispronunciation of Adivaraha.
                - Military Strength
                    - The empire contained 180,000 villages, cities, and rural areas.
                    - Covered 2000 km x 2000 km in size.
                    - The army was divided into four divisions, each with 700,000–900,000 men.
                    - Despite having only 2000 trained war elephants, they had India’s best cavalry.
        - Cultural and Scholarly Achievements
            - Patronage of Learning and Literature
                - The Pratiharas were great patrons of Sanskrit literature.
                - Poet Rajashekhar, a major Sanskrit dramatist, lived at the court of Mahipala (Bhoja’s grandson).
            - Architectural Contributions
                - The Pratiharas embellished Kanauj with many fine buildings and temples.
            - Relations with the Arab World
                - Indian scholars traveled to the Caliph’s court in Baghdad during the 8th-9th centuries.
                - Introduced Indian sciences, including:
                    - Mathematics
                    - Algebra
                    - Medicine
                - Despite political hostilities, scholarly exchanges and trade continued between India and West Asia.
        - Decline of the Pratihara Empire
            - Rashtrakuta Invasions
                - 915-918 AD – Rashtrakuta king Indra III attacked Kanauj and devastated the city.
                - The loss of Gujarat weakened the Pratihara empire, as it was central to overseas trade.
                - Al-Masudi’s account confirms that the Pratihara empire had no access to the sea after losing Gujarat.
            - Final Blow by Krishna III
                - 963 AD – Rashtrakuta ruler Krishna III invaded north India and defeated the Pratihara ruler.
                - This marked the rapid disintegration of the Pratihara empire.

    - The Rashtrakutas
        - Rise and Expansion
            - The Rashtrakuta dynasty ruled the Deccan while the Palas and Pratiharas controlled north India.
            - Founded by Dantidurga, who established his capital at Manyakhet (Malkhed) near modern-day Sholapur.
            - Initially dominated northern Maharashtra.
            - Engaged in conflicts with the Pratiharas over Gujarat and Malwa. Though they did not extend their empire to the Ganga valley, their raids brought rich plunder and fame.
            - Constantly fought against the Eastern Chalukyas of Vengi (modern Andhra Pradesh) and the Pallavas of Kanchi and Pandyas of Madurai.
        - Notable Rulers
            - Govinda III (793–814)
                - Launched a successful expedition against Nagabhatta of Kanauj and annexed Malwa.
                - Turned to the south, defeating the Kerala, Pandya, and Chola kings, and weakening the Pallavas.
                - Captured the Ganga kings (of Karnataka) and subdued the king of Lanka and his minister.
            - Amoghavarsha (814–878)
                - Ruled for 64 years but preferred religion and literature over war.
                - Credited with writing the first Kannada book on poetics.
                - A great builder, making Manyakhet a city that rivaled Indra’s abode.
                - Struggled with rebellions, which resurfaced after his death.
            - Indra III (915–927)
                - Re-established the empire after quelling rebellions.
                - Defeated Mahipala and sacked Kanauj in 915, making him the most powerful ruler of his time.
                - According to Al-Masudi, the Rashtrakuta king Bathara (Vallabharaja) was the greatest king of India, with most Indian rulers accepting his suzerainty.
                - Possessed large armies and numerous elephants.
            - Krishna III (934–963)
                - Fought against the Paramaras of Malwa and Eastern Chalukyas of Vengi.
                - Launched a campaign against the Cholas, who had supplanted the Pallavas of Kanchi.
                - Defeated Chola king Parantaka I in 949, annexing northern parts of the Chola empire.
                - Pressed down to Rameshwaram, setting up a pillar of victory and building a temple.
                - After his death, opponents united against his successor, leading to the sack and destruction of Malkhed in 972, marking the end of the Rashtrakuta empire.
        - Religious Tolerance and Cultural Contributions
            - Ruled Deccan for nearly 200 years (until the late 10th century).
            - Supported Saivism, Vaishnavism, and Jainism.
            - Ellora’s famous rock-cut temple of Shiva, built by Krishna I (9th century).
            - Amoghavarsha was a Jain but patronized all faiths.
            - Allowed Muslim traders to settle, and Islam was preached in their dominions.
                - Muslim communities had headmen and mosques in many coastal towns.
                - This policy promoted foreign trade, enriching the Rashtrakutas.
        - Patronage of Arts and Literature
            - Encouraged Sanskrit, Prakrit, and Apabhramsha literature.
            - Their courts featured Sanskrit scholars, poets, and writers.
            - The great Apabhramsha poet, Svayambhu, and his son possibly lived at the Rashtrakuta court.

    - Political Ideas and Organization
        - The Monarch and the Court
            - The system of administration in these empires was influenced by the Gupta Empire, Harsha’s kingdom, and the Chalukyas in the Deccan.
            - The monarch was the center of all affairs, serving as both the head of administration and the commander-in-chief of the armed forces.
            - The king held court in a magnificent darbar, attended by vassal chiefs, feudatories, ambassadors, and high officials.
            - The court was also a cultural hub, featuring dancing girls, musicians, and women from the king’s household, who attended on festive occasions.
            - In the Rashtrakuta Empire, Arab writers noted that women did not veil their faces.
        - Kingship and Succession
            - Kingship was generally hereditary, and absolute loyalty was emphasized due to frequent wars.
            - While kings adopted grand titles like Maharajadiraj and Chakravartin, their authority was often limited by vassals and autonomous chiefs.
            - Thinkers like Medhatithi believed in the right to bear arms for self-defense and opposing unjust rulers.
            - Rules of succession were not rigid: the eldest son often succeeded, but conflicts between siblings were common.
            - Some kings designated a Yuvaraj (successor), who assisted in administration, while younger sons were made provincial governors.
            - Rarely, princesses held government posts, such as Chandrobalabbe, a Rashtrakuta princess who administered the Raichur Doab.
        - Vassal Chiefs and Their Role
            - Empires comprised directly administered areas and vassal-ruled territories.
            - Vassal chiefs were autonomous in internal affairs but had obligations to the overlord, including loyalty, tribute payment, and military support.
            - Some vassal chiefs had to send a son to stay at the overlord’s court as a measure against rebellion.
            - Vassals attended the overlord's darbar on special occasions and sometimes married into the royal family.
            - Frequent wars occurred as vassals sought independence.
            - Rashtrakutas fought vassals in Vengi (Andhra) and Karnataka, while Pratiharas fought the Paramaras of Malwa and the Chandellas of Bundelkhand.
        - Ministers and Administration
            - Kings were assisted by ministers, often from leading families, with hereditary positions in some cases.
            - The Pala kings had a Brahmin family that supplied four successive chief ministers.
            - The central government had various departments, including:
                - Minister of correspondence (foreign affairs)
                - Revenue minister
                - Treasurer
                - Chief of armed forces (Senapati)
                - Chief justice
                - Purohita (priestly advisor)
            - Ministers, except the purohita, were expected to lead military campaigns when needed.
            - The king's household officers wielded significant power due to their proximity to the ruler.
        - Military Organization
            - A strong military was crucial for maintaining and expanding the empire.
            - The Pala, Pratihara, and Rashtrakuta kings maintained well-organized infantry, cavalry, and war-elephants.
            - Elephants, symbols of strength, were especially prized, with the Pala kings maintaining the largest number.
            - Horses were imported from Arabia, West Asia, and Central Asia.
            - The Pratiharas had the finest cavalry, while war-chariots had fallen out of use.
            - Rashtrakutas maintained numerous forts, each with independent commanders.
            - Infantry included both regular troops and levies from vassal chiefs, with the Pala infantry consisting of soldiers from Malwa, Assam, Gujarat, and Karnataka.
            - The Pala and Rashtrakuta kings had navies, though details of their strength are unknown.
        - Provincial and Local Administration
            - Pala and Pratihara Empires:
                - Provinces (baukti) were governed by an uparika, responsible for revenue collection and law enforcement.
                - Districts (visaya) were led by a visayapati.
                - Below the visaya was the parzaia.
                - Smaller chiefs (semantas or bhogapatis) dominated multiple villages and often merged with visayapatis.
            - Rashtrakuta Empire:
                - Provinces (rashira) were led by a rashtrapati, similar to an uparika.
                - Districts (visaya) had smaller units called bhuke and dhwAei.
                - Officials were paid through rent-free land grants, blurring distinctions between officials and hereditary chiefs.
        - Village and Urban Administration
            - The village was the fundamental administrative unit, led by a hereditary headman and an accountant.
            - Village elders (grama mahajana or grama mahatteara) assisted in governance.
            - In Karnataka (Rashtrakuta kingdom), village committees managed schools, tanks, temples, roads, and revenue collection.
                - Towns had similar committees, with trade guild heads included.
                - The koshia pata or kotwel managed law enforcement in towns.
            - Hereditary Revenue Officers:
                - Nad gavundas or desa gramakutas managed revenue collection, similar to later deshmukhs and deshpandes in Maharashtra.
                - Their growing power weakened village committees and central authority, contributing to feudalization.
        - Relationship Between State and Religion            
            - Rulers were followers of Hinduism, Buddhism, or Jainism and generously donated to religious institutions.
            - Rashtrakuta kings allowed Muslims to preach their faith.
            - Kings did not interfere with religious customs but protected Brahmans and upheld the varna system.
            - The purohita advised the king on religious matters but did not dominate state affairs.
            - Medhatithi, a Dharmashastra scholar, asserted that the king's authority derived from both the Dharmashastras and Arthashastra, emphasizing political over religious principles.
            - Religion was primarily used to legitimize and strengthen rulers, who built grand temples and made land grants for religious maintenance.
            
            
- South India: The Chola Empire (900-1200)
    - During the sixth to eighth centuries, several powerful kingdoms emerged in South India:
        - Pallavas and Pandyas – Dominated modern Tamil Nadu.
        - Cheras – Controlled modern Kerala.
        - Chalukyas – Dominated Maharashtra and the Deccan.
            - The Chalukyan king Pulakeshin II played a crucial role in regional politics by defeating Harsha and preventing his expansion into the Deccan.
        - Many of these kingdoms, especially the Pallavas and Pandyas, had strong navies, which:
            - Strengthened economic, religious, and cultural ties with Southeast Asia and China.
            - Enabled invasions and temporary rule over parts of Sri Lanka.

    - The Rise of the Chola Empire
        - Foundation and Early Expansion (9th Century)
            - Vijayalaya, the founder of the Chola Empire, was initially a feudatory of the Pallavas.
            - Around 850 AD, he captured Tanjore, marking the beginning of Chola rule.
            - By the end of the 9th century, the Cholas had:
                - Defeated the Pallavas of Kanchi (Tondaimandalam).
                - Weakened the Pandyas, bringing the southern Tamil region under their control.
        - Challenges and Revival
            - The Cholas faced threats from the Rashtrakutas.
            - Krishna III dealt them a major setback by defeating the Chola king and annexing the northern part of the empire.
            - However, the Cholas rapidly recovered after:
                - The death of Krishna III (965 AD).
                - The downfall of the Rashtrakuta Empire.
        - Chola Naval and Political Dominance
            - The Cholas established a vast empire, controlling a large part of the Indian peninsula.
            - They built a powerful navy, which enabled them to:
                - Conquer Sri Lanka and the Maldives.
                - Extend their influence to Southeast Asia, marking a high point in South Indian history.
        - The Chola Empire represents the climax of South India’s political and naval supremacy.

    - The Age of Rajaraja and Rajendra I
        - Rajaraja I (985-1014): Military Conquests and Naval Dominance
            - Rajaraja I was one of the greatest Chola rulers, expanding the empire through military and naval conquests.
            - His key victories included:
                - Defeating the Chera navy at Trivandrum and attacking Quilon.
                - Conquering Madurai and capturing the Pandyan king.
                - Annexing northern Sri Lanka, motivated by a desire to control Southeast Asian trade.
                - Conquering the Maldives, showcasing Chola naval strength.
                - Expanding into Karnataka and Vengi, annexing parts of the Ganga kingdom.
            - The Coromandel Coast and Malabar were important trade centers, and Chola dominance strengthened India’s overseas trade links.
        - Rajendra I (1014-1044): Expansion and Overseas Expeditions
            - Rajendra I, appointed heir during his father’s reign, gained administrative and military experience before becoming king.
            - He continued Rajaraja’s expansionist policies by:
                - Completely overrunning the Pandya and Chera kingdoms, incorporating them into the Chola Empire.
                - Completing the conquest of Sri Lanka, capturing the royal crown and insignia. Sri Lanka remained under Chola rule for 50 years.
        - Architectural and Historical Legacy
            - Both Rajaraja and Rajendra I commemorated their victories by building Shiva and Vishnu temples.
            - The most notable structure was the Brihadishwara Temple at Tanjore, completed in 1010.
            - Chola rulers inscribed their achievements on temple walls, providing a rich historical record.
        - Rajendra I’s Ganga Expedition (1022)
            - Rajendra I led a spectacular military campaign from Kalinga to Bengal, crossing the Ganga River and defeating two local kings.
            - This campaign mirrored Samudragupta’s earlier conquest.
            - To commemorate his victory, Rajendra I:
                - Assumed the title Gangaikondachola (‘The Chola Who Conquered the Ganga’).
                - Established a new capital, Gangaikondacholapuram, near the Kaveri River.
        - Naval Expeditions Against the Srivijaya Empire
            - One of Rajendra I’s most remarkable achievements was his naval campaign against the Srivijaya Empire.
            - The Srivijaya Empire (revived in the 10th century) controlled the Malay Peninsula, Sumatra, Java, and the trade route to China.
            - Despite cordial relations with the Buddhist Sailendra rulers of Srivijaya, conflict arose due to:
                - Chola ambitions to remove trade restrictions and strengthen Indian merchants’ influence.
            - The Chola navy launched expeditions in the Malay Peninsula and Sumatra, leading to the conquest of Kadaram (Kedah) and several key trading centers.
            - The Chola naval power was so dominant that the Bay of Bengal was referred to as a ‘Chola Lake’.
        - Diplomatic and Trade Relations with China
            - The Chola rulers maintained diplomatic and commercial ties with China.
            - Chola embassies were sent to China in 1016 and 1033, followed by a delegation of 70 merchants in 1077.
            - Chinese records note that the Cholas received 81,800 strings of copper-cash (over four lakh rupees) in exchange for goods like glassware, camphor, brocades, rhinoceros horns, and ivory.
            - The Chinese referred to all imported trade goods as ‘tribute’, though they were part of commercial exchanges.
        - Conflicts with the Later Chalukyas
            - The Cholas were in constant conflict with the Later Chalukyas, who succeeded the Rashtrakutas with their capital at Kalyani.
            - The struggle for dominance centered on:
                - Vengi (Rayalaseema)
                - The Tungabhadra Doab
                - Northwest Karnataka (Ganga-ruled territories)
            - Neither side secured a decisive victory, and prolonged warfare exhausted both kingdoms.
            - The wars became increasingly brutal:
                - Chalukyan cities like Kalyani were sacked and plundered, with mass killings of civilians, including Brahmins and children.
                - Similar tactics were used in the Pandya kingdom, where military colonies were settled to maintain control.
                - The Cholas destroyed Anuradhapura, the ancient capital of Sri Lanka, and harshly treated its king and queen.
            - These actions are seen as dark episodes in Chola history.
        - Chola Administration and Local Governance
            - Despite their militaristic policies, the Cholas established a well-organized administration in their conquered regions.
            - A key feature of Chola governance was the promotion of local self-government in villages, ensuring stability and continuity.
        - Decline of the Chola Empire
            - The Chola Empire flourished through the 12th century but declined in the early 13th century.
            - Simultaneously, the Later Chalukyan Empire in Maharashtra also collapsed.
            - The Pandyas and Hoysalas replaced the Cholas in the south, while the Yadavas and Kakatiyas succeeded the Later Chalukyas.
            - These regional powers patronized arts and architecture but weakened themselves through constant internal warfare.
            - Their conflicts ultimately left them vulnerable to Delhi Sultanate invasions, leading to their downfall in the early 14th century.

    - CHOLA GOVERNMENT AND LOCAL SELF-GOVERNMENT
        - Central Administration
            - The king was the supreme authority in the Chola administration. He had a council of ministers to advise him but held absolute power.
            - Chola rulers frequently toured their kingdom to oversee governance.
            - The empire was divided into four mandalams (provinces), sometimes governed by royal princes.
            - Officials were paid through revenue-bearing land assignments instead of salaries.
        - Administrative Divisions
            - The empire was structured into different administrative units:
                - Nadu – the basic administrative unit, consisting of villages with close kinship ties.
                - Valanadu – a group of nadus.
                - Hill people and tribals were gradually integrated into agricultural society through irrigation and land cultivation.
            - Brahmans and temples were granted lands, which helped expand cultivation.
        - Local Self-Government
            - Chola inscriptions reveal a well-developed village self-government system, consisting of two key assemblies:
                1.	Ur – A general village assembly.
                2.	Sabha / Mahasabha – A governing body in Brahman villages (agraharams), where:
                    - Executive committees managed village affairs.
                    - Educated landowners were elected by lot or rotation and served for three years.
                    - Committees oversaw land revenue collection, law enforcement, and justice.
                    - A Tank Committee managed water distribution for agriculture.
                    - The sabha had authority to distribute land, levy taxes, and raise village loans.
            - The Chola village self-governance system was highly effective, although its autonomy diminished over time due to growing feudal influences.

    - CHOLA MILITARY AND NAVY
        - The Cholas maintained a large and well-organized army consisting of:
            - Elephants, cavalry, and infantry (known as the three limbs of the army).
            - Infantry was primarily armed with spears.
            - Kings had bodyguards sworn to defend them at any cost.
        - Marco Polo, the Venetian traveler, mentioned that Chola bodyguards burned themselves on their king’s funeral pyre (possibly an exaggeration).
        - The Chola navy was powerful, dominating the Malabar and Coromandel coasts and, at its peak, the entire Bay of Bengal.

    - CHOLA TRADE, REVENUE, AND INFRASTRUCTURE
        - The Chola rulers built a network of royal roads for trade and military movement.
        - Trade and commerce flourished, with massive trade guilds engaging in commerce with Java and Sumatra.
        - Revenue sources included:
            - Land tax, fixed based on an elaborate land survey.
            - Tolls on trade and taxes on professions.
            - Plunder from neighboring territories.
        - Cholas prioritized irrigation, using the Kaveri River and other water sources. They constructed numerous tanks for irrigation.
        
    - CULTURAL LIFE DURING THE CHOLA PERIOD
        - Bhakti Movement and Religious Influence
            - The Bhakti movement reached its peak during the Chola period, closely linked to temple worship.
            - Many temples were built with royal patronage, and they became centers of devotion, art, and culture.
            - The Nayanmars (Shaivite saints) and Alvars (Vaishnavite saints) composed devotional hymns in Tamil between the 6th and 9th centuries.
            - The Tirumurai, a collection of Saivite hymns compiled in the 12th century, was regarded as the fifth Veda.
        - Temple Architecture and Urban Development
            - The Cholas promoted the Dravida style of temple architecture, characterized by:
                - Vimana – A multi-storeyed tower above the garbhagriha (sanctum).
                - Mandapa – A pillared hall for ceremonies, gatherings, and dances by devadasis.
                - Gopurams – Lofty gateway towers.
                - Courtyards – Enclosed spaces with multiple shrines, sometimes with irrigation facilities.
            - Temples were economic hubs, receiving revenue-free land grants and engaging in business, money-lending, and irrigation projects.
            - Notable Chola temples:
                - Brihadiswara Temple (Tanjore) – Built by Rajaraja I, also called the Rajaraja Temple.
                - Temple at Gangaikondacholapuram – Though now in ruins, it remains a significant architectural achievement.
        - Expansion of Temple Construction Post-Cholas
            - Temple building continued under later dynasties:
                - Chalukyas of Kalyani and the Hoysalas.
                - Hoysalesvara Temple (Halebid) – A prime example of the Chalukyan style, featuring:
                    - Sculptures depicting dance, music, war, and love.
                    - Images of Yakshas and Yakshinis (celestial beings).
        - Sculpture and Iconography
            - Chola sculptors excelled in bronze casting and stone carving.
            - Notable works include:
                - The Nataraja (Shiva as the cosmic dancer) – Considered a masterpiece of Chola bronze artistry.
                - The giant statue of Gomateswara (Shravanabelagola) – A remarkable Jain sculpture.
            - Many Chola bronzes are now preserved in museums worldwide.
        - Development of Literature and Languages
            - Tamil Literature
                - Kamban’s Ramayana (11th–12th century) is considered a Tamil literary classic.
                - Devotional and philosophical works by Nayanmars and Alvars contributed to Tamil’s religious literature.
            - Growth of Kannada and Telugu Literature
                - Kannada became a major literary language, patronized by the Rashtrakutas, Chalukyas, and Hoysalas.
                - Notable Kannada literary figures:
                    - Amoghavarsha (Rashtrakuta king) – Wrote a book on poetics.
                    - Pampa, Ponna, and Ranna – Called the “Three Gems of Kannada poetry”, influenced by Jainism.
                - Telugu literature developed under the Chalukyas:
                    - Nanniah began the Telugu Mahabharata, which was completed by Tikkanna in the 13th century.
        - Economic and Social Impact of Culture
            - Temples as economic centers – They:
                - Received wealthy merchant donations.
                - Engaged in business and money lending.
                - Funded agricultural projects like irrigation and tank construction.
            - Merchants and artisans grew in influence due to foreign trade under the Cholas.
            - Temples served as social hubs, integrating religion with daily life.
        - Conclusion
            - The period from the 8th to 12th century was a golden age of cultural and economic growth in South India.
            - The Chola era saw a blending of religion, art, and administration, influencing later dynasties.

- Economic and Social Life, Education, and Religious Beliefs (800-1200 CE)
    - Continuity and Change in Economic and Social Life (800–1200 CE)
        - Continuity of Earlier Features
            - Economic and social structures evolved gradually, unlike political developments.
            - Many features from before the 9th century persisted throughout this period.
        - New Elements of Change
            - Despite continuity, new factors emerged that distinguished this period from earlier times.
            - The extent and direction of change varied across different regions and social groups.
        - General Pattern of Historical Change
            - Every historical period reflects a mix of continuity and transformation in its economic, social, and religious aspects.
            - The rate of change in beliefs and societal structures was much slower compared to political shifts.

    - Trade and Commerce
        - Debate on Economic Conditions
            - The economic situation during this period is a topic of debate among historians. Some argue that it was a time of stagnation and decline, citing:
                - Setbacks in both foreign and long-distance trade within India.
                - The decline of towns, leading to greater localism and regionalism.
                - The virtual absence of gold coins until the tenth century as evidence of economic downturn.
            - However, despite the fall of the Roman Empire, India’s trade with the West remained strong due to the rise of new empires:
                - The Byzantine Empire (based in Constantinople).
                - The Sassanid Empire (based in Iran).
            - Both empires actively engaged in trade with India and the Indian Ocean region. With the rise of the Arab Empire in the seventh century, Western trade expanded further, connecting India, Southeast Asia, and China.            
        - Gold and Silver Inflow
            - Indian traders were not excluded from this expanding trade. India continued to attract gold and silver due to its favorable trade balance, earning the title of a land rich in precious metals. However, these metals were primarily used for:
                - Decorating temples and palaces.
                - Making jewelry.
                - Hoarding for future use, rather than being minted into coins.
            - The reason behind the limited use of gold and silver for coinage remains unclear.
        - Internal Trade and Agrarian Expansion
            - The rise of numerous kingdoms led to increased state authority and land expansion through:
                - Construction of bunds, wells, and irrigation systems.
                - Grants of rent-free land to Brahmins, encouraging settlement and agriculture (e.g., in Bengal, Sind, and Tamil regions).
                - The transition of nomadic tribes into agricultural communities.
            - The impact of this agrarian expansion on internal trade remains uncertain. However, the growth of small towns catering to local trade suggests a shift toward greater self-sufficiency.
        - Decline of Trade Guilds
            - The decline of long-distance trade in North India led to:
                - The diminishing role of guilds (shrenis) and sanghas, which had previously played a significant role in economic activities.
                - The loss of endowments for guilds, reducing their influence.
                - The transformation of some guilds into sub-castes (e.g., Dvadasa-shreni becoming a Vaishya sub-caste).
                - A setback for Jainism, which had strong mercantile patronage.
        - Restrictions on Travel
            - Some Dharma Shastras of this period imposed restrictions on travel beyond:
                - Areas where munja grass did not grow or where the black gazelle did not roam (i.e., beyond India).
                - Sea voyages, which were considered polluting.
            - However, these restrictions were not strictly followed. Indian merchants, scholars, and craftsmen traveled to Baghdad and other Muslim cities in West Asia. The bans may have applied mainly to Brahmins or were meant to prevent Indians from adopting foreign religious influences.
        - Overseas Trade with Southeast Asia and China
            - Despite inland trade challenges, overseas trade thrived:
                - Trade with Southeast Asia had begun by the sixth century, reflected in literature describing local languages, clothing, and cultures.
                - Indian merchants formed powerful guilds like Manigramam and Nandest, operating in foreign lands and making temple donations.
                - Hinduism and Buddhism spread through merchant activity, evident in:
                    - The Borobudur Buddhist temple (Java).
                    - The Angkor Wat Hindu temple (Cambodia).
                - Some Southeast Asian ruling families adopted Hindu practices and welcomed trade with India.
            - The chief Indian port for trade with Java, Sumatra, and Malaya was Tamralipti (Tamluk) in Bengal. Merchants from Gujarat also participated in this trade.
        - Role of India in Chinese Trade
            - China emerged as a key trade destination, importing:
                - Spices from India and Southeast Asia.
                - Ivory (mainly from Africa).
                - Glassware (from West Asia).
                - Medicinal herbs, lac, and incense.
            - India and Southeast Asia acted as staging centers for trade between China, West Asia, and Africa due to:
                - Favorable monsoon wind patterns, requiring ships to halt at Indian and Southeast Asian ports.
                - A strong shipbuilding tradition, especially in Malabar, Bengal, and Burma.
            - Canton (Kanfu to Arab travelers) was China’s main foreign trade port. The Pala and Sena rulers of Bengal and the Pallava and Chola rulers of South India actively promoted trade with China through diplomatic embassies. The Chola ruler Rajendra I even launched a naval expedition against Malaya to protect Indian trade interests.
            - By the tenth and eleventh centuries:
                - Many Indian merchants and monks were present in China.
                - Canton’s river was filled with ships from India, Persia, and Arabia.
                - There were three Hindu temples in Canton where Indian traders resided.
                - Indian influence reached Japan, where two Indians are credited with introducing cotton cultivation.
        - Decline of Indian Maritime Supremacy
            - Though India dominated early maritime trade, it eventually lost its position:
                - The Chinese government tried to restrict the export of gold and silver in the 13th century.
                - Larger and faster Chinese and Arab ships replaced Indian vessels.
                - Chinese ships were multi-story, carrying 600 passengers and 400 soldiers.
                - The use of the mariner’s compass boosted Chinese naval expansion, marking a period when Indian science and technology lagged.
        - Conclusion
            - Despite inland trade setbacks, India’s foreign trade with Southeast Asia and China flourished, led by South India, Bengal, and Gujarat. This period saw:
                - Strong maritime traditions.
                - Cultural exchanges through trade.
                - Economic prosperity, driven by merchant activity and overseas expansion.

    - Condition of the People
        - Prosperity in Handicrafts and Agriculture
            - Indian handicrafts, including textiles, gold and silver work, and metallurgy, maintained a high standard.
            - Indian agriculture thrived, with many Arab travelers attesting to the fertility of the soil and the skill of Indian peasants.
        - Luxury and Opulence of the Elite
            - Ministers, officials, and feudal chiefs lived in great ostentation, imitating kings:
                - Resided in grand houses, sometimes three to five stories high.
                - Wore costly foreign apparel, including imported woolen clothes, Chinese silk, and gold and silver jewelry.
                - Maintained large households with numerous women, domestic servants, and attendants.
                - Assumed grand titles (mahasamantadhipati) and used distinctive symbols like banners, decorated umbrellas, and yak-tail whisks.
            - Wealthy merchants also displayed royal lifestyles:
                - A Chalukyan millionaire hoisted banners with ringing bells over his house, owned many horses and elephants, and built temples with crystal floors and walls adorned with religious paintings.
                - Ministers Vaseupala and Tejahpala of Gujarat were among the richest merchants of their time.
        - Hardships of the Common People
            - Despite the wealth of the elite, prosperity was not universal:
            - Food was cheap, but many poor city dwellers struggled to eat enough.
            - Rajatarangini (12th-century Kashmir) describes:
                - Courtiers feasting on fried meat and perfumed wine.
                - Commoners surviving on rice and stpala-saka (a bitter wild vegetable).
            - Many resorted to robbery and plunder to survive.
        - Peasants and Taxation Burden
            - Villagers, forming the majority of the population, faced heavy taxation:
                - Traditional tax: One-sixth of produce, as per Dharmashastras.
                - Additional cesses: Grazing tax, pond tax, and arbitrary levies by local rulers.
                - Some land grants allowed landlords to impose unfair taxes on peasants.
            - Forced labor (vishai) was common:
                - In central India and Orissa, entire villages were granted to Brahmins and elites.
                - Cultivators, artisans, and herdsmen were tied to the land like medieval European serfs.
            - Chiefs exploited every opportunity to impose levies:
                - One Rajput chief reportedly taxed even sparrows, dead birds, pig dung, and funeral shrouds.
                - Another account tells of a village depopulated due to a chief’s excessive demands.
        - Impact of Wars and Famines
            - Wars and famines further worsened living conditions:
                - War tactics included:
                    - Destruction of water reservoirs.
                    - Burning of villages.
                    - Seizure of cattle and stored grain.
                    - Destruction of cities—considered legitimate by contemporary writers.
            - Frequent hardships increased burdens on common people, contributing to a feudal society.

    - Nature of Society
        - Rise of the Rajput and Feudal Class
            - A new class of powerful individuals emerged, known by various names:
                - Semanta, Randk, and Rdutia (Rajput)—as referred to by contemporary writers.
            - Their origins were diverse:
                - Government officers—increasingly paid through revenue-bearing village assignments instead of cash.
                - Defeated rajas and their supporters—who retained control over limited areas.
                - Local hereditary chiefs or military adventurers—who established power with armed supporters.
                - Tribal or clan leaders—who exercised control in specific regions.
            - A hierarchical structure developed among them:
                - Some were mere village chiefs, others ruled over multiple villages, while a few dominated entire regions.
                - They frequently competed against each other to expand their authority and privileges.
        - Revenue Assignments and Hereditary Control
            - Rulers granted jagir (fief) assignments to officers and supporters:
                - These were theoretically temporary and revocable by the ruler.
                - In practice, rulers rarely reclaimed them, except in cases of rebellion or disloyalty.
                - A prevailing belief considered it a sin to deprive even a defeated ruler of his lands.
            - Over time, these rulers controlled large territories but often had to deal with subordinate and defeated rulers seeking to regain independence.
            - Within these territories:
                - Officials treated their assignments as hereditary fiefs.
                - Government offices became hereditary, as seen in Bengal, where a family held the makamani office for four generations.
                - Many administrative functions shifted to hereditary chiefs, who:
                    - Assessed and collected land revenue.
                    - Assumed judicial powers, imposing punishments and fines.
                    - Sublet land to followers without royal approval, increasing the number of non-working landholders.
        - Debate on Feudalism in India
            - Some historians classify this society as ‘feudal’, while others prefer the term ‘medieval society’:
                - Unlike European feudalism, India lacked strict vassalage, serfdom, and manor systems.
                - However, both societies shared common traits:
                    - A dominant ruling class that lived off the surplus produced by peasants.
                    - Peasants in a dependent position, lacking autonomy.
            - Despite the debate, it is clear that from the 8th century onwards, Indian society increasingly emphasized localism and sub-regionalism, shaping both political and cultural developments in subsequent centuries.

    - THE CASTE SYSTEM
        - Brahmans and Rajputs
            - Strengthening of the Brahmans:
                - The caste system remained the foundation of society, but the Brahmans gained more influence.
                - Rulers of Sind, Bengal, and South India:
                    - Invited Brahmans to settle in their regions.
                    - Granted them large-scale, tax-free lands.
                - Brahmans played key roles in administration and society:
                    - Government positions: Local revenue officials, ministers, and accountants.
                    - Military involvement: Some served as advisors and warriors.
                    - Education and literature: Contributed to literary and intellectual advancements.
                - Writers of the period further exalted the Brahmans:
                    - Some claimed that Kshatriyas had disappeared.
                    - Vaishyas had declined to the status of Shudras.
                    - Brahmans remained the only dvija (twice-born or privileged) caste.
                - However, this did not fully reflect reality, as:
                    - Wealthy Hindu and Jain merchants continued to thrive.
                    - A new class of powerful landed chiefs emerged.
            - Rise of the Rajputs:
                - Many states during this period were ruled by Rajputs.
                - Rajputs were leaders of clans that:
                    - Dominated specific regions.
                    - Formed the core of armed forces.
                    - Governed collectively, with rulers often sharing power with clan members.
            - Debate on Rajput Origins:
                - Scholars hold different views on their origins:
                    - Some believe Rajputs were of mixed origin, including:
                        - Foreign groups like Shakas and Hunas.
                        - Indigenous tribes and even Brahmans.
                - Many Rajput clans (traditionally 36 in number) trace their lineage to:
                    - Solar and lunar Kshatriya families from the Mahabharata.
                - Modern scholars emphasize that:
                    - Various social groups claimed Kshatriya status to legitimize their power.
                    - Some asserted a Brahman-Kshatriya lineage through maternal descent.
            - Rajputization and Agrarian Expansion:
                - The rise of Rajputs was linked to:
                    - Colonization of tribal lands by Brahmans, traders, and warriors.
                    - Economic advancements, including:
                        - Improved irrigation through wells and bunds.
                        - Cultivation of superior crops.
                    - In this process:
                        - Some cultivators became Rajputs.
                        - Others remained Shudras.
            - Brahman-Rajput Alliance:
                - The agniput legend (11th century) linked four major Rajput clans to a sacrificial fire by sage Vashishtha:
                    - Pratiharas
                    - Solankis (Chalukyas)
                    - Parmars (Pawars)
                    - Chauhans (Chahamans)
                - Brahmans further reinforced Rajput legitimacy:
                    - Wrote genealogies connecting ruling families to ancient Kshatriya lineages.
                    - Example: The Gurjar-Pratiharas were linked to Lakshman (pratihar or door-keeper of Rama).
            - Political and Cultural Impact:
                - Rajputs became champions of expansionist Hinduism.
                - They symbolized power by:
                    - Constructing grand temples.
                    - Granting land and wealth to Brahman priests.
            - Flexibility of the Caste System:
                - The varna system was not entirely rigid:
                    - Groups could rise or fall in status.
                    - New castes emerged based on occupation and social mobility.

        - Shudras, Dalits, and Slaves
            - Position of Shudras:
                - Historian D.C. Sircar noted the gradual elevation of Shudras’ status.
                - Though excluded from Vedic studies, they gained access to:
                    - Smarta rituals, including birth, death, and name-giving ceremonies.
                - Many tribal groups were absorbed into the Shudra category.
                - Jats, originally a pastoral nomadic tribe from Sind,
                    - Migrated to Punjab and took up agriculture and warfare.
                    - Despite being categorized as Shudras, they considered themselves equal to Rajputs.
            - Inter-Caste Marriages and New Castes:
                - Marriages between higher castes and Shudras were discouraged but did exist:
                    - Anuloma marriages: High-caste man marrying a lower-caste woman (socially accepted).
                    - Pratiloma marriages: Low-caste man marrying a high-caste woman (socially unacceptable).
                - Many new castes emerged from these unions, often based on profession:
                    - Potters, weavers, barbers, etc.
                    - Tribals integrating into Hindu society.
                - Deterioration of Dalits’ Status:
                    - Dalits (formerly called Antyaja or untouchables) performed occupations considered impure:
                        - Scavenging
                        - Skinning dead animals
                        - Shoemaking
                        - Hunting
                    - Dalits were excluded from the varna system and treated as a separate fifth category.
                    - They faced severe discrimination:
                        - Required to live separately from upper castes.
                        - Their shadow was considered polluting (as debated in shastras).
                        - Chandals had to strike a wooden board while walking to warn upper castes.
                        - Were denied land ownership in most areas.
                - Existence of Slavery:
                    - Slavery was present but differed from later Turkic practices.
                    - Sources of slaves:
                        - Prisoners of war
                        - Debtors who failed to repay loans
                        - Famine victims, who sold themselves or their family for food
                        - Women, often purchased for domestic labor or companionship
                    - Treatment of slaves:
                        - Slaves were generally treated better than Dalits.
                        - They were not used as soldiers but could fight under certain circumstances.
                    - Opportunities for freedom:
                        - A slave who saved their master’s life gained freedom and property rights.
                        - A female slave who bore a child to her master became free.
                        - Emancipation of slaves was considered a meritorious act, and rules were established for it.

    - Condition of Women
        - Perception and Social Status:
            - Women were generally regarded as mentally inferior and expected to obey their husbands blindly.
