                    - He captured Jalor, which was strategically important on the Gujarat route.
                    - However, he failed to extend his control over Gujarat and Malwa.
                    - The Chalukyas of Gujarat repelled his attack.
                    - The Paramaras of Malwa were too strong for Turkish forces.
                    - Iltutmish did manage to raid Malwa, plundering Ujjain and Raisina.
            - Post-Iltutmish Turmoil and Rajput Gains
                - After Iltutmish’s death, many Rajput rulers rebelled, reclaiming their territories.
                - The Bhatti Rajputs of Mewat:
                - Encircled Bayana
                - Raided up to Delhi’s outskirts
                - The fort of Gwalior was recovered by the Rajputs.
            - Balban’s Rajput Campaigns
                - Failed to capture Ranthambhor and Gwalior.
                - Ruthlessly subdued Mewat, ensuring Delhi’s safety from Mewati raids for a century.
                - Maintained control over Ajmer and Nagaur.
                - Secured Turkish rule in eastern Rajasthan, taking advantage of continued Rajput infighting.
        - Impact of These Struggles
            - The territorial consolidation of the Delhi Sultanate during this period laid the groundwork for future expansions.
            - Key achievements:
                - Established a strong monarchy
                - Repelled Mongol invasions
                - Secured the Ganga Doab
                - Maintained control over Eastern Rajasthan
            - These developments set the stage for the next phase in Delhi Sultanate history—its expansion into Western India and the Deccan.


- The Delhi Sultanate—II (Circa 1200-1400) The Khaljis and the Tughlaqs
    - Political Confusion After Balban’s Death (1286)
        - Succession Crisis in Delhi
            - Balban’s chosen successor, Prince Mahmud, had died earlier in a battle with the Mongols.
            - His second son, Bughra Khan, was invited by the nobles to assume the throne but preferred to rule over Bengal and Bihar.
            - A grandson of Baibars was installed as Sultan, but he was too young and inexperienced to manage the situation.
        - Resentment Against Turkish Nobility
            - Turkish nobles attempted to monopolize high offices, leading to considerable resentment.
            - Many non-Turks, particularly the Khaljis, had arrived in India during the Ghurid invasion but were not given recognition in Delhi.
            - As a result, they sought opportunities in Bengal and Bihar or served as soldiers in the northwest to counter Mongol threats.
        - Rise of Indian Muslims in the Nobility
            - Over time, Indian Muslims were admitted into the nobility but were denied high offices.
            - Dissatisfaction among them was evident, as seen in the support for Imaduddin Raihan against Balban.
        - Balban’s Precedent for Power Struggles
            - Balban himself had sidelined the sons of Nasiruddin Mahmud.
            - His example demonstrated that a successful general, with sufficient support from the nobility and the army, could seize the throne by ousting the heirs of an established dynasty.

    - The Khaljis (1290-1320)
        - Rise of the Khalji Dynasty
            - Led by Jalaluddin Khalji, a group of Khalji nobles overthrew the incompetent successors of Balban in 1290.
            - The Khaljis were of mixed Turkish-Afghan origin and did not exclude Turks from high offices but ended the Turkish monopoly.
            - Their rebellion was welcomed by the non-Turkish sections of the nobility.
        - Jalaluddin Khalji (1290-1296)
            - Ruled for six years and attempted to soften the harsh policies of Balban.
            - Advocated that the state should be based on the willing support of the governed.
            - Acknowledged that since most Indians were Hindus, the Delhi Sultanate could not be a purely Islamic state.
            - Practiced a policy of tolerance, avoiding harsh punishments.
            - His policies were viewed as weak and unsuitable for the times.
            - His rule ended when he was treacherously murdered by his nephew and son-in-law, Alauddin Khalji, in 1296.
        - Alauddin Khalji (1296-1316) – Ruthless Consolidation of Power
            - Came to power by murdering Jalaluddin Khalji after accumulating vast wealth from his invasion of Deogiri (in the Deccan).
            - Faced several rebellions, both from nobles and his own relations.
            - To suppress opposition, he adopted extreme measures:
                - Mass execution of defected nobles and confiscation of their properties.
                - Elimination of rebellious family members.
                - Massacre of Mongol converts who had settled in Delhi and rebelled after campaigning in Gujarat.
                - Harsh punishments extended even to wives and children of rebels, a practice continued by his successors.
        - Measures to Control the Nobility
            - Introduced strict regulations to prevent conspiracies:
                - Nobles were forbidden from holding banquets or forming marriage alliances without the Sultan’s approval.
                - Ban on wine and intoxicants to discourage gatherings.
                - Established a spy service to monitor nobles’ activities.
            - These methods successfully subjugated the nobles, ensuring no further rebellions during his lifetime.
            - However, his policies weakened the dynasty in the long run.
        - Decline of the Khaljis (1316-1320)
            - After Alauddin Khalji’s death in 1316, his trusted general Malik Kafur took control.
            - Malik Kafur:
                - Placed a minor son of Alauddin on the throne.
                - Imprisoned or blinded Alauddin’s other sons.
                - Was soon assassinated by palace guards.
            - Khusrau Khan, a Hindu convert, took the throne.
                - Accused by historians of violating Islam, but he was no worse than previous monarchs.
                - No opposition from Muslim nobles or the Delhi population.
                - Even Nizamuddin Auliya, the famous Sufi saint, accepted his rule.
                - Indicated that Muslims of Delhi were no longer swayed by racial considerations and accepted rulers based on ability rather than lineage.
            - In 1320, a group of officers led by Ghiyasuddin Tughlaq revolted.
            - Khusrau was defeated and killed, marking the end of the Khalji dynasty and the rise of the Tughlaq dynasty.

    - The Tughlaqs (1320-1412)
        - Establishment of the Tughlaq Dynasty
            - Founded by Ghiyasuddin Tughlaq in 1320.
            - The dynasty ruled Delhi until 1412.
        - Key Rulers of the Tughlaq Dynasty
            - Ghiyasuddin Tughlaq (1320-1324): Established the dynasty.
            - Muhammad bin Tughlaq (1324-1351): Expanded the empire extensively.
            - Firuz Shah Tughlaq (1351-1388): Ruled over a smaller but still vast empire.
        - Extent of the Tughlaq Empire
            - Under Ghiyasuddin and Muhammad bin Tughlaq, the empire encompassed almost the entire Indian subcontinent.
            - Firuz Shah Tughlaq’s empire was slightly smaller but comparable to that of Alauddin Khalji.
        - Disintegration of the Tughlaq Empire
            - After Firuz Shah’s death (1388), the Delhi Sultanate weakened and North India fragmented into several small states.
            - Though the Tughlaqs ruled until 1412, the invasion of Delhi by Timur in 1398 effectively marked the end of their empire.
        - Overview of the Tughlaq Era
            - The expansion of the Delhi Sultanate from Alauddin Khalji’s time.
            - The internal reforms introduced during the period.
            - The factors leading to the decline and disintegration of the Sultanate.

    - Expansion of the Delhi Sultanate
        - Delhi Sultanate’s Initial Control Over Rajasthan
            - Eastern Rajasthan, including Ajmer, had come under Delhi Sultanate’s control.
            - Ranthambhor, the most powerful Rajput state, had gone out of Delhi’s control since Balban’s reign.
            - Jalaluddin Khalji attempted to invade Ranthambhor but failed, leaving southern and western Rajasthan outside the Sultanate’s rule.
        - Expansion Under Alauddin Khalji (1290-1320)
            - Within 25 years, the Delhi Sultanate’s armies:
                - Brought Gujarat and Malwa under control.
                - Subdued most Rajput princes in Rajasthan.
                - Overran the Deccan and South India, reaching Madurai.
            - Attempted to bring the entire region under direct Delhi administration.
            - This phase of expansion began with Alauddin Khalji and reached its peak under Muhammad bin Tughlaq.
        - Political Conditions Favoring Expansion
            - Malwa, Gujarat, and Deogir were ruled by Rajput dynasties that emerged in the 12th-13th century.
            - These kingdoms remained largely unchanged in governance, despite Turkish rule in the Ganga Valley.
            - Inter-kingdom rivalries made the conquest of Malwa and Gujarat easier and pushed the invaders further south:
                - The Malwa and Deogir rulers opposed Turkish expansion into Gujarat.
                - Deogir was constantly at war with Warangal (Telangana) and Hoysala (Karnataka).
                - The Hoysalas were in conflict with their neighbors, the Pandyas (Tamil region/Mabar).
        - Importance of Malwa and Gujarat for the Delhi Sultanate
            - These regions were fertile and populous.
            - They controlled western seaports and trade routes connecting them to the Ganga Valley.
            - Gujarat’s overseas trade brought in gold and silver from abroad.
            - Delhi’s supply of war horses had been disrupted due to Mongol activity in Central and West Asia.
            - Gujarat’s ports were crucial for importing Arabian, Iraqi, and Turkish horses, which were vital for the Sultanate’s army.
        - Alauddin Khalji’s Conquest of Gujarat (1299)
            - In early 1299, an army under two of Alauddin’s generals marched towards Gujarat via Rajasthan.
            - On the way, they raided and captured Jaisalmer.
            - Gujarat ruler Rai Karan fled without resistance, leaving major cities vulnerable.
            - Anhilwara, the capital of Gujarat, along with many temples and buildings, was sacked.
            - The Somnath temple, rebuilt in the 12th century, was plundered.
            - Wealthy Muslim merchants of Cambay were not spared.
            - Malik Kafur, later a key figure in the South Indian invasions, was captured and taken to Alauddin Khalji.
            - Aftermath of Gujarat’s Conquest
                - Gujarat came under Delhi Sultanate’s control.
                - The ease of conquest suggests:
                    - Rai Karan was unpopular among his subjects.
                    - A discontented minister of Rai Karan possibly invited Alauddin’s invasion.
                    - The Gujarat army was weak, and the administration was inefficient.
                - Rai Karan fled to southern Gujarat with Ramachandra of Deogir’s help.
                - This later led to war between Delhi and the Yadavas of Deogir.
        - Alauddin Khalji’s Conquest of Rajasthan
            - The Conquest of Ranthambhor (1301)
                - After subjugating Gujarat, Alauddin Khalji focused on Rajasthan, beginning with Ranthambhor.
                - Ranthambhor was ruled by Hamirdeva, a Chauhan successor of Prithviraj Chauhan.
                - Hamirdeva had won victories against Raja Bhoj of Dhar and the Rana of Mewar, but these conquests ultimately led to his downfall.
                - During the return from the Gujarat campaign, some Mongol soldiers rebelled over the division of loot.
                - The rebellion was crushed, and a massacre of the Mongols followed.
                - Two Mongol nobles fled to Ranthambhor seeking asylum.
                - Alauddin ordered Hamirdeva to expel or kill the Mongol nobles, but Hamirdeva refused.
                - Confident in the strength of his fort, which had previously resisted Jalaluddin Khalji, Hamirdeva sent defiant replies to Alauddin.
                - Alauddin’s first army was repulsed, forcing him to lead the campaign himself.
                - Poet Amir Khusrau, who accompanied the campaign, gave a detailed account of the siege.
                - After three months, the Rajputs performed ‘jashar’ (jauhar):
                    - Women self-immolated on a funeral pyre.
                    - Rajput warriors and Mongols fought to the last.
                - This is the first Persian account of ‘jashar’.
                - Ranthambhor fell in 1301.
            - The Siege of Chittor (1303)
                - After Ranthambhor, Alauddin moved against Chittor, the second most powerful state in Rajasthan.
                - Ruler Ratan Singh had angered Alauddin by:
                    - Refusing passage to the Sultanate’s army for the Gujarat invasion.
                    - Controlling the Ajmer-Malwa route, a strategic corridor.
                - The Legend of Padmini:
                    - Some later accounts claim Alauddin attacked Chittor due to his desire for Ratan Singh’s queen, Padmini.
                    - However, modern historians dismiss this as a later myth, first recorded over a century later by Malik Muhammad Jaisi in his epic Padmavat.
                    - The legend’s fantastical elements (Padmini being from Singhaldvipa and Ratan Singh crossing seven seas to marry her) make it historically doubtful.
                    - The idea that Alauddin could demand to see another ruler’s wife—or that Ratan Singh would agree to such an insult—contradicts Rajput customs and Islamic court protocol.
                - The Siege and Fall of Chittor (1303):
                    - Alauddin laid a close siege to the fort.
                    - After months of resistance, the fort was stormed.
                    - Jashar (jauhar) was performed:
                        - Rajput warriors died fighting.
                        - Padmini and other queens committed self-immolation.
                    - Ratan Singh was captured alive and held prisoner for some time.
                    - Alauddin assigned Chittor to his son, Khizr Khan, and placed a Muslim garrison there.
                    - Later, the fort was handed back to Ratan Singh’s cousin.
            - Subjugation of Other Rajput States
                - Jalor, on the route to Gujarat, was also conquered.
                - Almost all major Rajput states submitted to Alauddin Khalji.
                - However, he did not establish direct rule over Rajasthan:
                    - Rajput rulers continued to govern but had to:
                        - Pay tribute regularly.
                        - Follow Delhi’s orders.
                    - Muslim garrisons were placed in strategic towns like Ajmer and Nagaur.
                - Rajasthan was thoroughly subdued, securing Alauddin’s dominance over North and Central India.
        - Expansion into the Deccan and South India
            - Conquest of Malwa (Before 1306-07)
                - Even before completing the conquest of Rajasthan, Alauddin Khalji had annexed Malwa.
                - According to Amir Khusrau, Malwa was so vast that even wise geographers could not determine its frontiers.
                - Unlike Rajasthan, Malwa was brought under direct administration, and a governor was appointed.
            - Alauddin Khalji’s Campaigns in the Deccan (1306-07)
                - Expedition Against Rai Karan (Baglana)
                    - In 1306-07, Alauddin launched two campaigns.
                    - The first was against Rai Karan, the expelled ruler of Gujarat, who had taken refuge in Baglana (on the Malwa border).
                    - Rai Karan resisted but was ultimately defeated.
                - Campaign Against Rai Ramachandra (Deogir)
                    - The second campaign targeted Rai Ramachandra of Deogir, an ally of Rai Karan.
                    - Rai Ramachandra had earlier agreed to pay annual tribute to Delhi but had fallen into arrears.
                    - Alauddin’s slave-general Malik Kafur led the expedition.
                    - Rai Ramachandra surrendered, was taken to Delhi, and treated with honor:
                        - Granted the title “Rai Rayan” (King of Kings).
                        - Gifted one lakh tankas and a golden canopy (symbolizing rulership).
                        - Given a district in Gujarat.
                        - His daughter was married to Alauddin.
                    - This alliance proved crucial for further Delhi Sultanate expansion into the Deccan.
            - Malik Kafur’s Southern Expeditions (1309-1311)
                - Between 1309-1311, Malik Kafur led two major campaigns into South India:
                - Warangal (1309, Telangana)
                    - Warangal, ruled by Prataparudra, was attacked.
                    - Prataparudra was forced to surrender treasures, elephants, and promise an annual tribute.
                - Dwar Samudra, Mabar & Madurai (1311, Karnataka & Tamil Nadu)
                    - The second campaign targeted Dwar Samudra (Karnataka), Mabar, and Madurai (Tamil Nadu).
                    - Key highlights:
                        - Amir Khusrau, the court poet, glorified these campaigns in a book.
                        - First time Muslim armies penetrated as far south as Madurai.
                        - Brought back immense wealth but no new geographical knowledge.
                        - Muslim merchants and soldiers were already present in the region.
                        - Kafur plundered temples (e.g., Chidambaram) but failed to defeat the Tamil armies.
                    - As a reward, Alauddin appointed Kafur as “mulk-nai” (vice-regent) of the empire.
            - Direct Administration & Rebellions (1315-1316)
                - After Alauddin’s death in 1316, his policies started shifting towards direct control of the south.
                - In 1315, Rai Ramachandra died, and his sons revolted.
                - Malik Kafur crushed the rebellion and took direct control.
                - However, some outlying areas declared independence, and some remained under Ramachandra’s descendants.
            - Mubarak Shah’s Campaigns (Post-Alauddin, 1316-1320)
                - Mubarak Shah, Alauddin’s successor, reconquered Deogir and installed a Muslim governor.
                - He also raided Warangal, forcing the ruler to:
                    - Cede a district.
                    - Pay an annual tribute of 40 gold bricks.
                - Khusrau Khan, a slave of Mubarak Shah, raided Mabar and sacked Masulipatnam.
            - Expansion Under Muhammad bin Tughlaq (1320-1328)
                - Conquest of Warangal (1320-1323)
                    - Ghiyasuddin Tughlaq (1320) adopted an aggressive expansion policy.
                    - His son, Muhammad bin Tughlaq, was posted in Deogir.
                    - Warangal was attacked again (1320-23):
                        - Initially, Muhammad bin Tughlaq suffered a setback.
                        - A rumor of the Sultan’s death caused panic among Delhi forces.
                        - He reorganized and attacked again, defeating the Rai.
                        - Warangal was annexed.
                - Conquest of Mabar & Orissa (1323-1324)
                    - Mabar was annexed, and Muhammad bin Tughlaq raided Orissa, bringing back rich plunder.
                    - The following year, he subdued Bengal (independent since Balban’s death).
                - Conquest of Kampili (1328, Karnataka)
                    - The last Hindu principality in the region, Kampili (South Karnataka), was annexed in 1328.
                    - A rebel cousin of Muhammad bin Tughlaq had taken refuge there, providing an excuse for conquest.
                - Challenges of Overexpansion
                    - By 1324, the Delhi Sultanate stretched as far south as Madurai and east to Orissa.
                    - This rapid expansion created huge administrative & financial challenges.
                    - The next section examines Muhammad bin Tughlaq’s attempts to manage these problems.

    - Internal Reforms and Experiments
        - Consolidation and Reforms under Alauddin Khalji
            - By the time Alauddin Khalji ascended the throne, the Delhi Sultanate was well consolidated in its central region, covering:
                - Upper Ganga Valley
                - Eastern Rajasthan
            - This stability enabled the sultans to undertake internal reforms and experiments aimed at:
                - Improving administration
                - Strengthening the army
                - Enhancing land revenue administration
                - Expanding and improving cultivation
                - Ensuring the welfare of citizens in rapidly growing towns
            - While some measures were successful and marked new developments, others failed due to:
                - Lack of experience
                - Poor planning
                - Opposition from vested interests
            - Despite the setbacks, these reforms reflected the growing stability of the Turkish state, which was no longer solely focused on warfare and maintaining law and order.
            - Market Control and Agrarian Policy of Alauddin
                - Market Control Measures
                    - Alauddin’s market control measures were considered one of the great wonders of the world.
                    - After returning from the Chittor campaign, he issued orders to fix prices of all commodities, including food grains, sugar, cooking oil, clothing, horses, cattle, and slaves.
                    - To implement this, he established three regulated markets in Delhi:
                        - One for food grains
                        - One for costly cloth
                        - One for horses, slaves, and cattle
                    - Each market was overseen by a high officer (stahna), who maintained merchant registers and strictly controlled prices and shopkeepers.
                    - Regulating food grain prices was essential because cheap food supply was necessary to maintain citizen and army support.
                - Reasons for Market Control
                    - The Mongol invasions highlighted the need for a large army, which required reducing prices and lowering salaries to sustain expenses.
                    - Land revenue in the Doab region (Meerut to Kara near Allahabad) was now paid directly to the state rather than being assigned as iqta.
                    - The land revenue rate was raised to 50% of the produce, forcing peasants to sell grains at low prices to banjaras (grain traders) who then sold it at fixed prices in cities.
                    - The banjaras were registered, and their families were held collectively responsible for violations to prevent hoarding.
                    - The state set up warehouses to store food grains, releasing them during famines or shortages.
                    - Alauddin strictly monitored the market and imposed harsh punishments on shopkeepers who charged higher prices or used false weights.
                - Fixed Prices and Economic Impact
                    - Fixed prices of grains (as per Barani):
                        - Wheat → 7½ jitals per man
                        - Barley → 4 jitals per man
                        - Good quality rice → 5 jitals per man
                    - Horse price regulation was crucial for maintaining a strong army:
                        - First-grade horse → 100-120 sankas
                        - Inferior pony (tata) → 10-25 tankas
                    - Prices of cattle and slaves were strictly regulated, and detailed price lists exist in Barani’s records.
                    - Luxury goods (cloth, perfumes, etc.) were also price-fixed, though this was likely done to stabilize overall prices or to please the nobility.
                    - Large sums were given to Multani traders to bring fine cloth to Delhi, making it the largest cloth market.
                - Impact on Salaries and Army
                    - The state’s direct control over land revenue allowed salaries to be paid in cash, a first in the Sultanate.
                    - Cavalry soldiers’ salary:
                        - 238 sankas per year (20 tankas per month)
                        - Covered personal expenses, horse maintenance, and equipment
                    - Even in Akbar’s time, when prices were higher, a Mughal cavalryman earned only 20 rupees a month—showing Alauddin’s salary was not low.
                - Religious and Trade Implications
                    - Historian Barani claims the market policy was aimed at punishing Hindus, as most traders were Hindus engaged in profiteering.
                    - However, trade with Central Asia was controlled by Muslims (Khurasanis, Multanis), who also benefited from price regulations—something Barani ignores.
                    - It is unclear if price control applied only to Delhi or the entire empire, but since army garrisons were spread across towns, other cities likely benefited as well.
            - Agrarian Reforms of Alauddin
                - Land Revenue Administration
                    - Alauddin was the first Sultan to insist on land revenue being assessed by measuring cultivated land in the Doab region.
                    - This prevented landlords (pahuts and mugaddams) from shifting their tax burdens onto poorer peasants.
                    - New policies for landlords:
                        - They had to pay taxes on cattle and houses, like regular peasants.
                        - Illegal cesses they had imposed on villagers were abolished.
                        - Barani’s account: They became so impoverished that they could no longer ride caparisoned horses or chew betel leaves, and their wives had to work in Muslim households.
                - Administration and Corruption Control
                    - Revenue collection was directly handled by state officials (amils) rather than landlords.
                    - Officials were given sufficient salaries, but Alauddin imposed strict audits on their accounts.
                    - Corrupt officials faced severe punishments, including beatings and imprisonment.
                    - Barani’s exaggeration: Officials were so terrified that no one would marry their daughters to them! However, government service remained prestigious, and this claim seems unlikely.
                - Motives Behind Agrarian Reforms
                    - Though Barani claims the policies targeted Hindus, they were mainly aimed at rural elites rather than common peasants.
                    - The reforms were not socialistic; they were pragmatic measures taken to:
                        - Counter Mongol threats
                        - Strengthen state control over resources
                        - Maintain a strong standing army
                    - Barani states Alauddin regulated everything, from food grains to socks, from combs to chapatis, leading to harsh punishments and resentment among traders.
                    - Despite strict measures, his policies did not cause major rebellions or mass migration of peasants.
                - Legacy of Agrarian and Market Reforms
                    - Alauddin’s regulations ended after his death, but they had long-lasting effects:
                        - Strengthened the Delhi Sultanate’s military
                        - Successfully repelled Mongol invasions beyond the Indus
                        - Provided a blueprint for later agrarian policies of Sher Shah Suri and Akbar
        - Muhammad Tughlaq’s Experiments and Policies
            - Introduction
                - Muhammad bin Tughlaq (1324-51) is remembered as one of the most remarkable rulers of his time.
                - He was deeply read in religion and philosophy and had an open and critical mind.
                - Engaged with Muslim mystics, Hindu yogis, and Jain saints like Jinaprabha Suri.
                - Orthodox theologians criticized him as a ‘rationalist.’
                - He appointed officials based on merit, disregarding noble lineage.
                - However, his hasty and impatient nature led to many failures, earning him the title of an ‘ill-starred idealist.’
            - Accession to the Throne
                - Succeeded his father, Sultan Ghiyasuddin Tughlaq, under suspicious circumstances.
                - A hastily erected wooden pavilion collapsed during a reception, killing the Sultan.
                - Rumors spread that Muhammad Tughlaq had orchestrated his father’s death.
            - Major Experiments and Policies
                - Transfer of Capital to Daulatabad
                    - Deogir (renamed Daulatabad) was an important military base for Turkish rule in the south.
                    - The Sultan wanted better control over the region, which was prone to rebellion.
                    - Many officials, nobles, and Sufi saints were forced to migrate to Daulatabad.
                    - A road was built with rest houses to facilitate travel.
                    - However, harsh weather and distance led to many deaths.
                    - Discontent arose as people longed for Delhi, leading to abandonment of the plan.
                    - Impact
                        - Strengthened cultural ties between North and South India.
                        - Many settlers remained in Daulatabad, leading to the spread of Indo-Islamic culture in the Deccan.
                - Introduction of Token Currency
                    - Introduced bronze coins with the same value as silver tankas due to a silver shortage.
                    - Inspired by Chinese (Qublai Khan) and Mongol (Ghazan Khan of Iran) experiments.
                    - Traders and common people resisted accepting the new currency.
                    - Widespread forgery devalued the coins.
                    - Sultan was forced to withdraw the currency, exchanging silver for legitimate bronze coins.
                    - Massive financial loss and administrative failure.
                - Military Campaigns and Expansionist Policies
                    - Defense Against Mongols
                        - Faced Mongol invasions under Tarmashrin in Punjab and Sindh.
                        - Successfully repelled Mongols and extended power beyond the Indus.
                    - The Khurasan Project
                        - Recruited a large army to invade Khurasan and Iraq but later disbanded it.
                        - Unclear objective, possibly to restore the Hindukush-Qandahar frontier.
                    - The Qarachil Expedition
                        - Military campaign in the Kumaon Hills (Himalayas).
                        - Some historians suggest it was aimed at controlling horse trade from China (Sinkiang).
                        - Expedition suffered a disaster—out of 10,000 soldiers, only 10 returned.
                        - Resulted in Delhi Sultanate’s control over hill regions like Kangra.
                - Agrarian Reforms and Revenue Policies
                    - Peasant Rebellion in the Doab
                        - Muhammad Tughlaq sought to improve agriculture but over-assessed revenue.
                        - Fixed state share at half of the produce, but arbitrarily determined the rate.
                        - Prices were artificially fixed, worsening the crisis.
                        - A six-year famine devastated the region.
                        - Peasants fled, prompting harsh measures to force compliance.
                    - Diwan-i-Amir-i-Kohi: Agricultural Development Scheme
                        - Established a new department to promote better farming.
                        - Divided the region into development blocks led by officials.
                        - Provided loans for high-yield crops like wheat, sugarcane, grapes, and dates.
                        - Scheme failed due to corruption and misuse of funds.
                        - Loans remained unrecovered, but later Sultan Firuz Tughlaq wrote them off.
                        - Despite failure, the policy influenced future rulers like Akbar.
                - Nobility and Administrative Policies
                    - Changes in Nobility Structure
                        - Included commoners from professions like barbers, cooks, weavers, and wine-makers in administration.
                        - Many were Muslim converts, and some Hindus were also given positions.
                        - Old noble families resented the rise of these new officials.
                    - Inclusion of Foreigners in Nobility
                        - Welcomed nobles from various ethnic backgrounds, leading to a diverse but divided nobility.
                        - This fragmentation weakened central authority and fostered rebellion.
            - Conclusion: The Paradox of Muhammad Tughlaq’s Rule
                - His reign marked both the zenith and beginning of disintegration of the Delhi Sultanate.
                - Many of his policies had long-term impacts, despite short-term failures.
                - His hasty nature and harsh punishments led to administrative instability.
                - The Delhi Sultanate never fully recovered from the setbacks of his reign.

    - Decline and Disintegration of the Delhi Sultanate: Firuz and His Successors
        - Rebellions During Muhammad Tughlaq’s Reign
            - The latter half of Muhammad Tughlaq’s reign saw repeated rebellions across the empire.
            - Rebellions by ambitious nobles in the outlying areas were common, but most were suppressed with the help of the central army and loyal nobles.
            - Major revolts occurred in Bengal, Mabar (Tamil Nadu), Warangal, Kampili (Karnataka), West Bengal, Awadh, Gujarat, and Sindh.
            - Muhammad Tughlaq, distrustful of everyone, moved constantly to suppress uprisings, exhausting his armies.
            - The most serious revolts were in South India, where local governors initiated rebellions.
            - A plague in his army during a campaign in the South killed two-thirds of his forces, dealing him a severe blow.
            - After returning from the South, a rebellion by brothers Harihara and Bukka led to the formation of the Vijayanagara Empire in South India.
            - In the Deccan, foreign nobles established a principality near Daulatabad, which grew into the Bahmani Empire.
            - Bengal also became independent, though Muhammad Tughlaq managed to quell revolts in Awadh, Gujarat, and Sindh.
            - He died in Sindh and was succeeded by his cousin, Firuz Tughlaq.
        - Challenges and Policies of Firuz Tughlaq
            - Upon accession, Firuz sought to prevent the disintegration of the Delhi Sultanate.
            - He appeased the nobles, army, and theologians while only asserting control over manageable regions.
            - Unlike his predecessors, he made no attempt to reassert control over South India and the Deccan.
        - Military Campaigns
            - Led two unsuccessful campaigns into Bengal, resulting in its permanent loss.
            - Campaigned against Jajnagar (Orissa), looted temples but did not annex the region.
            - Attacked Kangra in the Punjab hills.
            - Longest campaigns involved rebellions in Gujarat and Thatta, where the army suffered greatly in the Rann of Kutch.
        - Administrative and Military Reforms
            - Firuz was not a strong military leader but ensured a peaceful and stable reign.
            - Introduced hereditary succession for nobles:
                - A noble’s son inherited his office and estate.
                - If no son existed, the son-in-law or slave took over.
                - Abolished the torture of nobles and officials during audits.
            - Extended hereditary succession to the army:
                - Soldiers could pass their positions to their sons or sons-in-law, or even slaves.
                - Payments were made through land assignments instead of cash.
                - Led to corruption, as soldiers often bribed clerks to pass off useless horses.
        - Relations with Theologians and Religious Policies
            - Presented himself as a devout Islamic ruler to win the support of theologians.
            - Banned practices deemed un-Islamic, such as Muslim women visiting saints’ graves.
            - Persecuted heretical Muslim sects and made jizya a separate tax, exempting only women, children, the disabled, and the indigent.
            - Ordered the public execution of a Brahman for preaching against the Sharia.
            - Despite his religious orthodoxy, he patronized music and had a fondness for wine.
        - Social and Economic Policies
            - Banned cruel punishments like amputation for theft.
            - Established hospitals for free treatment of the poor.
            - Provided dowries for poor girls.
            - However, these benefits mainly helped Muslims from good families who had fallen on hard times.
            - Created a Public Works Department to manage infrastructure projects:
                - Repaired and dug irrigation canals (e.g., a 200-km canal from Sutlej to Hansi).
                - Established new towns like Hissar-Firuzah (Haryana) and Firuzabad (Uttar Pradesh), many of which still exist.
        - Slave Policy
            - Ordered officials to capture young boys from conquered areas and send them as slaves to the Sultan.
            - Accumulated about 180,000 slaves:
                - Some were trained as artisans and placed in royal workshops (Karkhanas).
                - Others formed a special corps of soldiers directly dependent on the Sultan.
            - However, like earlier Turkish sultans, Firuz faced difficulties in maintaining loyalty among these slave soldiers.
        - Disintegration After Firuz’s Death (1388)
            - After Firuz’s death, administrative and political instability returned.
            - Power struggles emerged between:
                - The Sultan and the nobles.
                - Local zamindars and rajas, who began acting independently.
                - Firuzian slaves, who attempted to install their own ruler.
            - Sultan Muhammad, Firuz’s son, initially consolidated power with slave support.
            - However, he later suppressed the Firuzian slaves, imprisoning and scattering them.
            - His successor, Nasiruddin Mahmud (1394-1412), was unable to control rebellious nobles and defiant rajas.
            - Provincial governors became independent, confining the Sultan’s rule to a small area around Delhi.
            - A contemporary wit remarked:
                - “The dominion of the Lord of the Universe (title of the sultans of Delhi) extends from Delhi to Palam.”
        - Timur’s Invasion (1398) and Final Collapse
            - The weakness of the Delhi Sultanate invited Timur’s invasion in 1398.
            - Timur, a Turk claiming descent from Genghis Khan, had built a vast empire from Syria to the Indus.
            - His invasion of India was purely for plunder, targeting the wealth accumulated by Delhi’s sultans.
            - Timur’s army mercilessly sacked and plundered towns en route to Delhi.
            - Upon entering Delhi, his forces massacred Hindus, Muslims, women, and children alike.
            - The invasion drained India of enormous wealth, including gold, silver, and jewelry.
            - Timur also took skilled Indian artisans (masons, stone cutters, carpenters) to build monuments in Samarqand.
            - Impact of Timur’s Invasion
                - Though politically insignificant, the invasion marked:
                    - The end of strong rule by the Delhi sultans.
                    - The final decline of the Tughlaq dynasty, which lingered until 1412.
        - Long-Term Causes of Disintegration
            - The Delhi Sultanate’s decline was not due to a single ruler but a combination of factors:
                - Constant conflict between the monarch and nobles.
                - Struggles with local rulers and zamindars.
                - The pull of regional and geographical forces.
            - No ruler could fundamentally change society to overcome these persistent challenges.
            - The overextension of the empire under Ghiyasuddin and Muhammad Tughlaq set off a chain reaction of disintegration.
            - Firuz’s reforms—though stabilizing in the short term—weakened the central administration, leading to ultimate collapse.
        - Conclusion
            - The period from 1200 to 1400 brought significant transformations in Indian society:
                - Changes in the system of government.
                - Evolution of social and economic conditions.
                - Advancements in art and architecture.
            - These themes will be explored in the next chapter.

- Government, and Economic and Social Life under the Delhi Sultanate
    - Introduction
        - The establishment and expansion of the Delhi Sultanate (1200–1400) brought significant changes to India:
            - Development of a highly centralized state under the Turks.
            - Expansion of territorial control, reaching as far south as Madurai.
            - Influence on the administrative systems of successor states and the later Mughal Empire.
        - The beginning of the 15th century marked the decline of the Delhi Sultanate:
            - Disintegration led to the rise of independent regional states.
            - Despite political fragmentation, the Sultanate’s administrative legacy endured.

    - The Sultan: Supreme Authority in the Sultanate
        - Symbolic Connection with the Caliph
            - Many Turkish sultans in India declared themselves as the “Lieutenant of the Faithful”, recognizing the Abbasid caliph in Baghdad.
            - However, this was a symbolic and moral recognition, not a legal subordination.
            - By doing so, the Delhi Sultans asserted their authority within the larger Islamic world.
        - Powers and Responsibilities of the Sultan
            - The Sultan was the most powerful figure in the Sultanate, holding supreme political, military, and legal authority.
            - His primary responsibilities included:
                - Administration – Ensuring the smooth governance of the state.
                - Military leadership – Serving as the commander-in-chief of the armed forces.
                - Law and justice – Appointing judges while also acting as the final court of appeal.
            - Direct appeals could be made to the Sultan against any unjust actions by his officials.
            - Justice as a key function:
                - Balban was known for stern and impartial justice, punishing even his relatives and top officials.
                - Muhammad Tughlaq extended this policy to the religious classes (ulama), who had previously been exempt from severe punishments.
        - Succession and the Struggle for the Throne
            - No clear law of succession existed among the Muslim rulers of India.
            - Islamic theory favored election, but in practice:
                - Any son of a ruler could claim the throne.
                - The concept of primogeniture (eldest son inheriting the throne) was not fully accepted by either Muslims or Hindus.
                - Some rulers nominated a preferred successor, not necessarily the eldest son.
                - Iltutmish even nominated his daughter (Razia Sultan) over his sons.
            - However, noble approval was necessary for any nomination to be accepted.
            - Military strength was the decisive factor in succession:
                - A successful military leader could usurp the throne.
                - Public opinion also played a role—for example, the Khaljis avoided entering Delhi immediately after overthrowing Balban’s successors, instead building a new city, Siri, to establish their rule.

    - Central Administration of the Delhi Sultanate
        - The Role of the Sultan and His Ministers
                - The Sultan was the supreme authority and was assisted by a council of ministers.
                - These ministers were appointed at the Sultan’s discretion and could be removed at any time.
                - The number, powers, and functions of the ministers varied over time.
                - A well-defined administrative system emerged by the late 13th century.
        - The Wazir: Chief Minister of the Sultanate
            - The wazir was the most important administrative official after the Sultan.
            - Early Period:
                - Initially, wazirs were primarily military leaders.
            - 14th Century Changes:
                - The wazir became an expert in revenue affairs and controlled both income and expenditure.
                - Muhammad Tughlaq focused heavily on revenue administration, appointing Khwaja Jahan as his respected wazir.
                - A separate Auditor General (for expenditure) and an Accountant General (for income) were introduced.
            - Wazirs under Firuz Tughlaq:
                - Khan-i-Jahan, a converted Tailang Brahman, was appointed as wazir and held office for 18 years—a period considered the peak of the wazir’s influence.
                - His son, Khan-i-Jahan II, succeeded him but lost power after trying to manipulate succession following Firuz’s death.
            - The position of the wazir weakened after this period but regained prominence under the Mughals.
        - The Military Administration (Diwan-i-Arz)
            - The Diwan-i-Arz was responsible for military affairs and was led by the Ariz-i-Mamalik.
            - Key Responsibilities:
                - Recruitment, equipping, and payment of the army.
                - Maintaining troop discipline and military organization.
            - The Sultan as Commander-in-Chief:
                - The Sultan himself commanded the army to prevent power struggles.
            - Notable Military Reforms:
                - Balban emphasized military discipline and had soldiers march long distances under the pretext of hunting.
                - Alauddin Khalji introduced:
                    - Regular mustering of troops to prevent fraud.
                    - Branding system (dagh) for horses to ensure quality.
                    - Descriptive rolls of soldiers for better record-keeping.
                    - Direct salary payments in cash, abolishing the previous system of land grants (jagirs) for soldiers.
                - Alauddin’s army (claimed to be 300,000 by Barani) was instrumental in defending against Mongol invasions and conquering the Deccan.
            - Military Composition and Structure
                - The standing army was stationed across the empire, with a strong force in the capital.
                - The military included:
                    - Cavalry: Dominated by Turks and Afghans (considered the most prestigious).
                    - Infantry: Included Hindus, continuing from the Ghaznavid period.
                    - Elephants: Trained for warfare.
                    - Sappers and Miners Corps: Cleared roads and removed obstacles during marches.
        - Other Key Administrative Departments
            - The Religious and Judicial Department (Diwan-i-Risalat)
                - Functions:
                    - Managed religious affairs, pious foundations, and stipends for scholars and religious leaders.
                    - Supervised by the Chief Sadr, often a leading gazi.
                    - The Chief Gazi also headed the justice department.
                - Judicial System:
                    - Qazis (judges) were appointed across the empire, particularly in regions with large Muslim populations.
                    - Muslim law (Sharia) governed civil matters for Muslims.
                    - Hindus followed their own personal laws, enforced by village panchayats and caste leaders.
                    - Criminal law was based on regulations set by the Sultan.
            - The Communication & Correspondence Department (Diwan-i-Insha)
                - Handled state correspondence, including:
                    - Communication between the Sultan and other rulers.
                    - Correspondence with provincial and local officials.
                    - Management of formal and confidential diplomatic exchanges.
            - Intelligence and Internal Security
                - The Sultan maintained a network of intelligence agents (barids) across the empire.
                - The Chief Barid was always a trusted noble of the ruler.
                - This system kept the Sultan informed of developments across the empire.
        - The Royal Household and State Affairs
            - The Royal Household Department
                - Managed by the Wakil-i-Dar, who was responsible for:
                    - The Sultan’s personal comforts.
                    - Overseeing the large royal harem.
                    - Maintaining order and seating arrangements for nobles at royal receptions.
                - Managed a network of karkhanas (workshops) that:
                    - Stored goods needed by the royal family.
                    - Sometimes produced luxury items under royal supervision.
            - The Department of Public Works
                - Established by Firuz Tughlaq, it was responsible for:
                    - Construction of canals for irrigation.
                    - Building roads, mosques, and public buildings.
            - The Slave Department
                - Also introduced by Firuz Tughlaq to manage:
                    - The employment of royal slaves.
                    - Their work in state-run karkhanas and administrative offices.
        - Conclusion
            - The central administration of the Delhi Sultanate was highly structured, with clear divisions of responsibility. 
            - The Sultan held absolute power, assisted by a strong bureaucracy led by the wazir, and supported by specialized departments for military, finance, justice, intelligence, and public works. 
            - These institutions laid the foundation for later Mughal governance.
                    
    - Local Administration of the Delhi Sultanate
        - Division of Territories
            - After their conquest, the Turks divided the country into administrative units called igtas.
            - These were granted to leading Turkish nobles, who were known as mugtis or walis.
            - Over time, these igtas evolved into provinces (subas).
            - During the reign of Muhammad Tughlaq, there were 24 provinces, extending as far as Mabar in the south.
        - Role of the Mugti (Provincial Governor)
            - Initially, mugtis had significant autonomy in their regions.
            - Their main responsibilities included:
                - Maintaining law and order.
                - Collecting land revenue on behalf of the government.
                - Paying salaries to soldiers from the revenue collected.
                - Retaining any surplus after expenses.
            - As the central government gained strength, it tightened control over the mugtis:
                - It started to assess the actual income of provinces.
                - Salaries of soldiers and mugtis were fixed in cash.
                - Mugtis were required to remit excess revenue to the central treasury.
            - Auditing of provincial accounts (done every few years) often involved:
                - Harsh measures, including torture and imprisonment of mugtis.
                - Firuz Tughlaq later relaxed these strict policies towards the end of the Sultanate period.
        - Hierarchical Structure of Local Administration
            - Administrative Divisions Below Provinces
                - Below the provinces, the empire was divided into:
                    - Sarkars (Districts)
                    - Parganas (Administrative Units)
                    - Villages (basic unit of administration)
            - The Pargana Administration
                - Villages were grouped into parganas, which formed the core of local administration.
                - Ami (Administrator): Headed the pargana.
                - Important village officials included:
                    - Zamindars (landowners)
                    - Muqaddam (village headman)
                    - Patwari (village accountant)
        - Village Administration
            - Villages retained their traditional self-governing structure under Turkish rule.
            - The Sultanate did not interfere in village affairs as long as land revenue was paid.
            - Villages were traditionally grouped into units of 100 to 84 households (chasrasi system).
            - No significant administrative changes were initially made at the village level.
            - Land revenue collection continued through local officials, aiding the smooth transition of power to the Turks.
        - Reforms and Peasant Resistance
            - Administrative changes began under Alauddin Khalji in the early 14th century.
            - He introduced stricter revenue policies to increase state control.
            - These measures led to tensions between the government and peasants, sometimes resulting in rebellions.
        - Conclusion
            - The local administration of the Delhi Sultanate was a multi-tiered system, with provinces, districts, and villages functioning under different levels of authority. 
            - The mugtis initially had autonomy but were later brought under tighter central control. 
            - The village administration remained largely unchanged, ensuring stability, but Alauddin Khalji’s revenue reforms led to conflicts.

    - Economic and Social Life under the Delhi Sultanate
        - Limited Historical Records
            - Information about the economic condition of people during the Delhi Sultanate is scarce.
            - Contemporary historians primarily focused on court events rather than the lives of ordinary people.
            - However, some sources mention commodity prices and agricultural products.
            - Despite limited historical records, accounts like those of Ibn Battutah provide valuable insights into agriculture, trade, and daily life during the Delhi Sultanate. 
            - The economy was primarily agrarian, with village industries playing a crucial role in production and trade.
        - Ibn Battutah’s Observations
            - Ibn Battutah, a traveler from Tangier, North Africa, visited India in the 14th century.
            - He lived at the court of Muhammad Tughlaq for eight years and traveled widely across India.
            - His writings provide insights into:
                - Agricultural products (fruits, flowers, herbs, crops).
                - Condition of roads and trade routes.
                - Daily life of the people.
        - Agriculture and Crops
            - Agriculture was the backbone of the economy, with fertile soil allowing multiple harvests.
            - Key crops grown in different regions:
                - East and South: Rice, sugarcane (grown extensively).
                - North: Wheat, oilseeds.
                - Other major crops:
                    - Rice: Sown three times a year.
                    - Sesame: Used for oil production.
                    - Indigo: Important for dyeing fabrics.
                    - Cotton: Essential for the textile industry.
        - Village Industries and Economy
            - Agriculture supported small-scale village industries, including:
                - Oil pressing (from sesame and other seeds).
                - Jaggery (raw sugar) production.
                - Weaving and dyeing of cloth (linked to cotton and indigo cultivation).
            - These industries formed the economic backbone of villages, sustaining local economies.
        - Peasants and Rural Gentry under the Delhi Sultanate
            - The Peasant Majority
                - Peasants formed the overwhelming majority of the population.
                - They worked hard to sustain themselves but lived at a subsistence level.
                - Recurring famines and wars further worsened their hardships.
            - Prosperous Peasants and Rural Elites
                - Not all peasants lived in poverty; some were owner-cultivators with their own land.
                - These wealthier peasants were:
                    - Original settlers of the village.
                    - Dominant members of the village panchayat.
                - The village headmen (muqaddams) and smaller landlords (zaats):
                    - Enjoyed a higher standard of living.
                    - Held additional lands at concessional revenue rates.
                    - Sometimes exploited ordinary peasants by forcing them to pay extra revenue.
                - Their prosperity was evident as they:
                    - Rode costly Arabi and Iraqi horses.
                    - Wore fine clothes.
                    - Adopted upper-class lifestyles.
            - Alauddin Khalji’s Reforms
                - Alauddin Khalji took strict action against wealthy village elites:
                    - Restricted their privileges.
                    - Reduced their economic power.
                - However, after his death, these groups regained their dominance.
            - Hindu Rais and Autonomous Rajas
                - A distinct wealthy section included the Hindu rais (autonomous rajas).
                - Many retained their estates under Delhi Sultanate rule.
                - Evidence from Balban’s court suggests that:
                    - Hindu rais frequently visited the sultan’s court.
                    - They remained influential even in areas under direct Sultanate control.
        - Trade, Industry, and the Merchants
            - Growth of Trade and Urban Economy
                - The consolidation of the Delhi Sultanate, improved communications, and a sound currency system (silver tanka and copper dirham) led to the growth of trade.
                - Towns expanded, and urban life flourished. Ibn Battutah described Delhi as the largest city in the eastern Islamic world, with Daulatabad (Deogir) matching its size, reflecting strong trade between the north and south.
                - Other major trade centers:
                    - Northwest – Lahore, Multan
                    - East – Kara, Lakhnauti
                    - West – Anhilwara (Patan), Cambay (Khambayat)
                - A historian describes the Sultanate as having a “flourishing urban economy,” implying large-scale commerce.
            - Industries and Handicrafts
                - Bengal and Gujarat towns were known for fine fabrics, with other cities also producing high-quality textiles.
                - Major industrial centers and products:
                    - Cambay (Gujarat) – Textiles, gold, and silver work
                    - Sonatgaon (Bengal) – Raw silk and fine cotton (later called muslin)
                    - Other crafts: Leatherwork, metalwork, carpet weaving, woodwork (including furniture), and stone-cutting.
                - The Turks introduced new crafts such as paper-making, a technique originally discovered by the Chinese and spread via the Arab world.
            - Technological Advancements
                - Textile industry improvements:
                    - Introduction of the spinning wheel, which improved efficiency.
                    - Cotton cleaning was enhanced using the cotton carder’s bow (dunia).
                - Metallurgical advancements:
                    - Use of iron stirrups.
                    - Large-scale use of armor for cavalry.
                    - Growth in metal crafts.
                - Water-lifting technology:
                    - Improvement of the rakat (misnamed the Persian wheel) allowed deeper water extraction for irrigation.
                - Construction innovations:
                    - Introduction of a superior mortar enabled the construction of grand buildings featuring arches and domes.
            - Foreign Trade and Commerce
                - Indian exports:
                    - Textiles (high demand in Red Sea and Persian Gulf trade, later introduced to China).
                - Imports:
                    - West Asia – Satin, glassware, horses
                    - China – Raw silk, porcelain
                    - Africa – Ivory
                    - Southeast Asia – Spices
                - India had a favorable trade balance, bringing gold and silver into the country.
            - Merchant Communities
                - Overseas and coastal trade:
                    - Arabs were dominant in Indian Ocean trade but did not displace Indian traders (Tamils, Kalingas, Gujaratis – both Hindu and Muslim).
                    - Coastal and north Indian trade was largely controlled by Marwaris and Gujaratis (many were Jains).
                    - Muslim Bohra merchants were also active.
                - Overland trade (Central & West Asia):
                    - Controlled by Multanis (mostly Hindus, but also included Muslims like Khurasanis, Afghans, Iranians).
                    - Many merchants had settled in Delhi.
                - Wealth of merchants:
                    - The rich Gujarati and Marwari merchants, especially Jains, funded temple construction.
                    - Cambay was home to wealthy merchants with grand stone houses, gardens, and tanks.
                    - Hindu and Muslim merchants lived luxuriously, attended by sword-bearing pages.
                    - Multani merchants were so wealthy that Delhi’s nobles often borrowed money from them for celebrations.
            - Transport, Communication, and Postal System
                - Travel was dangerous due to robbers and marauding tribes. However, royal roads were well maintained, with sarais for travelers.
                - Major roads built:
                    - Peshawar to Sonargaon (built earlier).
                    - Delhi to Daulatabad (built by Muhammad Tughlaq).
                - Postal system:
                    - Relays of horses and runners ensured fast delivery.
                    - Runners, posted at towers every few kilometers, rang bells to signal the next relay.
                    - Efficiency examples:
                        - Fresh fruits from Khurasan reached the Sultan quickly.
                        - Ganga water was delivered to Muhammad Tughlaq in Daulatabad (40-day journey from Delhi).
            - Economic Growth and Prosperity
                - Improved communications and trade (both overland and by sea) accelerated economic activity.
                - The Turks introduced or popularized several crafts and techniques, strengthening industries.
                - The second half of the 14th century was marked by economic growth and relative affluence due to technological advancements and agricultural expansion.

    - The Sultan and the Nobles
        - The Sultan and his royal lifestyle
            - Palaces and Grandeur:
                - The Sultan and nobles lived luxuriously, comparable to the ruling elites of West and Central Asia.
                - Almost every Sultan built his own palace.
                - Balban had a magnificent court designed to instill awe.
                - Alauddin Khalji and successors continued this tradition.
                - Muhammad Tughlaq’s palace (as described by Ibn Battutah):
                    - Visitors passed through three heavily guarded gates.
                    - The ‘Court of Thousand Pillars’ was a vast hall supported by polished wooden pillars, lavishly decorated.
                    - This hall served as the public court of the Sultan.
            - Royal Gifts and Honors:
                - Muhammad Tughlaq gifted two robes of honor (summer & winter) to each noble annually.
                - Estimated 200,000 robes were distributed each year.
                - Made of imported fabrics—velvet, damask, wool—with brocade and costly materials.
                - Nobles received lavish gifts on occasions like:
                    - Sultan’s birthday
                    - Navroz (Persian New Year)
                    - Annual coronation day
            - Royal Treasury and Luxury Goods
                - Aarkhanas (Imperial Workshops):
                    - Supplied silk, gold, silverware, and luxury goods.
                    - Rare and exquisite items were sourced from across the empire.
                    - Firuz Tughlaq ordered superintendents to buy finely crafted items at any price.
                    - Extravagance was common: A single pair of shoes for the Sultan cost 70,000 tankas.
                    - Gold, silver, and jewels adorned most royal items.
                    - The royal treasury also provided luxuries for the Harem.
            - The Harem and Royal Household
                - Composition of the Harem:
                    - Contained queens and numerous female slaves from various countries.
                    - Included female relatives of the Sultan (mother, aunts, etc.).
                    - Had a large staff of servants and guards.
                    - Separate accommodations were provided for each member.
        - Nobles and Their Ostentatious Lifestyle
            - Control and Revival:
                    - Alauddin Khalji suppressed the nobility, restricting their excesses.
                    - Under the Tughlaqs, nobles regained their power and wealth.
            - Magnificent Palaces & Wealth:
                - Nobles imitated the Sultan’s extravagant lifestyle.
                - Lived in lavish palaces, wore costly apparel.
                - Surrounded by servants, slaves, and retainers.
                - Competed in hosting grand feasts and festivals.
                - Some nobles also patronized art and literature.
            - Salaries and Wealth of the Nobles
                - Under Muhammad Tughlaq:
                    - Vizier’s (chief minister’s) income = As large as the province of Iraq.
                    - Leading ministers: Earned 20,000–40,000 tankas/year.
                    - Chief Sadr (religious head): Received 60,000 tankas/year.
                    - Khan-i-Jahan (wazir of Firuz Tughlaq): Earned 15 lakh tankas annually.
                - Wealth Accumulation:
                    - Bashir (Firuz’s Ariz-i-Mamalik) left 13 crore tankas at death.
                    - Firuz confiscated most of it, distributing the rest to Bashir’s sons.
                    - Generally, nobles’ wealth remained secure and was inherited by their heirs.
            - Economic Influence and Land Ownership
                - Investment in Land & Agriculture:
                    - Many nobles purchased land and built gardens, orchards, and markets.
                    - Nobles & Sultans improved fruit cultivation (especially melons and grapes).
                    - Firuz Tughlaq planted 1,200 orchards near Delhi.
                - Decline of Nobility’s Power:
                    - Breakup of the Delhi Sultanate (early 15th century) disrupted these trends.
                
    - Town Life: Slaves, Artisans, and Others
        - Composition of Town Population
            - Turkish ruling class favored urban life.
            - Many towns developed around military garrisons and later became cultural centers.
            - Towns had a diverse population, including:
                - Nobles
                - Government clerks & officials – Educated by ulama, shared similar views.
                - Shopkeepers & artisans – Engaged in trade and handicrafts.
                - Beggars & commoners – Some carried arms, causing law-and-order challenges.
        - Slavery and Domestic Servants
            - Origins & Trade of Slaves
                - Slavery existed in India, West Asia, and Europe.
                - Sources of slaves:
                    - War captives – Most common method (practiced by Turks & Hindus).
                    - Born in household, purchased, or inherited (as per Hindu Shastras).
                    - Imported slaves from Africa (mainly Abyssinians).
            - Uses of Slaves
                - Domestic service and personal attendants.
                - Skilled labor (e.g., artisans, craftsmen).
                - Companionship.
                - Military service (e.g., Firuz Tughlaq employed 180,000 slaves).
                - Royal bodyguards and court duties.
            - Living Conditions of Slaves
                - Masters had to provide food & shelter.
                - Slaves could marry & own property.
                - Harsh treatment was common, but freeing slaves was considered meritorious (both among Hindus & Muslims).
        - Economic Conditions and Social Inequality
            - Food Prices & Cost of Living
                - Alauddin Khalji’s era (low prices):
                    - Wheat – 7½ jitals per man (135 kg).
                    - Barley – 4 jitals.
                    - Rice – 5 jitals.
                    - (48 jitals = 1 silver tanka).
                - Muhammad Tughlaq’s reign:
                    - Prices rose sharply.
                - Firuz Tughlaq’s era:
                    - Prices declined again (possibly due to expanded cultivation).
            - Wages & Standard of Living
                - Firuz Tughlaq’s period:
                    - A family (man, wife, servant, 1-2 children) could live on 5 tankas/month.
                - Alauddin Khalji’s period:
                    - Artisan wages – 2-3 jitals/day (14-20 tankas/month).
                    - Household servants – Earned even less.
                - Under Akbar:
                    - Unskilled laborer’s wages – 2-4 rupees/month.
                    - Hard living conditions for artisans & workers.
        - Social Inequality & Urban Lifestyle
            - Nobles & wealthy merchants (Hindus & Muslims) led lavish lives.
            - Commoners faced hardships, but their lives were enriched by festivals & fairs.
            - Rural Hindu society had fewer inequalities than urban Muslim society.

    - Caste, Social Manners, and Customs
        - Structure of Hindu Society
            - No major changes in Hindu social structure.
            - Brahmans continued to hold high status but were criticized if found unworthy.
            - Smriti texts reaffirmed caste duties:
                - Brahmans could engage in agriculture, even in normal times.
                - Kshatriyas were the only caste allowed to wield weapons and protect society.
                - Shudras had limited rights:
                    - Their highest duty was serving other castes.
                    - Could work in any occupation, except liquor & meat trade.
                    - Forbidden from studying & reciting the Vedas, but allowed to listen to Puranas.
                - Extreme caste restrictions:
                    - Some texts forbade eating a Shudra’s food, living with them, sitting on the same cot, or even receiving religious instruction from a learned Shudra.
                    - The harshest restrictions were imposed on Chandalas and ‘outcastes’.
        - Position of Women in Hindu Society
            - Early marriage for girls remained the norm.
            - Duties of a wife:
                - Complete service & devotion to her husband.
                - Marriage annulment was allowed in special cases (e.g., desertion, loathsome disease), but not universally accepted.
            - Widow remarriage was prohibited in the Kali Age, but mostly for upper castes.
            - Practice of Sati:
                - Some texts strongly approved it, while others set conditions.
                - Ibn Battuta reported that women burned themselves on their husband’s pyre with drum beating & official permission from the Sultan.
            - Women’s Property Rights
                - A widow had the right to inherit her husband’s property if it was not jointly owned.
                - She was not just a guardian of the property but had full disposal rights.
                - This marked a slight improvement in women’s property rights under Hindu law.
            - Rise of Purdah System
                - Purdah (seclusion of women) became widespread among upper-class Hindu women.
                - Origins & Influence:
                    - Practiced in ancient Iran & Greece.
                    - Arabs & Turks introduced it to India, influencing Hindu elites.
                    - Fear of invasions led to increased female seclusion.
                - Social & Religious Impact:
                    - Symbol of status among upper classes.
                    - Found religious justifications.
                    - Hindered women’s freedom, making them more dependent on men.
        - Social Divisions in Muslim Society
            - Muslims were divided into ethnic & racial groups:
                - Turks, Iranians, Afghans, Indian Muslims rarely intermarried.
                - Converts from lower Hindu castes faced discrimination.
            - Economic disparities were sharp, with some groups being wealthy nobles and others poor converts.
        - Hindu-Muslim Social Relations
            - Limited interaction between Hindu & Muslim upper classes:
                - Hindus restricted intermarriage & inter-dining with Muslims, applying Shudra-like rules to them.
            - Social & Administrative Cooperation:
                - Hindus and Muslims did not live in total isolation.
                - Hindus served in Muslim armies and as personal managers of nobles.
                - Local administration remained largely in Hindu hands.
                - Daily social & economic interactions were common despite caste restrictions.
            - Cultural Differences & Social Tensions:
                - Differences in customs, beliefs, and interests created tensions.
                - These differences slowed down mutual understanding and cultural assimilation.
                - Further developments in Hindu-Muslim relations will be discussed in a later section.

    - Nature of the State
        - Militaristic and Aristocratic Structure
            - The Turkish state in India was militaristic and aristocratic.
            - Turkish nobles initially monopolized high offices, excluding Tajiks, Afghans, and other non-Turkish groups.
            - Under the Tughlaqs, the nobility expanded, but noble birth remained crucial for high positions.
            - Muslims in towns had better chances for army enrollment and state jobs.
            - Hindus dominated trade and held positions in:
                - Rural aristocracy
                - Lower administrative offices
                - Their cooperation was essential for governance.
            - Power Struggles
                - A tacit power-sharing existed between:
                    - Rural Hindu aristocracy
                    - City-based Muslim administrators
                - Frequent conflicts arose over:
                    - Power and land (rather than religious differences).
                    - Control over surplus production from land.
                - Muslims also fought among themselves over power.
        - Islamic Character of the State
            - Formally, the Sultanate claimed to be Islamic.
            - Sultans emphasized the Muslim identity of the state by:
                - Following the Sharia as much as possible.
                - Preventing open violations of Islamic law.
                - Appointing Muslim theologians (ulama) to high offices.
                - Granting revenue-free lands to Muslim scholars.
            - Limits of Religious Authority
                - Sultans did not let theologians dictate state policies.
                - Example:
                    - Under Iltutmish, a group of theologians demanded strict enforcement of Islamic law, forcing Hindus to convert or face death.
                    - The wazir rejected this, stating that Muslims were too few to implement such policies.
            Zawabit (State Laws)
                - Since Sharia was not always practical, sultans framed their own state regulations (zawabit).
                - Example:
                    - Alauddin Khalji openly stated that he framed laws based on state needs, not religious laws.
                    - Historian Barani criticized the Delhi Sultanate as not truly Islamic but secular (Gehandart) in its considerations.
        - Status of Hindu Subjects
            - Hindus were given the status of zimmis (protected people).
            - In exchange for Muslim rule, they paid a tax called jizyah.
            - Jizyah Taxation
                - Purpose:
                    - A tax in lieu of military service.
                    - Levied on a graduated scale based on means.
                - Exemptions:
                    - Women, children, the poor, and Brahmins (initially).
                - Collection:
                    - Originally collected along with land revenue (as most cultivators were Hindus).
                    - Firuz Tughlaq later made it a separate tax and imposed it on Brahmins.
                - Misuse:
                    - Some theologians harassed Hindus using jizyah collection.
                    - However, jizyah alone was not a tool for forced conversion.
        - Privileged Classes and Social Hierarchy
            - Medieval states were based on privilege, not equality.
            - Before the Turks:
                - Rajputs & Brahmins were privileged.
            - Under the Sultanate:
                - Turks replaced Rajputs in privileged status.
                - Later, the privileged group included:
                    - Turks, Iranians, Afghans, and some Indian Muslims.
                    - Muslim theologians also held special privileges.
            - For the majority of Hindus, these political shifts had little impact on daily life.
        - Conclusion
            - The Sultanate was militaristic and aristocratic, dominated by a narrow clique of military leaders under the Sultan’s authority.
            - While claiming to be Islamic, it was pragmatic and often secular in governance.

    - Religious Freedom Under the Sultanate
        - Early Conquests and Treatment of Temples
            - During the early phase of conquest, many cities were sacked, and temples were targeted.
            - Reasons for targeting temples:
                - Justification of conquest.
                - Seizing treasures believed to be stored in them.
            - Hindu temples were converted into mosques.
                - Example: Quwwat-ul-Islam Mosque near Qutub Minar, Delhi.
                    - Originally a Vishnu temple.
                    - Inner sanctum (with deity) was demolished.
                    - Arches with Quranic verses were added.
                    - Temple pillars were reused to build a cloister.
                - Similar incidents occurred in Ajmer and other places.
