                - After dealing with Bairam Khan’s rebellion, Akbar sent another expedition to Malwa.
                - Baz Bahadur fled, seeking refuge first with the Rana of Mewar and later wandering across various regions.
                - Eventually, he submitted to Akbar and was enrolled as a Mughal mansabdar.
                - Malwa was firmly incorporated into the Mughal Empire.
                - Conquest of Garh-Katanga (1564-65)
                - Around the same time, Mughal forces invaded Garh-Katanga, a Gond kingdom covering the Narmada valley and northern Madhya Pradesh.
                - The kingdom had been strengthened by Aman Das (Sangram Shah), who had allied with Bahadur Shah of Gujarat.
                - It was the most powerful Gond kingdom, with claims of 20,000 cavalry, a large infantry, and 1,000 elephants—though the accuracy of these figures is uncertain.
            - Rani Durgavati’s Rule and Resistance
                - Sangram Shah’s son married a Chandel princess, Durgavati, who became regent after her husband’s death.
                - She was a skilled warrior and hunter, known for her courage and successful battles against Baz Bahadur of Malwa.
                - Mughal governor Asaf Khan, tempted by tales of her wealth and beauty, launched an invasion from Bundelkhand with 10,000 cavalry.
                - Some local rulers rebelled against the Gond kingdom, leaving Rani Durgavati with a weakened force.
                - She fought bravely but, after being wounded in battle and facing capture, stabbed herself to death rather than surrender.
            - Plunder of Garh-Katanga
                - Asaf Khan stormed the capital, Chauragarh (near modern Jabalpur).
                - The Mughal forces looted enormous wealth, described by Abul Fazl as “beyond calculation.”
                - Asaf Khan kept most of the loot, sending only 200 elephants to Akbar’s court.
                - Kamaladevi, the rani’s younger sister, was sent to Akbar’s court.
            - Akbar’s Intervention
                - After defeating the Uzbek nobles, Akbar forced Asaf Khan to return his illegal gains.
                - He restored Garh-Katanga to Chandra Shah (Sangram Shah’s younger son) but retained ten strategic forts to strengthen Mughal control over Malwa.
            - By 1576, Akbar had firmly secured Malwa and Garh-Katanga, further consolidating the Mughal Empire’s territorial expansion.
                
    - Expansion into Rajasthan, Gujarat, and Bengal
        - Conquest of Rajasthan (1568-1570)
            - Akbar spent the next ten years bringing most of Rajasthan, Gujarat, and Bengal under his control.
            - A key step in his Rajput campaign was the siege of Chittor, a strategic fortress commanding the shortest route from Agra to Gujarat and a symbol of Rajput resistance.
            - Akbar knew that conquering Chittor was essential to securing Rajput submission.
            - Siege of Chittor (1568)
                - Rana Udai Singh withdrew to the hills, leaving Jaimal and Patta to defend the fort.
                - Many local peasants also took refuge inside and actively participated in the defense.
                - The siege lasted six months, and when the Mughals stormed the fort, a massacre of 30,000 people followed—the only instance of such large-scale carnage by Akbar.
                - Jaimal and Patta fought bravely and perished. To honor their valor, Akbar ordered statues of them on elephants to be placed at the chief gate of Agra Fort.
            - Further Expansion in Rajasthan
                - After Chittor, Akbar conquered Ranthambhor, considered the strongest fortress in Rajasthan.
                - Jodhpur had already been subdued, and soon after, the rulers of Bikaner and Jaisalmer accepted Mughal suzerainty.
                - However, Mewar continued to resist.
        - Conquest of Gujarat (1572-1573)
            - Gujarat had been in disorder since the death of Bahadur Shah.
            - Its fertile lands, thriving crafts, and crucial trade links made it an attractive conquest.
            - Akbar also had a historical claim, as Humayun had ruled over Gujarat for some time.
            - Additionally, the Mirzas, who had rebelled near Delhi, had taken refuge in Gujarat, making it a potential rival power center.
            - Initial Mughal Advance (1572)
                - In 1572, Akbar marched towards Ahmedabad via Ajmer, and the city surrendered without a fight.
                - He then moved against the Mirzas, who controlled Broach, Baroda, and Surat.
                - At Cambay, Akbar saw the sea for the first time and even took a boat ride.
                - The Portuguese, who dominated the Indian Ocean, met Akbar for the first time, but his conquest of Gujarat thwarted their expansionist ambitions.
            - Battle Against the Mirzas and Gujarat’s Rebellion (1573)
                - While besieging Surat, Akbar personally led a daring assault with only 200 men, including Man Singh and Bhagwant Das of Amber.
                - Despite being heavily outnumbered, the Mughals routed the Mirzas.
                - However, as soon as Akbar left, revolts broke out across Gujarat.
                - On hearing the news, Akbar rushed back from Agra, covering the vast distance in just nine days, an unprecedented feat.
                - With only 3,000 soldiers, he defeated a rebel force of 20,000 near Ahmedabad in 1573, securing Gujarat for the Mughals.
        - Conquest of Bengal (1574-1576)
                - Bengal and Bihar remained under Afghan rule, with Daud Khan as their ruler.
                - The Afghans had also overrun Orissa and killed its ruler, but to avoid provoking the Mughals, Daud continued reading the khutbah in Akbar’s name.
                - Internal feuds and Daud Khan’s declaration of independence gave Akbar an excuse for intervention.
            - Mughal Advance into Bengal (1574-1575)
                - Akbar led an expedition with a strong flotilla of boats, recognizing that the Afghan ruler possessed a large army (40,000 cavalry, 1,50,000 infantry, thousands of cannons and elephants, and a powerful navy).
                - If the Afghans had a strong leader, history might have repeated the contest between Humayun and Sher Shah.
                - Akbar first captured Patna, securing Mughal communication in Bihar.
                - He then returned to Agra, leaving Khan-i-Khanan Munaim Khan in charge of the Bengal campaign.
                - The Mughals invaded Bengal, forcing Daud Khan to sue for peace.
        - Final Battle and End of Afghan Rule (1576)
            - Daud Khan rebelled soon after, but the Mughal armies were now better organized and led.
            - In a decisive battle in Bihar (1576), Daud Khan was defeated and executed on the spot.
            - With this victory, the last Afghan kingdom in northern India ended.
        - Conclusion: The First Phase of Akbar’s Expansion
            - With the conquest of Rajasthan, Gujarat, and Bengal, Akbar successfully extended Mughal rule across northern and western India.
            - This phase consolidated Mughal power and removed major rival centers of resistance.
            - Mewar was the only Rajput state still holding out, setting the stage for further campaigns.

    - Administration Under Akbar
        - Reforming the Land Revenue System
            - After the conquest of Gujarat, Akbar turned his attention to administrative reforms, particularly land revenue. 
            - The system established by Sher Shah had deteriorated after Islam Shah’s death, requiring Akbar to reorganize it from the ground up.
            - Early Revenue Methods and Their Challenges
                - Sher Shah’s system involved measuring cultivated land and setting fixed dues for each crop based on productivity. However, the annual updates to the central price schedule often delayed assessments, creating hardships for peasants.
                - Initially, Akbar relied on annual assessments, using reports from hereditary landholders (gantngos) about local cultivation and prices. However, corruption among these officials led to inaccuracies.
                - To address these issues, Akbar appointed officials called Agroris across northern India to verify revenue collections and reports.
        - Introduction of the Dahsala System (1580)
            - To standardize assessments, Akbar introduced the dahsala system, which:
                - Calculated the average produce of different crops over the past ten years.
                - Determined the state’s share as one-third of this average.
                - Converted the revenue share into cash, based on past average prices.
            - Further refinements included:
                - Grouping regions with similar productivity into assessment circles to adjust rates based on local prices.
                - Granting remissions to peasants when crops failed due to droughts or floods.
        - The Zaéti System and Its Implementation
            - The zaéti system involved land measurement using bamboos linked with iron rings to calculate dues precisely.
            - This system was implemented from Lahore to Allahabad, as well as in Malwa and Gujarat.
            - The dahsala system was an advancement of the zaéti system.
        - Alternative Revenue Systems
            - Apart from the dahsala, several other revenue methods were used:
                - Batai or Ghalla-Bakhsht
                    - Crop division between peasants and the state in a fixed proportion.
                    - Required officials to be present at harvest time, making it fair but labor-intensive.
                - Zadti and Atnat Systems
                    - Peasants were sometimes allowed to choose between paying in cash (zadti) or kind (atnat).
                    - For certain cash crops (cotton, indigo, sugarcane), revenue was always collected in cash.
                - Sasaq System
                    - A rough estimate of dues based on past payments.
                    - Some historians believe it was merely a method of computing past dues, while others argue it was an independent crop assessment method.
        - Land Classification and Taxation
            - Land was classified based on cultivation frequency:
                - Polaj – Regularly cultivated land.
                - Parauti – Occasionally left fallow, taxed fully when cultivated.
                - Chachar – Left fallow for 2–3 years, taxed at lower rates initially.
                - Banjar – Left fallow for long periods, gradually taxed over time.
            - Fields were also categorized as good, middling, or bad, with revenue adjusted accordingly.
        - Akbar’s Approach to Agricultural Development
            - Akbar instructed revenue officers (amil) to act as protectors of the peasantry.
            - They were to advance loans (saccavi) for seeds, tools, and animals, ensuring easy repayment.
            - Peasants had hereditary cultivation rights and could not be forcibly evicted if they paid revenue.
            - Zamindars, who had hereditary rights over land revenue, were urged to support cultivation efforts.
        - Legacy of Akbar’s Revenue Reforms
            - The dahsala system was not a permanent or ten-year settlement but remained flexible.
            - It formed the foundation of the Mughal revenue system until the late seventeenth century.
            - The zaéti system is often associated with Raja Todar Mal, though it was a collective effort of Akbar’s revenue officers.
            - Todar Mal’s Bandobast is sometimes used to refer to this system.
        - Akbar’s revenue policies ensured stable agricultural growth, fair taxation, and increased state revenue, forming a cornerstone of Mughal administration.

    - Mansabdari System and the Army
        - Introduction to the Mansabdari System
            - Importance of a strong army for expansion and governance
            - Role of the Mansabdari system in organizing the nobility and army
        - Structure of the Mansabdari System
            - Rank (Mansab) Assignments
                - Ranks ranged from 10 to 5000 for nobles
                - Princes received higher mansabs
                - Highest noble rank increased to 7000 by the end of Akbar’s reign
            - Evolution of the Mansabdari System
                - Initially, a single-rank system
                - Division into zat (personal status) and sawar (number of cavalrymen) from 1594-95
                - Three categories based on sawar maintenance
                - Importance of cavalry in the military hierarchy
        - Recruitment and Maintenance of the Army
            - Dagh System and Soldier Inspection
                - Descriptive rolls (chehra) for soldiers
                - Branding of horses for quality control
                - Periodic inspections by imperial officers
            - Cavalry Standards and Organization
                - High-quality horses (Arabic and Iraqi breeds)
                - 10-20 rule: each cavalryman to maintain two horses
                - Categorization of sawars based on horse availability
            -  Composition of the Military Contingents
                - Mixed recruitment policy (Mughals, Pathans, Hindustanis, Rajputs)
                - Special privilege for Mughal and Rajput nobles to maintain exclusive contingents
                - Other military personnel: bowmen, musketeers (bandukchi), sappers, and miners
        - Salaries and Payment System
            - Pay Structure of Soldiers and Officers
                - Sawar salary: Rs 20 per month
                - Infantry salary: Rs 3 per month
                - Higher pay for Iranis and Turanis compared to Rajputs and Hindustanis
            - Payment Methods
                - Salaries included in the mansabdar’s pay
                - Jagir system for payments (some paid in cash)
                - No hereditary rights granted through jagirs
        - Historical Origins of the Mansabdari System
            - Mongol Influence on the Mansabdari System
                - Chinggis Khan’s decimal army structure (units of 10 to 10,000)
                - Similar ranks under the Delhi Sultanate and Sur rulers
            - Classification of Mansabdars
                - Below 500: Mansabdars
                - 500 to below 2500: Amirs
                - 2500 and above: Amir-i-umda or Amir-i-azam
                - Hierarchical command structure and delegation of authority
        - Promotion and Career Progression
            - Career Mobility in the Mansabdari System
                - Entry at lower ranks with promotion based on merit
                - Demotion as a form of punishment
                - Combined civil and military service structure
            - Responsibilities of Mansabdars
                - Maintenance of horses, elephants, camels, mules, and carts
                - Prescribed quotas based on rank
                - Quality classification of horses and elephants
        - Salaries and Perks of Mansabdars
            - High Salaries of Mansabdars
                - 5000 zat rank: Rs 30,000 per month
                - 3000 zat rank: Rs 17,000 per month
                - 1000 zat rank: Rs 8,200 per month
                - Smallest mansabdars (100 zat) received Rs 7,000 per year
            - Additional Benefits
                - Personal expenses covered within salaries
                - Gentleman troopers (zhadis) and their privileges
                - Special muster-master for noble troopers
        - Akbar’s Personal Military Interests
            - Cavalry and Bodyguards
                - Akbar’s large cavalry regiment
                - Maintenance of a personal horse stable
            - Artillery and Warfare Innovations
                - Special focus on guns and artillery
                - Development of mobile detachable guns
                - Siege guns requiring 100-200 oxen and elephants
        - The Mughal Navy: A Missed Opportunity
            - Lack of a Strong Navy
                - Naval weakness as a persistent issue
                - Construction of warboats for eastern campaigns
                - Warboats over 30 meters long with 350-ton displacement

    - Organization of Government under Akbar
        - Local Administration
            - Akbar made hardly any changes in the local government structure.
            - The pargana and sarkar systems continued as before.
            - Chief officers of the sarkar:
                - Faujdar – In charge of law and order.
                - Amalguzar – Responsible for land revenue assessment and collection.
            - Division of Empire’s Territories:
                - Jagir – Allotted to nobles and members of the royal family, including queens.
                - Khalisa – Income from these villages went directly to the royal exchequer.
                - Inam – Lands granted to learned and religious men.
            - The Amalguzar was responsible for supervising all types of land holdings to ensure uniform tax collection.
            - Autonomous rajas were allowed to follow their traditional revenue systems, but Akbar encouraged them to adopt the imperial system.
        - Central Administration
            - Akbar’s central administration was based on the Delhi Sultanate’s structure, but he reorganized its functions with strict rules and regulations.
            - A new shape was given to governance, ensuring efficiency and discipline.
            - Role of the Wazir
                - Traditionally, the wazir was all-powerful, managing all departments.
                - Over time, the military and judiciary became independent from the wazir’s control.
                - Under Akbar:
                - The post of wazir was retained but stripped of major powers.
                - The wazir became a revenue expert, not the ruler’s chief advisor.
                - Title changed to: Diwan or Diwan-i-Ala instead of Wazir.
                - Duties of the Diwan:
                    - Responsible for all income and expenditure.
                    - Managed khalisa, jagir, and inam lands.
                    - Sometimes, multiple diwans shared responsibilities.
        - Military Administration
            - The Mir Bakhshi
                - Head of the military department.
                - Role and Responsibilities:
                - Considered head of the nobility.
                - Only leading grandees were appointed to this post.
                - Recommended appointments to mansabs and promotions to the emperor.
                - Once approved, the diwan confirmed and assigned a jagir to the appointee.
                - Intelligence and Information:
                - Mir Bakhshi also controlled intelligence agencies.
                - Officials under him:
                - Arids – Intelligence officers.
                - Waqia-navis – News reporters.
                - Their reports were submitted directly to the emperor.
            - Checks and Balances
                - The Diwan and Mir Bakhshi were nearly equal in power, ensuring balance in administration.
        - Imperial Household and Court Affairs
            - The Nur Saiman
                - In charge of the imperial household.
                - Responsibilities:
                - Managed provisions and supplies for the emperor’s female apartments (Aaram).
                - Supervised royal workshops (arkadanas), where many palace goods were produced.
                - Controlled court etiquette and the royal bodyguard.
                - Only nobles who had the emperor’s complete trust were appointed to this office.
        - Judicial Administration
            - Chief Officer: Chief Qazi (Gazi)
            - Sometimes, this post was combined with the Chief Sadr, who managed charitable and religious endowments.
            - The Chief Qazi had significant power and influence.
            - The office declined in reputation due to corruption, especially under Abdun Nabi.
        - Land Revenue System
            - Akbar scrutinized land grants and separated crown lands from jagir and khalisa lands.
            - The empire was divided into six circles for managing inam lands.
            - Notable features of inam grants:
                - Granted to all, regardless of religion.
                    - Many Hindu priests (marks) received grants, as seen in preserved sanads (documents).
                - Half of the inam land had to be cultivable wasteland to encourage expansion of agriculture.
        - Akbar’s Daily Schedule and Public Interaction
            - Akbar ensured direct accessibility to both common people and ministers.
            - Daily Routine:
                1.	Morning Audience (Jharoka Darshan) – People gathered to see the emperor and present petitions.
                2.	Public Audience (Diwan-i-Am) – Heard petitions and issued immediate orders.
                3.	Midday Break – Rest and meals.
                4.	Meetings with Ministers – Held in a private chamber (Ghusal Khana).
                5.	Confidential Consultations – Ministers met him privately for discussions.
        - Provincial Administration
            - In 1580, Akbar divided the empire into 12 Subas (provinces):
                - Bengal, Bihar, Allahabad, Awadh, Agra, Delhi, Lahore, Multan, Kabul, Ajmer, Malwa, Gujarat.
            - Each Suba (province) had:
                - Subedar (Governor) – Administrative head.
                - Diwan – Managed revenue.
                - Bakhshi – Supervised military affairs.
                - Sadr – Handled charity and religious grants.
                - Gazi – Administered justice.
                - Waqia-navis – Reported on events.
            - This system of checks and balances ensured efficient and fair governance across the empire.

    - Relations with the Rajputs
        - Mughal Policy Towards Rajputs
            - Akbar’s relations with the Rajputs should be understood in the broader context of Mughal policy towards powerful rajas and zamindars.
            - Humayun had already initiated a policy of winning over these elements.
            - Abul Fazl mentions that Humayun entered into matrimonial relations with zamindars to soothe their minds.
            - Akbar expanded and refined this policy over time.
        - Matrimonial Alliances with Rajputs
            - Amber (Jaipur):
                - Bhara Mal, ruler of Amber, visited Akbar’s court shortly after his accession.
                - In 1562, when Akbar was traveling to Ajmer, he learned that Bhara Mal was being harassed by a Mughal governor.
                - Bhara Mal personally paid homage to Akbar and cemented the alliance by marrying his daughter, Harkha Bai, to Akbar.
            - Earlier Precedents of Rajput-Muslim Marriages:
                - Rajput princesses had previously married Muslim rulers, e.g., Maldeo of Jodhpur married his daughters to Sultan Mahmud of Gujarat and Islam Shah Sur.
                - However, these marriages did not ensure stable relations.
                - Akbar’s policy differed—he gave religious freedom to his Hindu wives and honored their families in Mughal nobility.
                - Bhara Mal was made a grandee, his son Bhagwant Das rose to the rank of 5000, and grandson Man Singh to 7000, an honor given only to Aziz Khan Koka, Akbar’s foster brother.
            - Honors and Trust Extended to the Kachhawahas:
                - Akbar entrusted the infant prince Danyal to Bhara Mal’s family for upbringing.
                - Bhara Mal was given charge of Agra when Akbar moved to Gujarat in 1572.
        - Relations with Other Rajput States
            - Non-Matrimonial Alliances:
                - Akbar did not make matrimonial alliances a precondition for favor.
                - Hadas of Ranthambhor, Sirohi, and Banswara were granted favor without marriage ties.
                - Rao Surjan Hada was made in charge of Garh-Katanga and ranked at 2000.
            - Religious Tolerance and Rajput Loyalty:
                - In 1564, Akbar abolished the jizya tax, often seen as a symbol of Muslim domination.
                - He had already abolished the pilgrim tax and forcible conversion of prisoners of war.
                - After the conquest of Chittor, most Rajput rulers accepted Mughal suzerainty.
                - The rulers of Jaisalmer and Bikaner also entered into matrimonial alliances.
        - Conflict with Mewar and Rana Pratap
            - Refusal to Submit:
                - Mewar alone refused to accept Mughal suzerainty.
                - While Chittor and surrounding plains fell to the Mughals, Udaipur and the hilly region remained under Rana Udai Singh’s control.
            - Diplomatic Efforts:
                - In 1572, Rana Pratap ascended the throne.
                - Akbar sent several embassies (led by Man Singh, Bhagwant Das, and Raja Todar Mal) to persuade him to submit.
                - Rana Pratap briefly considered a compromise but refused to personally pay homage to Akbar.
        - Battle of Haldighati (1576)
            - Akbar’s Military Action:
                - Akbar moved to Ajmer in early 1576.
                - He sent Raja Man Singh with 5000 troops against Rana Pratap.
            - War Strategy:
                - Rana Pratap devastated lands near Chittor to deny supplies to the Mughals.
                - He fortified passes leading to Kumbhalgarh.
                - His army included Rajputs, Afghans (led by Hakim Khan Sur), and Bhils.
            - Outcome of the Battle:
                - Initial Rajput success, but Mughal reinforcements turned the tide.
                - Rana Pratap escaped; Mughal troops occupied Gogunda.
                - After Haldighati, Rana Pratap resorted to guerrilla warfare.
        - Aftermath of Mewar Conflict
            - Continued Resistance:
                - Mughal pressure continued, and Akbar isolated Mewar by securing treaties with neighboring states.
                - Rana Pratap lost Kumbhalgarh and Udaipur but relied on Bhil support.
            - Change in Mughal Priorities:
                - After 1579, Mughal attention shifted due to rebellions in Bihar and Bengal.
                - Akbar moved to Lahore (1585-1597) to address the northwest crisis.
            - Rajput Revival:
                - Rana Pratap recaptured many territories, including Kumbhalgarh, but never Chittor.
                - He established a new capital at Chavand near Dungarpur.
                - Died in 1597 at age 51 due to an internal injury.
        - Conflict in Marwar
            - Succession Dispute:
                - After Maldeo’s death (1562), his sons fought over Marwar’s throne.
                - Chandrasen, the younger son and Maldeo’s favorite, became king.
                - Mughal pressure forced him to cede parts of his kingdom to his elder brothers.
            - Mughal Takeover:
                - Chandrasen rebelled, leading Akbar to bring Marwar under direct Mughal rule.
                - Jodhpur was placed under Rai Singh Bikaneri.
            - Chandrasen’s Resistance:
                - He resorted to guerrilla warfare but was forced to flee to Mewar.
                - Died in 1581.
            - Final Settlement:
                - In 1583, Akbar gave Jodhpur to Udai Singh, Chandrasen’s elder brother.
                - Udai Singh’s daughter Jagat Gosain (Jodha Bai) married Salim (Jahangir).
        - Continued Rajput-Mughal Relations Under Jahangir
            - Jahangir’s Rajput Connections:
                - His mother was a Rajput princess.
                - He married princesses from Amber, Jodhpur, Jaisalmer, and Bikaner.
                - Rajput rulers received high honors in his court.
        - Settlement with Mewar (1613)
            - Struggles of Amar Singh:
                - After Rana Pratap’s death, his son Amar Singh continued resistance.
                - Akbar and Jahangir sent multiple expeditions, including two led by Jahangir himself.
            - Final Agreement:
                - In 1613, Jahangir personally reached Ajmer to direct the campaign.
                - Prince Khurram (Shah Jahan) led a massive invasion into Mewar.
                - Pressure from Mughal forces and internal struggles forced Amar Singh to seek peace.
                - He sent his son, Karan Singh, to Jahangir’s court.
            - Terms of Peace:
                - Jahangir graciously received Karan Singh.
                - Mewar’s territories, including Chittor, were restored.
                - The fortifications of Chittor could not be rebuilt to ensure Mughal control.
                - Karan Singh was ranked at 5000 and required to provide 1500 cavalry for Mughal service.
        - Significance of Akbar’s Rajput Policy
            - Mutual Benefits:
                - Rajputs provided military strength to the Mughal empire.
                - Peace in Rajasthan allowed Rajputs to serve in distant Mughal territories.
                - Rajput rulers were granted jagirs and appointed to key Mughal positions.
            - Expansion of the Empire:
                - Rajput loyalty became a pillar of Mughal consolidation.
                - High-ranking Rajputs like Man Singh and Bhagwant Das served as governors.
            - Lasting Impact:
                - Akbar’s policy of integrating Rajputs into the Mughal empire continued under Jahangir and Shah Jahan.
                - It ensured long-term stability in the empire’s western frontiers.

    - REBELLIONS AND FURTHER EXPANSION OF THE MUGHAL EMPIRE
        - Causes of Rebellions (1580-1581)
            - Akbar’s new administrative system tightened control over nobles.
            - Sentiments of regional independence remained strong, especially in:
                - Gujarat
                - Bengal
                - Bihar
                - Rajasthan (where Rana Pratap’s struggle continued)
            - Resistance was intensified by:
                - Strict enforcement of the dagh system (branding of jagirdars’ horses).
                - Resumption of large revenue-free grants held by religious figures.
                - Religious opposition to Akbar’s liberal policies.
                - Afghan discontent in eastern India.
                - Mirza Hakim (Akbar’s half-brother, ruler of Kabul) supporting the rebellion.
        - Rebellion in Bengal and Bihar (1580-1581)
            - Outbreak & Expansion:
                - Bengal & Bihar fell to rebels, extending to Jaunpur.
                - Rebels proclaimed Mirza Hakim as ruler and sought religious backing.
                - Fatwa issued calling on people to oppose Akbar.
            - Akbar’s Response:
                - Dispatched Todar Mal to suppress rebellion in Bihar and Bengal.
                - Sent Raja Man Singh to guard against Mirza Hakim’s expected invasion.
                - Todar Mal swiftly regained control before Mirza Hakim could act.
        - Mirza Hakim’s Invasion and Defeat (1581)
            - Attempt on Lahore:
                - Mirza Hakim attacked Lahore with 15,000 cavalry.
                - Raja Man Singh & Bhagwant Das successfully defended Lahore.
            - Akbar’s Counterattack:
                - Akbar marched with 50,000 disciplined troops.
                - Mirza Hakim was forced to retreat.
            - Occupation of Kabul (1581)
                - Akbar pursued Mirza Hakim and occupied Kabul.
                - Kabul handed over to Akbar’s sister, showing his broad-mindedness.
        - Threat from the Uzbeks and Northwestern Expansion (1584-1598)
            - Uzbek Threat:
                - Abdullah Khan Uzbek overran Badakhshan (1584).
                - Mirza Hakim’s death (due to alcoholism) left Kabul in disorder.
            - Akbar’s Response:
                - Sent Raja Man Singh to Kabul; moved personally to Attock on the Indus.
                - Blocked Uzbek entry into India by expanding into:
                    - Kashmir (1586) (including Ladakh & Baltistan).
                    - Baluchistan.
                    - Khyber Pass (suppressed rebellious Afghan tribes).
                - Loss of Raja Birbal in tribal expeditions.
            - Consolidation of Northwest:
            - Sindh conquered (1590) → Opened trade routes down Indus for Punjab.
            - Akbar stayed in Lahore until 1598, ensuring security after Abdullah Uzbek’s death.
        - Further Expansion: Eastern & Western India, Deccan
            - Eastern Expansion:
                - Orissa (under Afghan chiefs) conquered by Raja Man Singh.
                - Cooch-Bihar and East Bengal (Dacca) also annexed.
            - Western Expansion:
                - Kathiawar conquered by Mirza Aziz Koka (Akbar’s foster-brother).
            - Deccan Expansion:
                - Khan-i-Khanan Munim Khan and Prince Murad sent to Deccan.
                - Mughal control extended to Ahmadnagar, leading to first direct contact with the Marathas.
        - Conclusion: The Achievements of Akbar’s Expansion
            - By 1600, the Mughal Empire had:
                - Unified North India politically.
                - Established Mughal presence in Deccan.
                - Strengthened cultural and emotional integration within the empire.

    - Towards Integration: State, Religion, and Social Reforms
        - Religious Tolerance and Social Harmony Before Akbar
            - During the 15th century, several rulers promoted Hindu-Muslim unity:
                - Translating Sanskrit secular and religious texts into Persian.
                - Patronizing local languages and literature.
                - Adopting liberal religious tolerance policies.
                - Appointing Hindus to important positions in the court and military.
            - Popular saints like Kabir, Chaitanya, and Nanak emphasized:
                - The essential unity of Islam and Hinduism.
                - A religion based on love and devotion, rather than rituals or strict interpretations.
                - Creating an atmosphere of liberal religious thought and discouraging religious narrow-mindedness.
            - Akbar was born and raised in this environment of religious tolerance.
        - Akbar’s Early Reforms for Religious Equality
            - Abolished the jizyah (poll tax) on non-Muslims, removing religious discrimination.
            - Removed the pilgrim tax on bathing at Hindu holy sites like Prayag and Banaras.
            - Ended forced religious conversions of war prisoners.
            - Appointed capable Hindus to the nobility:
                - Rajput rulers joined Akbar’s court through alliances.
                - Todar Mal became Diwan, excelling in revenue administration.
                - Birbal was Akbar’s close companion.
                - Other key Hindu officials included Rai Paw Das (Rai Bikramajit).
        - Akbar’s Concept of Sovereignty and Religious Harmony
            - Akbar’s philosophy of rulership, as recorded by Abul Fazl, combined Timurid, Persian, and Indian ideas:
                - The ruler’s authority was divinely ordained (fara-i-‘izadi).
                - The king was a paternal figure, treating all subjects equally, irrespective of religion.
                - His duty was to maintain social balance and prevent sectarian conflicts.
                - This policy was known as sulh-i-kul (peace for all).
        - Akbar’s Religious Exploration and the Ibadat Khana (Hall of Prayer)
            - Initially, Akbar was an orthodox Muslim, deeply influenced by the state’s chief qazi, Abdun Nabi Khan.
            - However, he gradually turned away from narrow orthodoxy due to:
                - Exposure to mysticism and spiritual practices.
                - His personal contemplation and prayers.
                - Influence of scholars like Abul Fazl and Faizi, who were persecuted by orthodox mullahs.
            - In 1575, Akbar built the Ibadat Khana in Fatehpur Sikri:
                - Initially, only Muslim theologians participated, but discussions became disorderly.
                - Mullahs argued and insulted each other, leading to Akbar’s disillusionment.
                - He opened the Ibadat Khana to all religions—Christians, Zoroastrians, Hindus, Jains, and even atheists.
                - Discussions questioned even core Islamic doctrines (e.g., the Quran’s finality), shocking orthodox scholars.
                - Rumors spread that Akbar was forsaking Islam.
        - Akbar’s Conflict with Orthodox Ulema and the Mahzar Declaration
            - Akbar investigated the Chief Sadr, Abdun Nabi, and found him guilty of corruption and bigotry:
                - He was dismissed and exiled to Mecca.
                - Provincial sadrs were appointed for fairer distribution of charitable lands.
            - In 1580, a rebellion broke out, supported by orthodox ulema, who issued fatwas declaring Akbar a heretic.
                - Akbar crushed the rebellion and punished the qazi responsible.
            - To strengthen his authority, Akbar issued the Mahzar Declaration:
                - Stated that Akbar had the right to choose among different interpretations of Islamic law.
                - If Akbar’s decisions were in line with the Quran and for the benefit of the empire, they were binding.
                - Signed by leading wazirs, but later wrongly called the “Decree of Infallibility.”
        - Akbar’s Search for Religious Truth and the Tawhid-i-Ilahi
            - Akbar continued engaging with scholars of various religions:
                - Invited Hindu, Jain, and Zoroastrian scholars to discuss their faiths.
                - Sent a mission to Goa to bring Portuguese Christian priests to court.
                - Interacted with the Jain saint Hira Vijaya Suri.
            - Realized that all religions had common truths and that sectarian disputes were unnecessary.
            - Tawhid-i-Ilahi (Divine Monotheism):
                - Not a new religion, but an elite spiritual order based on Sufi principles.
                - Members took an oath of loyalty and recited “Allah-o-Akbar” as a mantra.
                - No religious texts, priesthood, places of worship, or rituals.
                - Only 18 nobles joined, while thousands enrolled as disciples (murids).
            - Orthodox critics misunderstood Tawhid-i-Ilahi, accusing Akbar of trying to create a new religion.
            - Akbar sought political unity through this order, tying nobles personally to his rule.
        - Social Reforms and Promotion of Knowledge
            - Major social reforms:
                - Discouraged sati (widow burning) unless voluntary.
                - Banned child widows from committing sati.
                - Legalized widow remarriage.
                - Discouraged polygamy, allowing multiple wives only if the first wife was barren.
                - Raised marriage age to 14 for girls and 16 for boys.
                - Restricted alcohol sales.
            - Educational and cultural advancements:
                - Revised the education system, including subjects like:
                    - Moral education, mathematics, agriculture, astronomy, logic, and history.
            - Established a major translation department:
                - Translated Mahabharata, Ramayana, Atharva Veda, Singhasan Battisi, Panchatantra, and the Bible into Persian.
                - The Quran was also translated into Persian for the first time.
            - Promoted arts and literature:
                - Gathered renowned scholars, poets, musicians, and artists—the Navaratnas (Nine Jewels).
        - Legacy of Akbar’s Religious Policies
            - Under Akbar, the Mughal state became secular, liberal, and socially progressive.
            - His principles of sulh-i-kul (universal peace and tolerance) influenced later Mughal rulers.
            - His Tawhid-i-Ilahi order declined after his death, but:
                - His policies of religious harmony remained influential.
                - Mughal nobles remained personally loyal to the emperor, even after Akbar.
            - Belief in the king’s divine powers continued—even orthodox rulers like Aurangzeb did not challenge it.
        - Conclusion
            - Akbar’s efforts towards religious integration, administrative reforms, and social progress marked a turning point in Indian history. 
            - Though his spiritual pursuits were misunderstood, his secular governance, tolerance, and patronage of knowledge helped shape the Mughal Empire into a more inclusive and enlightened state.

    - The Deccan and South India (Up to 1656)
        - Emergence of Powerful Deccani States
            - Breakup of the Bahmani Kingdom
                - Led to the rise of three powerful states: Ahmadnagar, Bijapur, and Golconda.
                - These states united to defeat Vijayanagara at the Battle of Bannihati (Talikota) in 1565.
            - Territorial Conflicts
                - Ahmadnagar and Bijapur fought over Shelapur, a rich and fertile tract.
                - Both states sought to conquer Bidar.
                - Ahmadnagar aimed to annex Berar, claiming superiority as the descendants of the Bahmani rulers.
                - Their claims were opposed by:
                    - Bijapur, which contested Ahmadnagar’s dominance.
                    - Gujarat rulers, who wanted control over Konkan and Berar.
                    - Bijapur and Golconda, who clashed over Naldurg.
        - Impact of Mughal Expansion
            - Mughal conquest of Gujarat (1572) changed Deccan politics
                - Akbar, though capable, avoided Deccan affairs initially.
                - Ahmadnagar took advantage and annexed Berar.
                - Ahmadnagar and Bijapur made a pact:
                    - Bijapur expanded southward, attacking Vijayanagara.
                    - Ahmadnagar consolidated control over Berar.
                - Golconda also expanded into Vijayanagara, which had fragmented into small nayakhoods.
        - Rise of the Marathas in Deccan Politics
            - Traditional Role of the Marathas
                - Served as auxiliaries or jagirdars (sardars) in the Bahmani kingdom.
                - Deccani Brahmins managed revenue affairs.
                - Some powerful Maratha families:
                    - More, Nimbalkar, Ghatge, etc.
                    - These were zamindars (deshmukhs) but not independent rulers like the Rajputs.
            - Nature of Maratha Chiefs
                - Lacked strong clan-based backing.
                - Many were military adventurers, shifting loyalties based on circumstances.
                - However, they formed the backbone of the Deccan’s landed aristocracy, similar to the Rajputs in North India.
        - Deccan Rulers’ Policy Toward the Marathas
            - Mid-16th Century Strategy: Winning Maratha Support
                - All three major Deccan states (Ahmadnagar, Bijapur, Golconda) recruited Maratha chiefs.
                - Ibrahim Adil Shah of Bijapur (r. 1535–1557):
                    - Patronized 30,000 Maratha auxiliaries (sardars).
                    - Introduced Marathi in revenue accounts.
                    - Promoted new families like the Bhonsales (originally Ghorpades), Dafles (Chavans), etc..
                - Maharashtrian Brahmins played key roles in diplomacy.
                - The title of Peshwa was granted to a Maratha chief, influencing Ahmadnagar and Golconda.
        - Comparison with Mughal Policies
            - The Deccani rulers allied with local landed classes even before the Mughals adopted a similar strategy under Akbar.

    - Mughal Advance Towards the Deccan
        - Strategic Importance of the Deccan
            - Logical Next Step for the Mughals
                - After consolidating power in northern India, the Mughals naturally turned their attention southward.
                - Though divided by the Vindhya mountains, the north and south had always been linked through:
                    - Trade and commerce (travelers and merchandise).
                    - Religious exchanges (Sufi saints, pilgrims).
                    - Political interactions (involvement of Gujarat, Malwa, and Orissa in southern affairs).
            - Historical Precedents
                - The Tughlaq conquest of the Deccan had strengthened commercial and cultural ties.
                - After the decline of the Delhi Sultanate, many Sufi saints and job seekers migrated to the Bahmani court.
                - By the 1560s and early 1570s, the Mughals had already conquered Malwa and Gujarat, making further Deccan involvement inevitable.
            - Early Mughal Moves
                - 1576: Akbar sent a Mughal army to Khandesh, forcing its ruler to submit.
                - However, Akbar was preoccupied with the northwest frontier (1586–1598) and could not focus on the Deccan.
                - Meanwhile, political instability in the Deccan worsened.
        - Political Turmoil in the Deccan
            - Frequent Wars and Instability
                - Constant warfare among Deccani states.
                - Factional struggles erupted upon the death of rulers, with nobles competing for power.
                - Major factions:
                    - Deccanis vs. newcomers (sardars/gharibs).
                    - Internal rivalries among Habshis (Abyssinians/Africans) and Afghans.
            - Lack of Local Loyalty
                - These factions had little connection to the people and culture of the Deccan.
                - Marathas were slowly assimilating into the political system but had not yet gained much influence.
                - As a result, rulers and nobles commanded little loyalty from the population.
        - Sectarian Conflicts and Religious Strife
            - Rise of Shiism in the Deccan
                - Safavid Iran adopted Shiism as the state religion, leading to:
                    - Persecution of Sunnis in Iran.
                    - Migration of many Shia nobles and scholars to Akbar’s court.
                - Golconda formally adopted Shiism, while:
                    - Bijapur and Ahmadnagar had strong but fluctuating Shia factions.
                    - These differences fueled party strife and instability in the Deccan.
            - Spread of Mahdawi Ideas
                - Belief in the Mahdi (a prophesied savior from the Prophet’s family) gained traction.
                - Saiyid Muhammad of Jaunpur (15th century) proclaimed himself the Mahdi and spread his ideas.
                - Mahdawi dairas (circles) were established across India, including the Deccan.
                - Orthodox Sunnis opposed Mahdawism and Shiism alike, deepening sectarian divisions.
            - Akbar’s Concern Over Sectarian Strife
                - Feared that these bitter rivalries would spill into the Mughal Empire.
                - Promoted Sulah-i-Kul (universal peace) to maintain religious harmony.
        - Portuguese Threat in the Deccan
            - Portuguese Interference in Pilgrimage and Trade
                - Blocked pilgrim traffic to Mecca, even targeting royal ladies.
                - Aggressively converted locals to Christianity.
                - Attempted to expand their control over the mainland.
            - Mughal Resistance
                - Tried to seize Surat, but a Mughal commander repelled them.
                - Akbar believed that coordinating the Deccani states under Mughal leadership could counter the Portuguese threat.
        - Akbar’s Decision to Intervene in the Deccan
            - Key Motivations
                - Political instability in the Deccan.
                - Religious conflicts (Shia-Sunni strife and Mahdawi movements).
                - Portuguese expansionism.
                - Strategic necessity after consolidating the north.
        - Thus, these factors pushed Akbar towards involvement in Deccan affairs, setting the stage for the Mughal-Deccan conflicts in the years ahead.

    - Conquest of Berar, Ahmadnagar, and Khandesh
        - Early Mughal Moves
            - 1591: Akbar launched a diplomatic offensive, sending embassies to Deccani states, urging them to accept Mughal suzerainty.
                - Only Khandesh complied due to its vulnerability.
                - Ahmadnagar’s ruler, Burhan Nizam Shah, rudely rejected the envoy.
                - Other states made vague promises but did not accept Mughal overlordship.
        - Political Turmoil in the Deccan
            - Factional Struggles and Internal Conflicts
                - 1595: Burhan Nizam Shah died, triggering a succession crisis in Ahmadnagar.
                    - Four claimants emerged, backed by rival factions.
                    - Bahadur, the legitimate heir, was supported by Ibrahim Adil Shah II of Bijapur.
                    - Chand Bibi, Burhan’s sister, took charge of defending Bahadur’s claim.
                    - Rival Ahmadnagar nobles (Deccanis) invited Mughal intervention.
        - First Mughal Invasion of Ahmadnagar (1595–1596)
            - Mughal army led by Prince Murad (Governor of Gujarat) and Abdur Rahim Khan-i-Khanan.
            - Faced little resistance until they reached Ahmadnagar fort, where Chand Bibi defended it valiantly.
            - After a 4-month siege, a treaty was signed in 1596:
                - Ahmadnagar ceded Berar to the Mughals.
                - Bahadur was recognized as king.
                - Mughal suzerainty was acknowledged.    
        - Renewed Deccani Resistance and Mughal Victory (1597)
            - Deccani states (Bijapur, Golconda, and Ahmadnagar) formed an alliance to resist the Mughals.
            - Battle of 1597: A combined Deccani army (three times larger than the Mughals) invaded Berar.
            - Mughal victory forced Bijapur and Golconda to withdraw, leaving Chand Bibi isolated.
        - Second Mughal Siege of Ahmadnagar (1600)
            - Chand Bibi, though inclined to honor the 1596 treaty, could not stop her nobles from harassing the Mughals in Berar.
            - The Mughals besieged Ahmadnagar again.
            - Facing no aid, Chand Bibi attempted negotiations but was murdered by a rival faction.
            - Ahmadnagar fell in 1600:
                - Bahadur Nizam Shah was imprisoned in Gwalior.
                - Balaghat was annexed, and a Mughal garrison was stationed in Ahmadnagar.
        - Mughal Expansion into Khandesh (1601)
            - Akbar moved south to assess the situation and punish Khandesh’s ruler for disrespecting Prince Daniyal.
            - Siege of Asirgarh (1601):
                - The fort, one of the strongest in the Deccan, was besieged.
                - Pestilence broke out, forcing Khandesh’s ruler to surrender.
                - Khandesh was fully incorporated into the Mughal Empire.
        - Peace Settlement with Ahmadnagar (1601)
            - Murtaza Nizam Shah II was proclaimed ruler by surviving Nizam Shahi nobles.
            - Prince Daniyal negotiated a peace agreement:
                - Ahmadnagar, Balaghat, and Telangana were ceded to the Mughals.
                - Murtaza retained part of the kingdom under the condition of loyalty.
        - Unresolved Deccan Problem
            - 1601: After capturing Asirgarh, Akbar returned north to deal with Salim’s rebellion.
            - Despite Mughal conquests, Deccan remained unstable:
                - Mughals lacked a strong local ally to consolidate their rule.
                - Frequent disputes among Mughal commanders weakened control.
                - Akbar sought an alliance with Bijapur; Ibrahim Adil Shah II offered his daughter’s hand to Prince Daniyal.
                - 1602: Daniyal died from excessive drinking, leaving the Deccan problem unresolved.
        - Legacy and Impact
            - Although Khandesh, Berar, and Balaghat were annexed, Mughal control in the Deccan remained weak.
            - Akbar’s successor, Jahangir, had to reopen the Deccan campaign to establish lasting Mughal dominance.

    - Rise of Malik Ambar and Mughal Struggles in the Deccan
        - The Fall of Ahmadnagar and the Rise of Malik Ambar
            - After Ahmadnagar fell and Bahadur Nizam Shah was captured, the state was on the brink of collapse.
            - Neighboring states, particularly Bijapur and Golconda, could have absorbed Ahmadnagar if not for the emergence of Malik Ambar.
            - Who Was Malik Ambar?
                - Origins:
                    - An Abyssinian (Ethiopian), born in Ethiopia.
                    - Sold at a slave market in Baghdad and brought to the Deccan by a merchant.
                    - Served under Changez Khan, an influential Habshi noble in Ahmadnagar.
                - Entry into Politics:
                    - Initially went to Bijapur after the Mughal invasion of Ahmadnagar.
                    - Later returned and joined the Habshi faction, which opposed Chand Bibi.
        - Formation of a Nizam Shahi Resistance (1600–1601)
            - Malik Ambar found a Nizam Shahi prince and declared him Murtaza Nizam Shah II.
            - Declared himself Peshwa (Prime Minister) of Ahmadnagar.
            - Built a powerful army using:
                - Maratha troopers (Bargis) – experts in guerilla warfare (hit-and-run tactics, cutting off supplies).
                - Bijapur’s tacit support in resisting Mughal expansion.
        - Early Mughal Challenges and Ambar’s Resistance
            - Mughal Commander: Abdur Rahim Khan-i-Khanan (a skilled general and diplomat).
            - 1601: Khan-i-Khanan crushed Ambar at Nander (Telangana).
            - Despite defeat, Ambar survived by making peace with Khan-i-Khanan, using the truce to strengthen his position.
        - Renewed Mughal Weakness and Ambar’s Counteroffensive (1605–1610)
            - After Akbar’s death (1605), internal conflicts weakened the Mughal hold on the Deccan.
            - Ambar launched a massive campaign to push the Mughals out of Berar, Balaghat, and Ahmadnagar.
            - Bijapur’s ruler, Ibrahim Adil Shah II, supported Ambar:
                - Gave Ambar the fort of Qandahar (Telangana) as a secure base.
                - Lent him 10,000 horsemen, supported by land grants.
                - Strengthened the alliance through a marriage between Ambar’s son and the daughter of a powerful Habshi noble of Bijapur (1609).
        - Mughal Setbacks and Loss of Ahmadnagar (1611–1619)
            - Khan-i-Khanan was forced to retreat to Burhanpur.
            - By 1619, Ambar had regained all Mughal conquests in the Deccan.
            - Prince Parvez was sent with a large Mughal army but failed to defeat Ambar.
            - Ahmadnagar was lost, and Parvez had to sign a humiliating treaty.
        - Mughal Counteroffensive: Jahangir’s Response (1616–1618)
            - Khan-i-Khanan returned with a new strategy:
                - Exploited Ambar’s growing arrogance, which alienated his Maratha and Habshi allies.
                - Convinced key Maratha leaders (Jagdev Rai, Babaji Kate, Udaji Ram) to defect.
            - 1616: Mughals crushed the Deccani alliance (Ahmadnagar, Bijapur, Golconda).
            - Khirki (new Nizam Shahi capital) was captured and burned.
            - Prince Khurram’s (Shah Jahan’s) Campaign (1618)
                - Jahangir sent Prince Khurram (Shah Jahan) with a massive army.
                - Ambar was forced to surrender, but the treaty did not expand Mughal territory significantly.
                - Jahangir’s policy:
                    - Avoid deep involvement in the Deccan’s instability.
                    - Strengthen ties with Bijapur rather than extend Mughal control.
                    - Called Adil Shah of Bijapur “son” in a diplomatic move.
        - Ambar’s Final Years and Continued Resistance (1620–1626)
            - 1620: Deccani forces suffered another major defeat.
            - Forced to restore Mughal territories and pay Rs. 5,00,000 indemnity.
            - The Deccan alliance weakened due to infighting.
            - Conflict with Bijapur (1622–1625)
                - Ambar attacked Bijapur to recover Sholapur, a long-disputed territory.
                - Reached Bijapur’s capital, burned Nauraspur, and forced Adil Shah into a fort.
        - Legacy of Malik Ambar
            - Brilliant military strategist but failed to secure a lasting peace.
            - His success proved the importance of the Marathas in Deccan politics.
            - Adopted Todar Mal’s revenue system in Ahmadnagar:
                - Abolished exploitative land contracts (Ijara).
                - Introduced Zabti (measured land assessment).
            - Final Years (1622–1626)
                - 1622: Recovered many lost territories while Mughals were distracted by Prince Khurram’s rebellion.
                - However, his actions led to Shah Jahan’s determination to fully annex Ahmadnagar.
                - 1626: Died at age 80, leaving his successors to face the Mughal onslaught.

    - Shah Jahan’s Deccan Campaign
        - Shah Jahan’s Early Experience in the Deccan
            - Ascended the throne in 1628.
            - Had prior experience in the Deccan from leading two expeditions.
            - Spent considerable time in the region during his rebellion against his father.
        - Attempt to Recover Lost Mughal Territories
            - Shah Jahan aimed to reclaim Berar and Balaghat from the Nizam Shahi ruler.
            - Appointed Khan-i-Jahan Lodi to lead the mission.
            - Lodi failed and was recalled to court.
            - He later rebelled, joined the Nizam Shah, and attempted to expel the Mughals.
            - The Nizam Shahi ruler continued Malik Ambar’s policy of resisting Mughal authority.
        - Diplomatic Strategy: Isolating Ahmadnagar
            - Concluded that Ahmadnagar had to be eliminated for Mughal stability in the Deccan.
            - Offered one-third of Ahmadnagar’s territory to Bijapur in exchange for cooperation.
            - Reached out to Maratha sardars, encouraging them to join the Mughals.
            - The Adil Shah of Bijapur, still resentful over past conflicts, agreed and posted an army at the Nizam Shahi border.
        - Maratha Defections to the Mughal Side
            - Jadhav Rao, a prominent Maratha noble, was executed by the Nizam Shah for alleged Mughal ties.
            - His son-in-law, Shahji Bhonsale (father of Shivaji), defected to the Mughals.
            - Shah Jahan granted him a mansab of 5000 and jagirs in the Poona region.
            - Several other Maratha sardars joined the Mughal side.
        - Mughal Invasion of Ahmadnagar (1629)
            - Two Mughal armies were sent:
                - One in the west (Balaghat region).
                - One in the east (Telangana region).
            - Shah Jahan personally moved to Burhanpur to coordinate the campaign.
            - Mughals occupied large parts of Ahmadnagar under relentless pressure.
            - Parenda, the last stronghold, was besieged.
            - The Nizam Shah made a desperate appeal to Bijapur for help.
        - Bijapur’s Sudden Policy Reversal (1630–1631)
            - The Adil Shah of Bijapur had initially supported the Mughals but grew uneasy about their advances.
            - The Mughals refused to hand over the promised territories.
            - Adil Shah switched sides, offering help to the Nizam Shah in exchange for Sholapur.
            - This forced the Mughals to lift the siege of Parenda and retreat.
        - Betrayal and Fall of Ahmadnagar (1632–1633)
            - Fath Khan, son of Malik Ambar, was appointed Peshwa by the Nizam Shah to negotiate peace.
            - Instead, Fath Khan betrayed Ahmadnagar:
                - Murdered the Nizam Shah.
                - Installed a puppet ruler.
                - Recognized Mughal suzerainty by reading the khutba and striking the sikka in Shah Jahan’s name.
                - Was rewarded with a high position in Mughal service.
            - The Poona jagir previously given to Shahji Bhonsale was transferred to Fath Khan.
            - Shahji defected from the Mughals in response.
            - In 1633, Daulatabad was besieged and surrendered, marking the end of the Nizam Shahi dynasty.
        - Continued Resistance and Mughal Strategy
            - Despite Ahmadnagar’s fall, Shahji Bhonsale revived the resistance by installing a Nizam Shahi prince.
            - Bijapur supported Shahji, providing 7,000–8,000 horsemen and persuading many Nizam Shahi nobles to defect.
            - Shahji’s forces grew to 20,000 cavalry, reclaiming large portions of Ahmadnagar state.
        - Mughal-Bijapur Conflict and Final Settlement (1636)
            - Shah Jahan realized that the key issue was Bijapur’s continued interference.
            - He launched a large-scale invasion of Bijapur.
            - He simultaneously offered to renew the earlier treaty, dividing Ahmadnagar’s lands.
            - The Adil Shah changed his stance again, removing anti-Mughal leaders like Murari Pandit.
            - In 1636, a new treaty (ahdnama) was signed:
                - Bijapur recognized Mughal suzerainty.
                - Agreed to pay an indemnity of 20 lakh rupees.
                - Pledged not to interfere in Golconda’s affairs, which came under Mughal protection.
                - Future disputes between Bijapur and Golconda were to be arbitrated by the Mughals.
        - Treaty with Golconda (1636)
            - The Qutb Shah of Golconda agreed to:
                - Include Shah Jahan’s name in the khutba.
                - Remove the Iranian emperor’s name.
                - Pay 2 lakh huns annually to the Mughals (instead of the previous tribute to Bijapur).
        - Significance of the 1636 Settlements
            - These treaties enabled Shah Jahan to achieve Akbar’s ultimate objectives in the Deccan.
            - Mughal suzerainty was firmly established over the region.
            - The Deccani states were now free to expand southward, reducing future threats to the empire.

    - Expansion of Bijapur and Golconda in Karnataka (Post-1636)
        - Conquests in the Karnataka Region
            - After the 1636 treaties, Bijapur and Golconda launched expansionist campaigns in Karnataka.
            - They overran the fertile lands from the Krishna River to Tanjore and beyond.
            - The region was ruled by several petty principalities, including:
                - Nayaks of Tanjore, Jinji, and Madurai.
                - These rulers nominally acknowledged the authority of the Rayal, the former ruler of Vijayanagar.
        - Mughal Mediation and Division of Territories
            - With Shah Jahan’s mediation, Bijapur and Golconda agreed to divide the conquered lands:
                - Bijapur received two-thirds of the territories.
                - Golconda received one-third.
            - This arrangement ensured temporary stability despite frequent quarrels between the two states.
        - Rise and Overexpansion of Deccan Sultanates
            - The rapid conquests doubled the size of Bijapur and Golconda.
            - The states reached their peak in power and prosperity.
            - However, their internal cohesion weakened due to:
                - Overextension of territory.
                - Difficulty in consolidating rule over newly conquered areas.
        - Emergence of Powerful Nobles
            - Ambitious nobles began carving out their own spheres of influence:
                - Shahji Bhonsale and his son Shivaji in Bijapur.
                - Mir Jumla, the most influential noble in Golconda.
            - The Mughals, sensing a shifting balance of power in the Deccan, began demanding compensation for their neutral stance during the conquests.
        - Turning Point: 1656
            - The situation escalated after:
                - The death of Muhammad Adil Shah (Bijapur) in 1656.
                - Aurangzeb’s appointment as the Mughal viceroy of the Deccan.
            - These developments set the stage for further conflicts, which will be discussed in the next chapter.

    - Cultural Contributions of the Deccani States
        - The Deccani states made significant contributions to literature, music, religious harmony, and administration. 
        - They patronized scholars, poets, and musicians, fostering a rich cultural legacy that blended Hindu and Muslim traditions.
        - Literary and Intellectual Patronage
            - Ali Adil Shah (d. 1580)
                - Engaged in discussions with Hindu and Muslim saints and was regarded as a Sufi.
                - Invited Catholic missionaries to his court before Akbar did.
                - Maintained an excellent library and appointed Waman Pandit, a well-known Sanskrit scholar, as its curator.
                - Actively promoted Sanskrit and Marathi, a tradition continued by his successors.
            - Ibrahim Adil Shah II (1580–1627)
                - Ascended the throne at the age of nine and was deeply concerned for the poor, earning him the title ‘Aba Baba’ (Friend of the Poor).
                - Had a keen interest in music and composed the book Kitab-i-Nauras, featuring songs set to various ragas (musical modes).
                - Built a new capital, Nauraspur, and invited a large number of musicians to settle there.
                - Frequently invoked Saraswati, the goddess of music and learning, in his compositions.
                - Due to his broad approach, he was called ‘Jagat Guru’ (World Teacher).
                - Extended patronage to all, including Hindu saints and temples, granting support to Pandharpur, the center of Vithoba worship, which played a crucial role in the Bhakti movement in Maharashtra.
                - His tolerant and inclusive policies were continued by his successors.
        - Religious and Administrative Inclusivity
            - The Ahmadnagar state employed many Maratha families in its administration.
            - The Qutb Shahs of Golconda appointed both Hindus and Muslims to key military, administrative, and diplomatic roles.
            - Under Ibrahim Qutb Shah (d. 1580):
                - Murari Rao became Peshwa, second only to Mir Jumla, the wazir (prime minister).
                - The Nayakwaris, a military-cum-landed elite, held power throughout the dynasty’s rule.
            - From 1672 to 1687, the administration of Golconda was dominated by Madanna and Akkanna, until the kingdom was absorbed into the Mughal Empire.
        - Development of Literature and Poetry
            - Sultan Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah
                - A contemporary of Akbar, he was a major patron of literature and architecture.
                - A poet of high caliber, he wrote in Dakhini Urdu, Persian, and Telugu and left behind an extensive diwan (collection of poetry).
                - His works introduced a secular tone in poetry, covering themes of nature, love, and social life, in addition to praising God and the Prophet.
                - His patronage established Golconda as a major intellectual center, attracting many literary figures.
            - Growth of Dakhini Urdu
                - A key cultural development of this period was the growth of Urdu in its Dakhini form.
                - Successors of Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah and many other poets and writers adopted Urdu as a literary language.
                - Urdu absorbed elements from Hindi and Telugu, incorporating new forms, idioms, themes, and vocabulary.
                - The Bijapur court also promoted Urdu, with the poet laureate Nusrati writing a romantic tale about Prince Manohar of Kanak Nagar and Madhu Malati.
                - From the Deccan, Urdu spread to North India in the 18th century, influencing literary traditions across the subcontinent.
        - The Deccani states played a vital role in promoting literature, music, and religious inclusivity, fostering a unique cultural identity that had a lasting impact on Indian history.
        - Architecture and Art of the Deccani States
            - The Deccani states made remarkable contributions to architecture and art, showcasing a blend of Persian, Indian, and regional styles. 
            - Their architectural achievements remain some of the finest in Indian history.
            - Architectural Marvels
                - Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah and the Char Minar (1591–92)
                    - Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah constructed many buildings, the most famous being the Char Minar.
                    - It was completed in 1591–92 and stood at the center of the newly founded city of Hyderabad.
                    - The structure features:
                        - Four lofty arches facing different directions.
                        - Four four-story minarets, each rising to 48 meters.
                        - Double-screened arches with intricate carvings.
                - Bijapuri Architectural Excellence
                    - The rulers of Bijapur upheld a high standard in architecture, known for its grand structures and meticulous craftsmanship.
                    - Notable Bijapuri Buildings
                        - Ibrahim Rauza
                            - A mausoleum built for Ibrahim Adil Shah.
                            - Considered a masterpiece of Deccani architecture.
                        - Gol Gumbaz (1660)
                            - Features the largest single dome ever constructed.
                            - The structure is well-balanced, with a massive dome and tall, tapering minarets at the corners.
                            - It has remarkable acoustics—a whisper on one side of the huge main hall can be heard clearly at the other end.
            - Flourishing of Painting
                - Painting flourished in the Deccan, reaching its peak during the reign of Ibrahim Adil Shah II (1580–1627).
                - Deccani paintings displayed a unique fusion of Persian, Mughal, and local styles, featuring vibrant colors and expressive figures.
            - Cultural and Communal Harmony
                - The Deccani states overcame sectarian violence of the sixteenth century.
                - They upheld high standards of communal harmony, reflected in their music, literature, architecture, and painting.
            - The Deccan’s artistic and architectural achievements stand as a testament to its rich cultural heritage, blending diverse traditions into a unique and enduring legacy. 🎨🏛️


- India in the First Half of the Seventeenth Century
    - Political and Administrative Developments in India
        - The Era of Progress and Growth (Early 17th Century)
            - The first half of the seventeenth century was marked by progress and growth.
            - The Mughal Empire was ruled by:
                - Jahangir (1605–1627)
                - Shah Jahan (1628–1658)
            - In southern India, the states of Bijapur and Golconda provided:
                - Stability
                - Peace
                - Cultural growth
            - Administrative Policies
                - The Mughal rulers consolidated Akbar’s administrative system.
                - Maintained alliances with the Rajputs and expanded political influence by forming ties with:
                    - The Afghans
                    - The Marathas
                - Focused on cultural growth by:
                    - Constructing beautiful marble buildings
                    - Making the Mughal court a center of cultural life
        - Foreign Relations and Trade
            - Mughals stabilized relations with neighboring Asian powers:
                - Iran
                - The Uzbeks
                - The Ottoman Turks
            - This helped expand India’s foreign trade.
            - Trade concessions were granted to European trading companies to promote commerce.
            - Negative Aspects
                - Economic disparity: The wealth of the ruling class did not benefit peasants and workers.
                - Scientific stagnation: The Mughals ignored Western advancements in science and technology.
                - Political instability: Succession disputes threatened political, economic, and cultural stability.
        - Jahangir’s Accession and Challenges (1605–1627)
            - Jahangir, Akbar’s eldest son, succeeded to the throne without resistance.
            - His younger brothers died due to excessive drinking before Akbar’s death.
            - Shortly after accession, his son Khusrau rebelled but was defeated near Lahore and imprisoned.
            - Conflicts During Jahangir’s Reign
                - Mewar Conflict
                    - Jahangir ended the four-decade-long struggle with Mewar.
                - Deccan Rebellion
                    - Continued conflict with Malik Ambar, who rejected Akbar’s settlement.
                - Eastern India – Afghan Resistance
                    - Afghan chiefs controlled Bengal and received support from Hindu rulers:
                        - Rajas of Jessore, Kamrup (Western Assam), and Cachar.
                    - Akbar had weakened Afghan power but rebellions continued.
        - Jahangir’s Bengal Campaign (1608–1611)
            - Appointment of Islam Khan
                - In 1608, Jahangir appointed Islam Khan, grandson of Shaikh Salim Chishti, as Governor of Bengal.
                - Islam Khan took strategic measures to control Bengal:
                    - Relocated the provincial capital from Rajmahal to Dacca for better control.
            - Islam Khan’s Military Campaigns
                - Defeat of Musa Khan and Barah Bhuiyan (Twelve Zamindars)
                    - Sonargaon was captured after three years of campaigning.
                    - Musa Khan surrendered and was sent to the Mughal court as a prisoner.
                - Defeat of Usman Khan
                    - Usman Khan was defeated in a fierce battle.
                    - The Afghan resistance was crushed.
                    - Jessore and Kamrup were annexed, securing Mughal control over Eastern Bengal.
            - Governance and Reconciliation
                - Jahangir understood that lasting control required goodwill.
                - Treated defeated Afghan chiefs with sympathy:
                    - Many detained zamindars were released and reinstated.
                    - Musa Khan’s estates were restored.
                - Afghans were integrated into the Mughal nobility, strengthening Mughal rule.
        - Later Years of Jahangir’s Reign (1622–1627)
            - By 1622, Jahangir had:
                - Subdued Malik Ambar in the Deccan.
                - Ended the conflict with Mewar.
                - Pacified Bengal.
            - Seemed poised for a long period of peace, but two key events disrupted stability:
                - Persian conquest of Qandhar (major blow to Mughal prestige).
                - Jahangir’s failing health, leading to:
                    - A succession struggle among his sons.
                    - Power struggles among nobles.
                    - Nur Jahan’s active political role in the final years of his reign.

    - Nur Jahan: Influence and Political Role
        - Early Life and Marriage to Jahangir
            - Nur Jahan’s early life:
                - First married to an Iranian noble, Sher Afgan.
                - Sher Afgan died in a clash with the Mughal governor of Bengal.
                - After his death, she stayed in Agra with an elderly relative of Jahangir.
            - Marriage to Jahangir (1611):
                - Met Jahangir at the Meena Bazar.
                - Historians do not believe Jahangir was responsible for her first husband’s death.
                - Came from a respectable family.
                - Her father, Itimad-ud-Daula, was made joint diwan in Jahangir’s first year.
        - Rise of Nur Jahan’s Family
            - Itimad-ud-Daula’s career:
                - Briefly lost favor due to his son’s involvement in Khusrau’s rebellion.
                - Restored to his position after proving his capabilities.
                - Promoted to chief diwan after Nur Jahan’s marriage to Jahangir.
                - Remained a powerful and loyal official until his death ten years later.
            - Nur Jahan’s brother, Asaf Khan:
                - A learned and capable noble.
                - Appointed as khan-i-saman, a prestigious post requiring the emperor’s trust.
                - Married his daughter to Khurram (Shah Jahan).
                - Shah Jahan became Jahangir’s favorite after Khusrau’s rebellion.
        - The “Nur Jahan Junta” Controversy
            - Some modern historians claim:
                - Nur Jahan, her father, and brother formed a “junta” with Shah Jahan.
                - This group controlled Jahangir and influenced imperial appointments.
                - Led to a division in court factions:
                    - The Nur Jahan faction vs. Opponents.
                - Shah Jahan rebelled in 1622, believing Jahangir was under Nur Jahan’s influence.
            - Other historians dispute this view:
                - Jahangir retained political control until his health declined in 1622.
                - His autobiography shows that major decisions were his own.
                - Shah Jahan’s rise was due to his own capabilities, not Nur Jahan’s backing.
                - Jahangir ensured that no noble or prince became too powerful, preventing threats to his authority.
        - Nur Jahan’s Political and Cultural Influence
            - Political Role:
                - Coins were issued in her name.
                - Given the title “Badshah Begum”.
                - Nobles regularly sought her intervention with the emperor.
                - Jahangir still promoted nobles outside her influence, proving he was not entirely dependent on her.
            - Cultural Contributions:
                - Dominated the royal household.
                - Introduced new Persian-influenced fashions.
                - Encouraged Persian art and culture at court.
            - Personal Life and Skills:
                - Jahangir’s constant companion, even on hunting expeditions.
                - Skilled rider and expert marksman.
                - Under Mughal rule, no woman before her had achieved such political significance.
            
    - Shah Jahan’s Rebellion (1622–1626)
        - Causes of the Rebellion
            - The immediate trigger was Shah Jahan’s refusal to proceed to Qandhar, which was under siege by the Persians.
            - His concerns:
                - Feared a long and difficult campaign.
                - Worried that his absence from court would lead to political intrigues against him.
            - His demands before agreeing to the campaign:
                - Full command of the army, including veteran troops from the Deccan.
                - Complete control over Punjab.
                - Authority over several key forts.
            - Jahangir’s reaction:
                - Enraged by Shah Jahan’s defiance.
                - Believed the prince was planning a rebellion.
                - Responded with harsh letters and punitive measures, worsening the conflict.
        - Shah Jahan’s Attempt to Capture Agra
            - Based in Mandu, he launched a sudden attack on Agra, aiming to seize the imperial treasury.
            - Supporters:
