                - Had the full backing of the Deccan army and nobles stationed there.
                - Gujarat and Malwa declared their loyalty to him.
                - Supported by his father-in-law, Asaf Khan, and several nobles at court.
            - Battle near Delhi:
                - Shah Jahan defeated by Mahabat Khan’s forces.
                - Mewar contingent fought bravely, preventing a total rout.
            - Further defeats:
                - Another imperial army was sent to reclaim Gujarat.
                - Shah Jahan was eventually driven out of Mughal territories.
        - Seeking Refuge and Regrouping
            - Took refuge with the Deccan rulers, his former enemies.
            - Later, he crossed into Orissa, surprising the governor.
            - Soon, he captured Bengal and Bihar, strengthening his position.
        - Final Defeat and Reconciliation (1626)
            - Mahabat Khan was again sent to crush the rebellion.
            - Shah Jahan was forced to retreat back to the Deccan.
            - Alliance with Malik Ambar:
                - Teamed up with Malik Ambar, who was at war with the Mughals.
                - However, the situation did not favor him.
            - Shah Jahan’s Apology and Jahangir’s Pardon:
                - Wrote letters of apology to Jahangir.
                - Jahangir, valuing reconciliation, pardoned his son.
                - As part of the settlement:
                    - Shah Jahan’s two sons, Dara and Aurangzeb, were sent to the Mughal court as hostages.
                    - A Deccan territory was assigned to Shah Jahan for his expenses.
            - The agreement was finalized in 1626.
        - The Aftermath of Shah Jahan’s Rebellion
            - Consequences of the Rebellion
                - Duration & Disruptions:
                    - The rebellion lasted four years, distracting the empire from other pressing concerns.
                    - Led to the loss of Qandhar to the Persians.
                    - Encouraged the Deccan rulers to reclaim territories surrendered during Akbar’s reign.
                - Exposing a Fundamental Flaw in the Mughal System:
                    - A successful prince often became a rival power center.
                    - This became particularly problematic when the reigning emperor appeared weak or inactive.
                    - Shah Jahan’s claim:
                        - Argued that with Jahangir’s failing health, all power had shifted to Nur Jahan Begum.
                        - However, this is debatable, as Asaf Khan (his father-in-law) was still the imperial diwan.
                        - Moreover, despite his physical weakness, Jahangir remained mentally sharp and no major decision was taken without his consent.
            - Mahabat Khan’s Coup (1626)
                - Jahangir’s illness heightened fears of power struggles among nobles.
                - Mahabat Khan’s discontent:
                    - He had been instrumental in defeating Shah Jahan’s rebellion.
                    - Felt sidelined at court.
                    - Summoned to provide accounts, but instead arrived with a loyal Rajput force.
                - Seizing Jahangir:
                    - Captured the emperor while the royal camp was crossing the Jhelum River en route to Kabul.
                    - Nur Jahan escaped, but failed in her initial rescue attempts.
                    - Later surrendered herself to Mahabat Khan to remain close to Jahangir.
            - Nur Jahan’s Political Maneuvering
                - Over six months, she:
                    - Exploited Mahabat Khan’s mistakes.
                    - Turned the nobles against him by stirring resentment among his Rajput soldiers.
                - Mahabat Khan’s downfall:
                    - Realizing his weakened position, he abandoned Jahangir and fled from court.
                    - Later, he joined Shah Jahan in the Deccan, awaiting another opportunity.
                - Nur Jahan’s victory:
                    - Considered one of her greatest triumphs, showcasing her political acumen and resilience.
                    - However, her influence waned following Jahangir’s death.
            - Jahangir’s Death & Shah Jahan’s Rise (1627–1628)
                - Jahangir passed away near Lahore in 1627.
                - Asaf Khan’s swift action:
                    - He had been preparing for Shah Jahan’s succession.
                    - Took control of the imperial court with support from the diwan, nobles, and army.
                    - Placed Nur Jahan under house arrest.
                    - Summoned Shah Jahan to Agra, where he was enthroned with great celebration.
                - Securing Power:
                    - To eliminate rivals, Shah Jahan ordered the execution of his:
                        - Imprisoned brother.
                        - Cousins.
                        - Other potential threats.
                    - This set a dangerous precedent—leading to future dynastic struggles.
                    - Ironically, Shah Jahan himself would later suffer the same fate at the hands of his son, Aurangzeb.
            - Nur Jahan’s Final Years & Shah Jahan’s Reign
                - Nur Jahan’s retirement:
                    - Granted a settlement by Shah Jahan.
                    - Lived in seclusion until her death eighteen years later.
                    - Buried in Lahore.
                - Shah Jahan’s reign (1628–1658):
                    - Marked by significant administrative, military, and cultural achievements.
                    - His Deccan policy had long-term implications.
                    - The Mughal foreign policy reached its zenith under his rule.

    - Foreign Policy of the Mughals
        - The Geopolitical Landscape in the 15th and 16th Centuries
            - Following the breakup of the Timurid Empire in the late 15th century, three powerful empires emerged:
                - The Uzbeks – Dominated Transoxiana (Central Asia).
                - The Safavids – Controlled Iran.
                - The Ottomans – Ruled over Turkey, the Middle East, and parts of Europe.
            - Each of these empires had distinct relationships with the Mughals, influencing their foreign policy decisions.
        - The Mughals and the Uzbeks: A Rivalry Rooted in History
            - The Uzbeks were natural enemies of the Mughals.
            - They had previously expelled Babur and the Timurid princes from Samarkand and Khurasan.
            - The Uzbeks also clashed with the Safavids, as both sought control over Khurasan.
            - Importance of Khurasan:
                - It linked Iran and Central Asia.
                - Key trade routes to China and India passed through it.
            - Mughal-Safavid Alliance Against the Uzbeks
                - Since the Mughals and Safavids had no major territorial disputes (except over Qandhar), they formed a natural alliance against the Uzbeks.
                - The Uzbeks tried to exploit sectarian differences, as the Safavids (Shia) had persecuted Sunnis.
                - However, the Mughals (Sunnis) were broadminded and not swayed by sectarian concerns.
                - Uzbek Counterstrategy:
                    - Encouraged fanatic Afghan and Baluchi tribesmen in the northwest frontier (Peshawar-Kabul) to attack the Mughals.
        - The Ottoman Empire and Its Expanding Influence
            - The Ottoman Turks (Usmanlis) were the most powerful empire in West Asia.
            - They expanded rapidly, conquering:
                - Asia Minor & Eastern Europe.
                - Syria, Egypt, and Arabia (by 1529).
            - They received the title “Sultan of Rum” from the caliph in Cairo and later assumed the title Padshah-i-Islam.
            - Ottoman-Safavid Rivalry
                - The rise of the Safavids (Shia) in Iran alarmed the Ottoman Sultans (Sunni).
                - The Ottomans feared that Safavid expansion would encourage Shiism within their own empire.
                - 1514 Battle: The Ottoman Sultan defeated the Shah of Iran in a crucial war.
                - Their conflicts continued, particularly over:
                    - Baghdad.
                    - Northern Iran (Erivan region).
                    - Control of Persian Gulf trade routes.
                    - Expanding influence along the Arabian coast.
        - The Mughal Balancing Act: Navigating Between Iran, Uzbeks, and the Ottomans
            - Ottoman-Persian Rivalry: Made Iran eager to befriend the Mughals.
            - The Uzbeks proposed a tripartite alliance between the Ottomans, Mughals, and Uzbeks against Iran, but the Mughals refused.
                - Such an alliance would have disturbed the Asian balance of power.
                - Would have left the Mughals vulnerable to an aggressive Uzbek expansion.
            - Mughal-Iranian Alliance:
                - Helped counter the Uzbek threat.
                - Boosted trade with Central Asia.
            - Why the Mughals Did Not Form a Stronger Alliance with the Ottomans
                - The Ottomans had a strong navy and were engaged in naval conflicts against European fleets in the Mediterranean.
                - The Mughals lacked a powerful navy, limiting potential cooperation.
                - More importantly, the Mughals were wary of acknowledging Ottoman superiority, as the Ottoman Sultan claimed to be the Caliph’s successor.
        - Conclusion: Key Factors Shaping Mughal Foreign Policy
            - Hostility toward the Uzbeks due to their historical conflicts.
            - Strategic alliance with the Safavids to counter the Uzbeks and facilitate trade.
            - Maintaining distance from the Ottomans, avoiding submission to their Caliphal authority.
            - Geopolitical balancing to prevent over-dependence on any single power.
        - These considerations defined the Mughal foreign policy and influenced their diplomatic and military strategies in the region.

    - Akbar and the Uzbeks
        - Early Mughal-Uzbek Rivalry and Safavid Influence
            - 1510: The Safavids defeated Uzbek chief Shaibani Khan, allowing Babur to briefly regain Samarkand.
            - However, the Uzbeks later defeated the Persians, forcing Babur to abandon Samarkand.
            - Despite this setback, Persian assistance to Babur established a tradition of friendship between the Mughals and the Safavids.
            - Later, Humayun also received help from Shah Tahmasp (Safavid ruler) when he sought refuge after losing India to Sher Shah Suri.
        - Rise of the Uzbeks under Abdullah Khan and Akbar’s Diplomatic Response
            - 1570s: Abdullah Khan Uzbek expanded his power rapidly.
                - 1572–73: He seized Balkh, which, along with Badakhshan, had served as a buffer between the Mughals and the Uzbeks.
                - 1577: He sent an embassy to Akbar, proposing to partition Iran following the death of Shah Tahmasp (1576), which had thrown Iran into disorder.
            - Abdullah Khan’s Proposal:
                - Urged Akbar to join a Sunni alliance to invade Iran and liberate Iraq, Khurasan, and Fars from the Safavid “innovators” (Shias).
                - Approached the Ottoman Sultan with a similar proposal.
            - Akbar’s Strategic Response
                - Refused to engage in sectarian conflicts, emphasizing that differences in law and religion were not sufficient grounds for conquest.
                - Rejected Abdullah Khan’s derogatory remarks against the Safavids, citing the long-standing Mughal-Safavid friendship.
                - Justified his stance by highlighting that after the conquest of Gujarat, a new route for Hajj pilgrims had been established, eliminating concerns about religious travel restrictions.
                - Sent an embassy to Abdullah Khan, making it clear that the Mughals had no interest in a war against Iran.
        - Akbar’s Growing Interest in Central Asia
            - Granted asylum to Timurid prince Mirza Sulaiman, who had been ousted from Badakhshan by his grandson.
            - To facilitate Mughal movements, the Khyber Pass was improved for wheeled traffic.
            - Fear of a Mughal invasion made the gates of Balkh remain closed most of the time.
            - Uzbeks Instigate Unrest on the Northwest Frontier
                - To forestall a Mughal invasion of Badakhshan, Abdullah Khan instigated unrest among frontier tribes through an agent named Jalal, a religious fanatic.
                - The situation worsened to the extent that Akbar had to personally move to Attock.
                - During these operations, Raja Birbal (Akbar’s close friend) was killed in a battle in the Khyber Pass.
        - Direct Mughal-Uzbek Border and the Hindukush Agreement
            - 1585: Abdullah Khan suddenly conquered Badakhshan, prompting both Mirza Sulaiman and his grandson to seek refuge at Akbar’s court.
            - In the same year, with the death of Mirza Hakim (Akbar’s half-brother), Kabul was annexed into the Mughal Empire.
                - This marked the first direct border between the Mughals and the Uzbeks.
            - Diplomatic Engagements Between Akbar and Abdullah Khan
                - Abdullah Khan sent another embassy to Akbar, who was stationed at Attock on the Indus River.
                - Akbar’s continued presence near the frontier unsettled Abdullah Khan.
                - Meanwhile, the Uzbeks successfully captured Khurasan from Iran, reducing the immediate Persian threat.
            - The Hindukush Agreement (1585–1595)
                - Akbar chose diplomacy over war and sent an envoy to Abdullah Khan with a letter and a verbal message.
                - A mutual agreement was reached:
                    - The Hindukush Mountains were established as the boundary between the two empires.
                    - Mughals relinquished claims over Badakhshan and Balkh (formerly ruled by Timurid princes until 1585).
                    - In return, the Uzbeks agreed not to lay claim to Kabul and Qandhar.
                    - Although neither side formally abandoned its claims, this agreement secured a defensible frontier for the Mughals.
                - 1595: Akbar further strengthened his hold on the frontier by acquiring Qandhar.
        - End of the Uzbek Threat and Akbar’s Return to Agra
            - 1586 onwards: Akbar remained in Lahore to monitor the frontier situation.
            - 1598: With the death of Abdullah Khan Uzbek, the Uzbek power fragmented into warring principalities.
            - This eliminated the Uzbek threat for a considerable period, allowing Akbar to return to Agra.

    - Relations with Iran and the Question of Qandhar
        - Mughal-Safavid Relations and the Uzbek Factor
            - The fear of Uzbek power was the primary reason for the alliance between the Mughals and the Safavids, despite:
                - Uzbek efforts to incite anti-Shia sentiments against Iran.
                - Mughal disapproval of the intolerant policies of the Safavids.
            - The only contentious issue between them was Qandhar, which both claimed on strategic, economic, sentimental, and prestige grounds.
            - Historically, Qandhar had been a part of the Timurid Empire, ruled by Babur’s cousins in Herat, until the Uzbeks ousted them in 1507.
        - Strategic and Economic Importance of Qandhar
            - Vital for the defense of Kabul:
                - The fort of Qandhar was one of the strongest in the region, with ample water supply.
                - Located at the junction of roads leading to Kabul and Herat, it dominated southern Afghanistan.
                - A modern commentator remarked:
                    “The Kabul-Ghazni-Qandhar line represented a strategic and logical frontier; beyond Kabul and Khyber, there was no natural line of defense. Moreover, the possession of Qandhar made it easier to control the Afghan and Baluch tribes.”
            - Increasing Mughal Interest in Qandhar:
                - After Akbar conquered Sindh and Baluchistan, Qandhar’s strategic and economic importance grew further.
                - It was a fertile province and a key hub for trade between India and Central Asia.
                - The route through Qandhar to Multan and the Indus River gained significance as Iran’s internal conflicts frequently disrupted traditional trade routes.
                - Akbar also highlighted Qandhar’s importance as an alternative pilgrimage and trade route to Mecca in his discussions with Abdullah Uzbek.
            - For the Safavids, Qandhar was less crucial:
                - Though an important outpost, it was not a vital part of Persia’s defense system, unlike its significance for the Mughals.
        - Early Mughal-Safavid Control Over Qandhar
            - 1522: Babur captured Qandhar while Uzbeks threatened Khurasan.
            - Mid-16th century: Shah Tahmasp seized Qandhar after Humayun’s death.
            - Akbar did not initially attempt to retake it, as it did not impact Mughal security at the time.
            - The situation changed when Abdullah Uzbek’s expansion threatened both Iran and the Mughals.
        - Mughal Conquest of Qandhar (1595) and Aftermath
            - 1595: Akbar recaptured Qandhar, not as part of an anti-Persian agreement with the Uzbeks, but to:
                - Establish a defensible northwestern frontier.
                - Counter the Uzbek advance, since by then, Khurasan had fallen under Uzbek control, cutting off Qandhar from Persia.
            - Despite this conquest, Mughal-Safavid relations remained cordial.
        - Jahangir, Shah Abbas I, and the Persian Reconquest of Qandhar (1622)
            - Shah Abbas I (ruled 1588–1625), one of the greatest Safavid rulers, sought good relations with the Mughals.
                - Regular exchange of embassies and gifts occurred between him and Jahangir.
                - Established diplomatic and commercial ties with the Deccan Sultanates, which Jahangir did not object to.
                - Cultural relations between the two empires deepened, with Nur Jahan playing an active role.
                - A famous Mughal painting depicts Jahangir and Shah Abbas embracing, symbolizing their perceived friendship.
            - 1620: Shah Abbas demanded the return of Qandhar and prepared for an invasion.
                - Jahangir was caught off guard, as he was diplomatically isolated and militarily unprepared.
                - Though efforts were made to fortify Qandhar, Shah Jahan’s unrealistic demands for leading the campaign caused delays.
                - 1622: Persia recaptured Qandhar.
            - Aftermath of the Loss:
                - Shah Abbas attempted to smooth tensions by sending a lavish embassy and offering diplomatic justifications, which Jahangir formally accepted.
                - However, the cordiality in Mughal-Safavid relations ended after this event.
        - Shah Jahan’s Recapture of Qandhar (1638)
            - 1629: After Shah Abbas’ death, Iran faced internal disturbances.
            - 1638:
                - After resolving his Deccan campaigns, Shah Jahan persuaded Ali Mardan Khan (Persian governor of Qandhar) to defect to the Mughal side.
                - Thus, Qandhar was restored to the Mughals.

    - The Balkh Campaign and Mughal-Uzbek Relations
        - Background: Mughal Concerns and the Uzbek Threat
            - Shah Jahan’s conquest of Qandhar was only a step toward a larger goal: securing the northwestern frontier from recurrent Uzbek attacks.
            - The Uzbeks, under Nazr Muhammad (ruler of Bokhara and Balkh) and his son Abdul Aziz, were ambitious and frequently raided Kabul and Ghazni, aided by Afghan tribes.
            - The Mughal court viewed these incursions as a serious threat to stability in Kabul and the Khyber Pass region.
        - Mughal Intervention and Initial Advances (1646)
            - Nazr Muhammad’s appeal for help:
                - Following a rebellion by his son Abdul Aziz, Nazr Muhammad retained only Balkh and sought Mughal assistance.
                - Shah Jahan, now secure from Persian threats, accepted the appeal.
            - Deployment of Mughal forces:
                - Shah Jahan moved from Lahore to Kabul and sent a large Mughal army under Prince Murad (his son) to support Nazr Muhammad.
                - The army composition:
                    - 50,000 cavalry, 10,000 infantry
                    - Included musketeers, rocketeers, gunners, and a Rajput contingent
                - Departed from Kabul in mid-1646
            - Shah Jahan’s strategy:
                - Murad was instructed to treat Nazr Muhammad with respect and restore Balkh to him if he remained submissive.
                - If Nazr Muhammad wished to reclaim Samarqand and Bokhara, the Mughals were to assist him.
                - The broader objective: Establish a Mughal-friendly ruler in Bokhara.
        - Setbacks: Prince Murad’s Miscalculations
            - Murad’s impatience led to diplomatic failure:
                - Instead of waiting for coordination with Nazr Muhammad, Murad marched on Balkh unilaterally.
                - Ordered his men to enter Nazr Muhammad’s fort, causing Nazr Muhammad to flee.
                - This forced the Mughals to occupy Balkh, but they faced a hostile and uncooperative local population.
            - Rising Uzbek resistance:
                - Abdul Aziz rallied Uzbek tribes in Transoxiana.
                - Raised an army of 120,000 men across the Oxus River to challenge the Mughals.
        - Mughal Victory under Prince Aurangzeb (1647)
            - Aurangzeb replaces Prince Murad:
                - Murad, eager to return home, was replaced by Prince Aurangzeb.
                - The Mughals did not defend the Oxus directly (as it was easily fordable) but instead:
                    - Stationed pickets at strategic locations
                    - Kept the main force together for rapid response
            - Battle of Balkh (1647):
                - Abdul Aziz’s forces crossed the Oxus and engaged the Mughals outside Balkh.
                - The Mughals decisively defeated the Uzbeks, forcing them to retreat.
        - Diplomatic Breakdown and Mughal Withdrawal
            - Nazr Muhammad, now in Persian refuge, sought Mughal help again.
            - Shah Jahan’s conditions for reinstating Nazr Muhammad:
                - Nazr Muhammad was required to personally apologize and submit to Prince Aurangzeb before being restored to power.
                - However, Nazr Muhammad refused to humble himself, knowing that the Mughals could not hold Balkh indefinitely.
            - Mughal evacuation of Balkh (October 1647):
                - As winter approached and supplies ran low, the Mughals retreated.
                - Uzbeks harassed the retreating forces, leading to heavy Mughal casualties.
                - Aurangzeb’s leadership prevented a disaster, ensuring some order during the withdrawal.
        - Assessment of the Balkh Campaign
            - Military Success
                - First major victory of Indian arms in Central Asia (Balkh conquest, Uzbek defeat).
                - Defeated a 120,000-strong Uzbek force at Balkh (1647).
            - Political and Strategic Failure
                - Mughals lacked resources to hold Balkh long-term.
                - Balkh’s hostile population and Persian hostility made Mughal influence unsustainable.
                - Nazr Muhammad refused submission, leading to Mughal withdrawal.
            - Long-term Impact
                - Prevented the formation of a united Uzbek state, which could have threatened Mughal control over Kabul.
                - Nazr Muhammad remained friendly toward the Mughals after being reinstated, maintaining diplomatic ties.
                - However, the campaign ultimately yielded little political gain and drained significant Mughal resources.
        - Conclusion: Shah Jahan’s Strategic Dilemma
            - Unlike some interpretations, Shah Jahan did not aim to fix the Mughal frontier along the Oxus River, as it was not defensible.
            - Nor was his goal to reclaim Samarqand and Farghana, despite nostalgic references.
            - His primary aim was to secure Balkh and Badakhshan as buffer regions against Uzbek threats.
            - Alternative strategy:
                - Perhaps sticking to the Kabul-Ghazni-Qandhar line (established by Akbar) would have been wiser.
                - This could have saved men and money while ensuring long-term stability.

    - Mughal-Persian Relations—The Last Phase
        - Persian Conquest of Qandhar (1649) and Mughal Response
            - The Mughal setback in Balkh led to:
                - Revival of Uzbek hostility in the Kabul region.
                - Afghan tribal unrest in the Khyber-Ghazni area.
                - Persian confidence to attack Qandhar, capturing it in 1649.
            - Shah Jahan’s Reaction:
                - The loss of Qandhar was a severe blow to Mughal prestige.
                - He launched three consecutive campaigns under royal princes to recover it.
        - Three Failed Mughal Campaigns to Recover Qandhar (1649–1653)
            - First Attempt (1649) – Led by Aurangzeb
                - Army size: 50,000 troops.
                - Battle outcome:
                    - The Mughals defeated the Persians outside Qandhar, but failed to capture the fort due to strong Persian resistance.
            - Second Attempt (1652) – Led by Aurangzeb
                - Tried again after three years, but failed once more.
            - Third Attempt (1653) – Led by Dara Shikoh
                - Most ambitious effort, led by Shah Jahan’s favorite son.
                - Despite his claims, Dara failed to force the fort into surrender.
                - Use of heavy artillery:
                    - Attempted to breach the fort with two of the empire’s largest cannons.
                    - Proved ineffective against Qandhar’s strong fortifications.
        - Causes of Mughal Failure at Qandhar
            - Not due to weak artillery, as some historians suggest.
            - Key factors:
                - Strength of Qandhar’s fortifications.
                - Skill and determination of the Persian defenders.
                - Limitations of medieval artillery in siege warfare.
            - Comparable to the Mughal experience in the Deccan, where strong forts posed similar challenges.
        - Strategic Impact of the Loss of Qandhar
            - Shah Jahan’s attachment to Qandhar was more sentimental than strategic.
                - The declining power of both the Uzbeks and the Safavids had reduced Qandhar’s strategic importance.
            - More damaging than the loss itself was the repeated Mughal failures, which:
                - Hurt their military prestige.
                - Undermined their reputation in Central Asia.
            - However, the Mughal Empire remained powerful, as seen during Aurangzeb’s reign.
                - In 1680, even the Ottoman Sultan sent an embassy to Aurangzeb, seeking his support.
        - Aurangzeb’s Diplomatic Approach and the 1668 Crisis
            - Abandoning the Qandhar contest:
                - Aurangzeb chose not to pursue further military campaigns.
                - Quietly resumed diplomatic relations with Persia.
            - Crisis of 1668:
                - Shah Abbas II of Iran insulted the Mughal envoy, made disparaging remarks about Aurangzeb, and threatened invasion.
                - Mughal military activity increased in Punjab and Kabul, preparing for a possible Persian attack.
                - However, before war could break out, Shah Abbas II died.
                - His weak successors ensured that Persia ceased to be a threat to India until Nadir Shah emerged more than 50 years later.
        - Long-Term Mughal Foreign Policy and Strategic Frontier
            - The Mughals successfully maintained a scientific frontier in the northwest:
                - Hindukush served as the natural boundary, with Qandhar as its outer bastion.
                - Military and diplomatic measures together protected India from foreign invasions.
            - Diplomacy was central to Mughal foreign policy, reinforcing their military strategy.
            - Friendship with Persia remained a priority, despite the temporary setbacks over Qandhar.
            - The idea of reclaiming the Mughal homelands in Central Asia was more of a diplomatic ploy than a serious military objective.
        - The Mughal Empire’s Status Among Major Islamic Powers
            - The Mughals insisted on parity with the leading Asian powers:
                - Safavids (Persia):
                    - The Safavids claimed superiority due to their lineage from the Prophet.
                - Ottoman Sultans (Turkey):
                    - Held the title Padshah-i-Islam and claimed to be the successors of the Caliphs of Baghdad.
            - Mughal foreign policy also promoted India’s commercial interests:
                - Kabul and Qandhar served as twin gateways for India’s trade with Central Asia.

    - Growth of Administration: Mansabdari System and the Mughal Army
        - Continuity and Modifications in Administration
            - The administrative and revenue systems introduced by Akbar continued under Jahangir and Shah Jahan, with only minor modifications.
            - However, significant changes took place in the functioning of the mansabdari system, particularly in salary structures, military obligations, and land distribution.
        - Changes in Mansabdars’ Salaries and Military Composition
            - Under Akbar
                - Mansabdars’ salaries:
                    - Paid at an average rate of Rs. 240 per annum per sawar for maintaining their contingents.
            - Under Jahangir
                - Salary Reduction:
                    - Reduced to Rs. 200 per annum per sawar.
                - Additional Allowances:
                    - Mansabdars were allowed to retain 5% of their salary to cover contingent expenses.
                - Ethnic Composition of the Military:
                    - The Mughal military favored mixed contingents to prevent tribal or ethnic exclusivity.
                    - Soldiers were drawn from:
                        - Irani and Turani Mughals, Indian Afghans, and Rajputs in fixed proportions.
                    - However, in special cases, Mughal or Rajput mansabdars were permitted to recruit exclusively from their ethnic groups.
        - Structural Modifications in the Mansabdari System
            - Jahangir’s Reforms: Du-aspah Sih-aspah System
                - Introduced to allow nobles to maintain larger numbers of troopers without increasing their sawar rank.
                - A mansabdar granted this distinction was required to maintain double the number of troopers indicated by his sawar rank.
                - Example:
                    - A mansabdar with a zat rank of 3000 and a sawar rank of 3000 du-aspah sih-aspah had to maintain 6000 troopers.
                    - Normally, a mansabdar’s sawar rank could not exceed his zat rank.
            - Shah Jahan’s Reforms: Reducing the Sawar Requirement
                - Further reduction in the number of sawars a noble was required to maintain.
                - A noble was expected to maintain only one-third of his sawar rank, and in some cases, only one-fourth.
                - Example:
                    - A noble with a zat rank of 3000 and a sawar rank of 3000 had to maintain only 1000 troopers (one-third rule).
                    - If he held the du-aspah sih-aspah distinction, he would maintain 2000 troopers instead of 6000.
        - Payment Methods: Jagirs vs. Cash Salaries
            - Mansabdars were theoretically paid in rupees, but their salaries were typically disbursed through jagirs.
            - Preference for jagirs:
                - Cash payments were often delayed and involved bureaucratic hurdles.
                - Control over land was a symbol of social prestige.
            - The Mughals bureaucratized the nobility by implementing a structured system of ranks and regulations.
            - However, they failed to eliminate the nobility’s feudal attachment to land, which remained a major challenge.
        - Allocation of Jagirs and the Revenue System
            - The revenue department maintained a register of assessed income (jama).
            - Income was recorded not in rupees but in dams (40 dams = 1 rupee).
            - This document, called jama-dami, provided income estimates for different regions.
            - Issues with Financial Constraints
                - As the number of mansabdars increased and financial pressures grew, even these modifications were insufficient.
                - Drastic salary cuts could have led to discontent among nobles, which the rulers could not afford.
                - To manage expenses, a new scaling system was introduced to further reduce the quota of troopers and horses a noble had to maintain.
        - The Month-Scale System
            - The salaries of mansabdars were adjusted based on a month-scale, reducing their financial obligations.
            - Salaries were calculated for 10 months, 8 months, 6 months, or even less.
            - Example:
                - A mansabdar with a zat rank of 3000 and a sawar rank of 3000 was initially required to maintain 1000 troopers under the one-third rule.
                - Under the 10-month scale, he had to maintain 1800 horses.
                - Under the 5-month scale, he had to maintain only 1000 horses.
            - Allowances rarely fell below 5 months or exceeded 10 months.
            - Effects on Jagirs and Cash Salaries
                - This system applied to both jagirs and cash salaries.
                - Under Shah Jahan, agricultural expansion and cash crop production led to increased jagir incomes (jama-dami).
                - However, this increase matched the overall price rise, reducing its financial benefit.
                - Many Marathas in Mughal service were given mansabs on a 5-monthly basis or even less, granting them high rank in name while reducing their actual cavalry obligations.
                - This weakened the efficiency of the Mughal cavalry, as the availability of remounts declined.
        - Challenges in the Mansabdari System
            - Importance of the Dagh (Branding) and Jagirdari System
                - The mansabdari system relied on the proper functioning of:
                    - Dagh system (branding of horses and soldiers).
                    - Jagirdari system (land allocation for salaries).
                - If the dagh system was poorly enforced, the state suffered financial losses.
                - If the jama-dami was exaggerated or jagirdars were not paid properly, it led to discontent and poor military upkeep.
            - Effectiveness Under Shah Jahan
                - Despite these challenges, the system functioned effectively under Shah Jahan due to:
                    - Meticulous administration.
                    - Careful selection of competent officials, particularly wazirs.
                    - Strict discipline, clear promotion policies, and a well-structured rewards system.
                - This ensured that the Mughal nobility remained loyal and efficient in both administration and military affairs, securing and expanding the empire.

    - The Mughal Army
        - The Mughal army was primarily cavalry-based, with mansabdars providing the majority of the troops. 
        - However, the Mughal emperors also maintained independent military units outside the mansabdari system. 
            - These included the ahadis, the royal bodyguards (walashahis), the infantry (piyadgan), and the artillery corps.
        - Cavalry: The Backbone of the Army
            - The cavalry formed the principal arm of the Mughal army.
            - Apart from the mansabdars’ contingents, the emperors recruited ahadis, individual troopers who were personally selected and highly trusted.
            - Ahadis were known as “gentlemen-troopers” and received significantly higher salaries than regular soldiers.
            - Each ahadi mustered up to five horses, though some shared a horse.
            - Their duties were diverse:
                - Many worked in imperial offices as clerks, court painters, or foremen in royal karkhanas (workshops).
                - Some were adjutants and messengers, carrying imperial orders.
                - Others served as skilled musketeers (banduq-andaz) and bowmen (tir-andaz).
            - During Shah Jahan’s reign, the number of ahadis reached 7,000, and they were well-distributed across different army divisions.
            - In addition to the ahadis, the emperors maintained:
                - A corps of royal bodyguards (walashahis), responsible for protecting the emperor.
                - Armed palace guards, who served on foot within the citadel and palace.
        - Infantry (Piyadgan): A Miscellaneous Force
            - The infantry was a large but heterogeneous group.
            - It mainly consisted of matchlock-bearers (banduqchis), who received salaries of 3 to 7 rupees per month.
            - Other categories of foot soldiers included:
                - Porters, servants, news runners, swordsmen, wrestlers, and slaves.
                - Slaves were not as numerous as in the Delhi Sultanate period.
                - Occasionally, a slave could rise to become a gentleman-trooper, but infantrymen generally had low social status.
        - Artillery: Strengths and Weaknesses
            - The Mughal artillery was well-organized and consisted of:
                - Heavy guns, used for sieging forts and defending cities.
                - Light artillery, which was highly mobile and accompanied the emperor on campaigns.
            - The Mughals initially employed Ottoman and Portuguese gunners to improve their artillery.
            - By Aurangzeb’s time, Mughal artillery had significantly improved, making it difficult for foreigners to find employment in this sector.
            - However, there were limitations:
                - Some big guns were too large and impractical.
                - A modern writer noted that these huge cannons made more noise than impact, as they:
                    - Could not be fired frequently.
                    - Were prone to bursting, killing the operators.
                - Despite this, Shah Jahan’s field artillery was well-organized and effective.
                    - French traveler Bernier described the Mughal “artillery of the stirrup”, which included:
                        - 50 small brass field guns.
                        - Each gun mounted on a painted carriage with two ammunition boxes.
                        - Guns drawn by two horses, with a third horse in reserve.
                    - Swivel guns were also mounted on elephants and camels for added mobility.
        - Strength of the Mughal Army
            - Under Shah Jahan, the Mughal army had:
                - 2,00,000 cavalry (excluding troops stationed in districts).
                - 40,000 infantry (excluding non-combatants).
            - Under Aurangzeb, the cavalry strength rose to 2,40,000.
        - Mughal Military Efficiency Compared to Other Powers
            - European travelers, like Bernier, often criticized the Mughal army’s efficiency.
            - A deeper analysis shows:
                - The Mughal infantry was poorly organized, lacked discipline, and was ineffective compared to European armies.
                - In Europe, infantry became a dominant force due to the flintlock gun, which outclassed cavalry.
                - Indian rulers only realized this weakness in the 18th century, when European-trained armies easily defeated them.
            - However, the Mughals were not inferior to other Asian armies:
                - The Mughal army defeated the Uzbeks in the Balkh campaigns, proving it could match Central Asian and Persian forces in open battle.
                - Compared to European armies, the Mughals lagged in:
                    - Infantry training and organization.
                    - Naval warfare, which was a key strength of European maritime powers.
            - The Mughal military system was deeply tied to the jagirdari system, which functioned like a feudal structure. 
            - The army’s strength and efficiency depended on the stability of land revenue administration. 
            - Ultimately, the inability to modernize the infantry and artillery, along with weaknesses in naval power, contributed to the Mughal Empire’s decline in later years.


- Economic and Social Life under the Mughals
    - Economic and Social Conditions under the Mughals
        - Introduction
            - By the end of the seventeenth century, the Mughal Empire had reached its territorial peak but faced numerous political and administrative challenges. 
            - In the economic and social spheres, the period from Akbar to the late seventeenth century saw no fundamental changes, though there were significant social and economic developments.
        - Standard of Living and Village Life
            - Living Conditions of the Masses
                - European travelers who visited India described its wealth, particularly the opulent lifestyle of the ruling elite, in contrast to the widespread poverty of peasants, artisans, and laborers.
                - Babur noted the scanty clothing of the lower classes, stating that “peasants and people of low standing go about naked.”
                - European travelers like Ralph Fitch (late sixteenth century) and De Laet observed that many commoners had inadequate clothing, especially during winters.
                - Footwear was a luxury. Nikitin recorded that people in the Deccan walked barefoot, and Moreland found little mention of shoes north of the Narmada, except in Bengal.
            - Housing and Furniture
                - Villagers lived in simple mud houses, similar to those still seen today.
                - Furniture was minimal—cots, bamboo mats, and earthen utensils made by the village potter were common.
                - Copper and bell-metal utensils were expensive and rarely used by the poor.
            - Diet and Food Availability
                - Staple foods: rice, millet, pulses, fish (in Bengal and coastal areas), and meat (in the southern peninsula).
                - In North India, chapatis made of wheat or coarse grains, along with pulses and vegetables, were common.
                - Ghee and oil were cheap, making them a staple part of the diet, whereas salt and sugar were expensive.
                - On balance, while clothing was scarce, people had access to relatively better food.
        - Economic Structure of Villages
            - Peasant Classes and Land Ownership
                - Peasant income is hard to assess as villages operated largely on barter, with village artisans receiving compensation in commodities rather than money.
                - Peasant classifications:
                    - Khudkasht (resident cultivators): Owned their land, paid land revenue at customary rates, and often rented out plows and bullocks to poorer peasants.
                    - Muzarian (tenant cultivators): Worked on rented land, paid higher land revenue, and had lesser economic stability.
                    - Landless laborers: Often from the “untouchable” or lower castes, they worked under severe hardships and faced the greatest suffering during famines.
            - Village Hierarchy and Inequality
                - Villages were highly unequal, with dominant castes monopolizing land ownership and exploiting lower sections.
                - While khudkasht cultivators had relatively better conditions, they too were under the control of zamindars, who in turn faced heavy state demands.
        - Agriculture and Cash Crops
            - Agricultural Efficiency and Innovations
                - India had a diversified agricultural economy, growing wheat, rice, gram, barley, pulses, and bajra, alongside cash crops like cotton, indigo, chay (red dye), sugarcane, and oilseeds.
                - Cash crops paid higher land revenue and were often sold for cash.
                - Peasants adapted to price changes and were open to cultivating new crops.
                - New crops introduced during the seventeenth century:
                    - Tobacco
                    - Maize
                - Eighteenth-century additions:
                    - Potatoes
                    - Red chilies
            - Peasant Rights and Land Tenure
                - Peasants could not be dispossessed as long as they paid revenue.
                - Land could be sold with community approval, and children inherited land rights.
                - However, land revenue demands (sometimes as high as half the produce) left peasants with minimal surplus, preventing agricultural expansion.
        - Economic Conditions in Cities
            - Urban Workforce and Wages
                - Cities had a large population of poor artisans, servants, slaves, soldiers, and manual laborers.
                - Salaries:
                    - Lowest-grade servants earned less than two rupees per month.
                    - Common laborers and foot soldiers earned around three rupees per month.
                - It was estimated that a man could feed his family on two rupees a month.
            - Comparison with Later Periods
                - Moreland noted that real wages had remained unchanged since Mughal times, though food had been more affordable then.
                - Under British rule, India’s economic stagnation contrasted sharply with Europe’s wealth growth during the same period.
        - Conclusion
            - Despite hardships, Mughal-era peasants had relatively stable access to food and land. However, widespread inequality persisted, with peasants burdened by high taxation and exploitative village hierarchies. 
            - In urban areas, low wages and economic stagnation prevented upward mobility. While agricultural output remained robust, limited technological advancements and heavy state taxation hindered long-term economic growth.

    - The Ruling Classes: The Nobles and Zamindars
        - Composition of the Mughal Nobility
            - The ruling class in medieval India included the nobility and zamindars.
            - The Mughal nobility formed a privileged class socially and economically.
            - Theoretically, entry into the nobility was open to all, but aristocratic families—both Indian and foreign—had a significant advantage.
        - Origins and Ethnic Diversity
            - Initially, most Mughal nobles were from Turan and neighboring regions like Tajikistan, Khurasan, and Iran.
            - Although Babur was a Turk, the Mughals did not adopt a narrow racial policy.
            - Babur attempted to ally with Afghan nobles, but their instability led to continued conflicts in Bihar and Bengal until Akbar’s reign.
            - Over time, more Afghans, Indian Muslims (Shaikhzadas or Hindustanis), and Hindus were recruited.
        - Inclusion of Hindus in the Nobility
            - Akbar began inducting Hindus, particularly Rajputs, into the nobility.
            - The Kachhwahas dominated initially, and by 1594, Hindus formed about 16% of the nobility.
            - Hindu nobles, including Raja Man Singh, Raja Birbal, and Raja Todar Mal, held influential positions.
            - The recruitment of Rajputs reinforced the aristocratic nature of the nobility.
            - Many Kayasthas and Khatris also served in administrative roles, with some rising to noble status.
        - Expansion and Stability Under Later Rulers
            - Under Jahangir and Shah Jahan, the nobility became more stable, regulated by the mansabdari system.
            - Rules ensured orderly promotions, discipline, and competent recruitment.
            - The high salaries and religious tolerance of the Mughal court attracted foreign talent, leading to a “reverse brain drain.”
            - While many nobles migrated to India, they permanently settled, assimilating into Indian culture while retaining some unique traits.
        - Increase in Indigenous Nobles and Maratha Integration
            - By Jahangir and Shah Jahan’s reigns, most nobles were India-born.
            - The number of Afghans, Indian Muslims, and Hindus in the nobility continued to grow.
            - The Marathas entered the nobility during this period, with Jahangir recognizing their importance in the Deccan.
            - Shah Jahan continued this policy, and Aurangzeb later recruited many Marathas and Deccani Muslims.
            - Under Aurangzeb, Hindus formed about 33% of the nobility, with Marathas comprising more than half of them.
        - Wealth and Extravagance of the Nobility
            - Nobles received extremely high salaries but had significant expenditures:
                - Maintaining large households, servants, stables, and harems.
                - Living in luxurious mansions with gardens and running water.
                - Wearing expensive clothing and indulging in lavish meals.
                - Spending heavily on imported fruits and luxury items like ice.
                - Wearing elaborate jewelry, including earrings introduced by Jahangir.
                - Presenting mandatory gifts to the emperor twice a year, though they received gifts in return.
            - Despite their wealth, few nobles remained debt-free or left substantial inheritances.
        - Economic Role of the Nobility
            - Nobles contributed to economic activity by:
                - Purchasing or receiving land grants, developing orchards, and building markets (mandis).
                - Lending money to traders and engaging in commerce, often through intermediaries.
                - Abul Fazl encouraged nobles to invest in business ventures, even though Islamic law condemned usury.
            - Some nobles misused their power to monopolize trade, but this did not significantly disrupt commerce.
            - Even royal family members engaged in trade, with some owning ships for foreign trade.
            - Mir Jumla, a noble under Aurangzeb, ran a vast trade network with Persia, Arabia, and Southeast Asia.
        - Cultural and Bureaucratic Aspects of the Nobility
            - The Mughal nobility, despite ethnic divisions, formed a composite ruling class.
            - It promoted shared cultural values by supporting painters, musicians, poets, and scholars in Persian and Hindawi.
            - Though primarily feudal, it also exhibited bureaucratic and commercial tendencies.
            - The ruling class did not obstruct economic growth but whether it could have led India toward capitalism remains debatable.
        - Challenges and Decline Under Aurangzeb
            - The nobility expanded rapidly in the 17th century, leading to tensions and factionalism.
            - Issues within the sagirdari system weakened discipline and efficiency under Aurangzeb.
            - These challenges contributed to the decline of the nobility, a topic explored in later sections.

    Zamindars and the Rural Gentry
        - Ownership and Rights in Land
            - Land ownership in medieval India was primarily hereditary, but new rights were constantly created:
                - Anyone who first brought land under cultivation was considered its owner.
                - Large areas of cultivable wasteland (banjar) allowed new settlements and land ownership.
                - Many zamindars owned land they cultivated and had hereditary rights to collect land revenue (taluqa/zamindari).
                - For revenue collection, zamindars received a share of up to 25% in some regions.
                - However, peasants retained hereditary rights and could not be dispossessed if they paid revenue.
        - Hierarchy in Rural Society
            - Zamindars vs. Peasants
                - Zamindars had hereditary rights over land revenue collection but were not absolute landowners.
                - Peasants had the right to cultivate the land and could not be removed arbitrarily.
            - Rajas and Chiefs
                - Above zamindars were rajas, who controlled larger or smaller territories with varying autonomy.
                - Persian writers referred to rajas as zamindars to emphasize their subordinate status.
                - Despite this, rajas held a higher position than ordinary zamindars.
                - Medieval rural society was highly segmented and hierarchical.
        - Military and Fortifications
            - Zamindars, rajas, and chiefs maintained private armed forces:
            - Lived in forts (garhis), which served as refuges and status symbols.
            - Possessed significant military strength (as per Ain-i-Akbari under Akbar):
                - 3,84,558 cavalry
                - 42,77,057 foot soldiers
                - 1,863 elephants
                - 4,260 cannons
            - However, due to dispersion, they could not field all forces at one location.
        - Social and Economic Role
            - Zamindars had close ties with peasants, often through caste, clan, or tribal affiliations.
            - They were present throughout India under various names:
                - Deshmukh, Patil, Nayak, etc.
            - Their widespread influence made them indispensable to the central authority.
        - Living Standards
            - Wealth varied among zamindars:
                - Smaller zamindars may have lived like peasants.
                - Larger zamindars had lifestyles comparable to petty rajas or nobles.
                - Most formed a loosely organized rural gentry, living in the countryside.
        - Beyond Revenue Collection
            - Zamindars were more than just tax collectors:
                - Maintained caste and kinship ties with landowning agricultural communities.
                - Set social standards and influenced local society.
                - Provided capital and organization for:
                    - Establishing new villages.
                    - Expanding and improving agriculture.

    - The Middle Strata in Medieval India
        - Debate on the Existence of a Middle Class
            - French traveler Bernier claimed India had no “middle state”, stating people were either extremely rich or in misery.
            - However, this was not entirely accurate:
                - India had a large class of wealthy traders and merchants, some among the richest in the world at the time.
                - These merchants had traditional rights, including protection of life and property.
                - Unlike European merchants, they did not administer towns.
                - In Europe, such rights were acquired under special conditions but were later curtailed by strong territorial states (e.g., France and Britain).
        - Defining the Middle Strata
            - If the “middle state” refers to people between the rich and the poor, then such groups existed in Mughal India:
                - Small mansabdars
                - Petty shopkeepers
                - Master craftsmen
                - Professionals, including:
                    - Hakims (physicians)
                    - Leading musicians and artists
                    - Historians and scholars
                    - Qazis (judges) and theologians
            - Petty officials and clerks, managing Mughal administration.
        - Economic and Social Standing
            - Petty officials:
                - Paid in cash but often supplemented income through corruption.
            - Scholars and religious figures:
                - Given small land grants for sustenance:
                    - Known as madad-i-maash in Mughal India.
                    - Called sasan in Rajasthan.
                - Though grants were renewed by rulers, they often became hereditary in practice.
        - Role and Influence
            - Members of the middle strata often formed part of the rural gentry, serving as a link between villages and towns.
            - Writers, historians, and theologians frequently emerged from this class.
            - However, the middle strata were not a single, unified class:
                - Interests varied significantly.
                - Members belonged to different religious and caste groups, reflecting the diversity of medieval Indian society.

    - Organization of Trade and Commerce in Medieval India
        - Structure of Indian Trading Classes
            - The trading classes in India were large, well-organized, and highly professional.
            - Traders were divided based on their scale of operations:
                - Long-distance and inter-regional traders: Known as seth, bora, or modi.
                - Local and retail traders: Called deoparis or banik.
                - Baniks also acted as agents in villages and towns, purchasing food grains and cash crops.
                - Banjaras: Specialized in transporting bulk goods over long distances with thousands of oxen carrying food grains, pulses, ghee, and salt.
                - Luxury goods like textiles and silks were transported using camels, mules, or carts.
                - Bulk goods were more efficiently transported via rivers and boats.
        - Inter-Regional Trade and Major Trade Centers
            - Trade was not limited to luxury goods; foodstuffs and textiles were key components.
            - Major trade routes and centers:
                - Bengal: Exported sugar, rice, muslin, and silk.
                - Coromandel Coast: A hub for textile production with active trade links to Gujarat.
                - Gujarat: The gateway for foreign goods; exported fine textiles and silks (patola) to North India.
                - North India: Imported luxury items while exporting indigo and food grains.
                - Lahore: A handicraft production center and a distribution hub for Kashmiri shawls and carpets.
                - Punjab and Sindh: Products moved down the Indus River, linking trade to Kabul and Qandahar.
        - Trade Networks and Financial Systems
            - A complex trading network linked wholesalers, merchants, and local traders via agents (gumashtas) and commission agents (dalals).
            - European traders (Dutch and English) observed the activeness and efficiency of Indian merchants.
            - Financial mechanisms facilitated trade:
                - Hundi (letters of credit): Allowed easy transmission of money across the country.
                - Hundis often included insurance, with rates depending on value, destination, and mode of transport (land, river, or sea).
                - Sarrafs (shroffs): Money changers who also dealt in hundis, functioning as private bankers.
                - Rich traders like Virji Vohra established agency houses across India, West Asia, and Southeast Asia, reducing financial risk.
        - Diversity of Trading Communities
            - The trading community in India was not restricted to a single caste or religion:
                - Gujarati merchants: Included Hindus, Jains, and Bohras (Muslims).
                - Rajasthan: Oswals, Maheshwaris, and Agarwals, later known as Marwaris.
                - Central Asia trade: Dominated by Multanis, Afghans, and Khatris.
                - South India: Led by Chettis (Coromandel Coast) and Muslim traders from Malabar (both Indian and Arab).
        - Wealth and Influence of Merchants
            - Some Indian merchants were as wealthy and powerful as the merchant princes of Europe:
                - Virji Vohra: Dominated Surat trade, owned a large fleet of ships, and was among the wealthiest of his time.
                - Abdul Ghafur Bohra (died 1718): Left 55 lakh rupees and 17 sea-going ships.
                - Malay Chetti, Kashi Viranna, and Sunca Rama Chetti: Wealthy traders with extensive commercial dealings.
                - Agra, Delhi, Balasore, and Bengal had many rich merchants.
        - Merchants’ Lifestyle and Legal Protection
            - Wealthy merchants in port towns lived luxuriously, mimicking nobility.
            - European travelers noted their well-built houses, though ordinary merchants lived above their shops.
            - Bernier observed that merchants hid their wealth to avoid being taxed unfairly.
            - However, Mughal emperors protected merchant property:
                - Sher Shah and Jahangir enacted laws safeguarding merchant wealth.
                - Property of merchants without heirs was preserved for public works (e.g., mosques, wells, bridges).
            - Though local officials sometimes harassed merchants, their property was generally safe.
        - Trade Infrastructure and Security
            - Road safety and transportation:
                - Sarais (inns) were built every 5 kos on major highways.
                - Insurance covered road safety risks.
                - The transport system was as developed as in Europe at the time.
            - Despite their wealth, traders had a low social status.
            - Merchants’ political influence:
                - Each merchant community had a leader (nagarseth) to negotiate with officials.
                - Merchants sometimes organized strikes (hartal) to push their demands.
                - Some Mughal nobles and royals, like Mir Jumla, were directly involved in trade.
                - However, the Mughals did not actively promote trade interests like European states (Britain, France, Holland).
        - Factors Behind Trade Expansion in the Seventeenth Century
            - Mughal political integration and law and order facilitated trade.
            - The Mughals developed roads and sarais for easy movement.
            - Taxes were levied only at empire entry points; illegal road tolls (rahdari) were discouraged.
            - Mughal rupees (silver coins of high purity) became the standard currency in India and abroad, aiding trade.
            - Commercialization of the economy:
                - Salaries of soldiers and administrative staff were paid in cash.
                - Under the zabti system, land revenue was collected in cash, boosting the money economy.
                - Around 20% of rural produce was marketed, leading to the rise of small towns (qasbas).
                - Demand for luxury goods fueled the expansion of handicraft production and urban centers.
        - Growth of Major Towns and Manufacturing
            - Sixteenth-century urbanization:
                - Agra and Fatehpur Sikri were larger than London (as per Ralph Fitch).
                - Lahore was among the largest cities in Europe or Asia (Monserrate).
                - Bernier stated that Delhi was comparable to Paris, and Agra was even bigger.
                - Other major towns: Ahmadnagar, Burhanpur, Multan, Patna, Rajmahal, and Dacca.
                - Ahmedabad was as large as London, while Patna had 2 lakh residents.
        - State Involvement in Trade and Industry
            - The Mughals concentrated rural wealth in the hands of the nobility, stimulating demand for luxury goods.
            - Government directly supported industries like:
                - Arms manufacturing (guns, cannons, armor).
                - Shipbuilding:
                    - Shah Jahan (1651) initiated a naval program for voyages to West Asia.
                    - By 1652, six ships were commissioned annually.
                - Indian shipyards adopted European models, reducing freight costs.
            - Indian steel swords were in high demand worldwide.

    - Foreign Trade and the European Traders
        - India’s Foreign Trade and Its Impact
            - India maintained extensive trade with Southeast and West Asia, supplying foodstuffs like sugar and rice.
            - Indian textiles were in high demand across Asia, with an English agent observing, “From Aden to Achin, from head to foot, everyone was clothed in Indian textiles.”
            - India primarily imported metals like tin and copper, certain spices, warhorses, and luxury items.
            - Due to its trade surplus, India received significant imports of gold and silver, prompting Bernier to remark that India was the “sink of gold and silver.”
        - Arrival of European Traders
            - The Portuguese arrived in India towards the end of the fifteenth century.
            - By the seventeenth century, other European traders, including the Dutch, English, and later the French, came to India due to economic expansion in Europe.
        - Decline of Portuguese Power and Rise of the Dutch
            - Portuguese influence weakened in the second half of the sixteenth century.
            - The Dutch established themselves in Masulipatam in 1606 and later dominated the spice trade in Java and Sumatra.
            - Indian textiles became the primary medium of exchange for spices, leading the Dutch to expand southward to the Coromandel Coast, where they set up operations at Pulicat.
        - Establishment of English Trade in India
            - The English initially sought spices but faced hostility from the Dutch, forcing them to focus on India.
            - In 1612, after defeating a Portuguese fleet, they established a factory at Surat, confirmed by a farman from Jahangir in 1618.
            - The English recognized Gujarat’s significance in textile exports and captured Ormuz (a Portuguese base) in 1622 with Persian help, securing access to the Persian Gulf.
            - By the early seventeenth century, both the Dutch and English had firmly established themselves in Indian trade, effectively ending Portuguese dominance.
        - Indian Traders and Their Resilience
            - Despite European dominance at sea, Indian traders retained a strong position in Asian trade.
            - Indian traders had better market knowledge and accepted lower profit margins (10–15%) than the Dutch (40–50%), making them competitive.
            - The Dutch and English depended on Mughal support and Indian traders to sustain their factories and operations.
            - European companies reduced costs by freighting Indian merchants’ goods on their ships, benefiting both parties.
            - Indian shipping expanded significantly, with the number of ships at Surat increasing from 50 in the mid-seventeenth century to 112 by the century’s end.
        - Expansion of European Trade Beyond Asia
            - European traders sought Indian exports for Europe, initially focusing on pepper and indigo, the best of which was produced at Sarkhej (Gujarat) and Bayana (Agra).
            - By the mid-seventeenth century, English traders developed a strong textile export trade to Europe.
            - Gujarat initially supplied the cloth, but increasing demand led to expansion into Agra and later the Coromandel Coast.
            - By 1660, cloth exports from Coromandel tripled those from Gujarat, with Masulipatam and Fort St. George (later Madras) becoming major ports.
            - The Dutch also entered this textile trade alongside the English.
        - Expansion into Bengal and Other Regions
            - The English explored Lahri Bandar (Indus River mouth) for Multan and Lahore’s produce, but Gujarat trade remained dominant.
            - They focused on Bengal and Orissa, despite disruptions from Portuguese and Magh pirates.
            - By 1650, the English had established themselves at Hooghly and Balasore, exporting raw silk, sugar, textiles, and saltpeter (used for gunpowder and as ship ballast).
            - By the end of the century, exports from eastern India equaled those from Coromandel.
        - Indian Textiles and European Demand
            - By the late seventeenth century, Indian textiles became highly popular in England.
            - In 1701, English authorities banned printed calicoes from Persia, China, and India due to domestic agitation, but the demand remained strong.
            - White calico exports surged from 9.14 lakh pieces in 1701 to 20 lakh pieces in 1719.
        - Intra-Asian Trade and Indian Overland Trade
            - While European trade with India grew, intra-Asian trade remained more profitable.
            - Only 14% of spices produced were exported to Europe, with most consumed in India and China.
            - Indian textile exports to West Asia and East Africa increased, while coffee from Yemen emerged as a new trade item.
            - Overland trade flourished through major centers like Lahore and Multan, with Indian traders settling across Iran, Central Asia, and even Russia.
            - However, overland trade declined with the collapse of the Safavid and Mughal empires.
        - Economic Consequences of Growing Foreign Trade
            - The influx of gold and silver led to rapid economic growth, but prices doubled in the first half of the seventeenth century.
            - The social impact of this price rise is unclear but likely weakened traditional village ties and made the nobility more materialistic.
        - European Attempts to Reduce Gold and Silver Exports
            - European nations sought to reduce gold and silver exports by monopolizing the spice trade and capturing Indian textile markets, but with limited success.
            - They turned to empire-building, with the Dutch controlling Java and Sumatra.
            - The English and French competed for control over India but could not succeed while India remained politically strong under the Mughals and later under capable provincial rulers.
            - European conquest became possible only when internal and external pressures weakened these Indian states.

- Cultural and Religious Developments
    - Cultural Development Under the Mughals
        - Mughal Period as a Second Classical Age
            - The Mughal era witnessed an outburst of cultural activity in architecture, painting, literature, and music.
            - Traditions established during this time set a norm and deeply influenced succeeding generations.
            - The period is often regarded as a “second classical age”, following the Gupta age in northern India.
        - Fusion of Indian and Turko-Iranian Traditions
            - Indian cultural traditions were amalgamated with the Turko-Iranian influences brought by the Mughals.
            - The Timurid court at Samarqand had been a major cultural center of West and Central Asia.
            - Babur was conscious of this cultural heritage, critical of many existing Indian cultural forms, and determined to set proper standards.
        - Foundation of Mughal Cultural Growth
            - The fourteenth and fifteenth centuries had already seen a rich cultural development across various regions of India.
            - The Mughals built upon this existing foundation, making their cultural achievements possible.
            - People from different regions, faiths, and races contributed to this cultural growth.
            - The resulting culture of the period exhibited a composite national character, blending diverse traditions.

    - Mughal Architecture
        - Distinctive Features of Mughal Architecture
            - The Mughals built forts, palaces, gates, mosques, baolis (step-wells), and public buildings.
            - Formal gardens with running water were a notable feature, even in palaces and pleasure resorts.
            - Mughal gardens, such as Nishat Bagh (Kashmir), Shalimar Bagh (Lahore), and Pinjore Garden (Punjab foothills), have survived to this day.
        - Pre-Mughal Contributions
            - Sher Shah gave a new impetus to architecture.
            - His mausoleum at Sasaram (Bihar) and mosque in the Old Fort (Delhi) are architectural marvels.
            - These structures mark the climax of pre-Mughal architecture and the beginning of the new Mughal style.
        - Akbar’s Architectural Innovations
            - Akbar, the first Mughal ruler with time and resources for large-scale construction, built a series of forts, the most famous being Agra Fort (red sandstone, massive gates).
            - Mughal forts drew upon the Indian tradition of fort-building seen at Gwalior and Jodhpur.
            - The climax of fort-building was Shah Jahan’s Red Fort in Delhi.
        - Fatehpur Sikri
            - In 1572, Akbar began a palace-cum-fort complex at Fatehpur Sikri, completed in eight years.
            - Built atop a hill with an artificial lake, it included buildings influenced by Gujarat and Bengal styles, with deep eaves, balconies, and ornate kiosks.
            - The Panch Mahal, designed for fresh air, used pillars similar to Indian temples.
            - The Gujarat style was prominent in a palace believed to be for Akbar’s Rajput wife or wives.
        - Persian and Central Asian Influences
            - Glazed blue tiles were used for wall decoration and roofing.
            - The Jama Masjid and its grand entrance, Buland Darwaza (“Lofty Gate”), were built to commemorate Akbar’s victory in Gujarat.
            - The half-dome portal style, borrowed from Iran, became a defining feature of later Mughal architecture.
        - Jahangir’s Contribution to Mughal Architecture
            - Builders shifted from sandstone to marble.
            - Pietra dura (floral designs with semi-precious stones) became prominent, especially under Shah Jahan.
        - Shah Jahan and the Climax of Mughal Architecture
            - The Taj Mahal, a masterpiece, incorporated all architectural elements developed by the Mughals.
            - Humayun’s Tomb, built in Delhi during Akbar’s reign, with its massive marble dome, is considered a precursor to the Taj Mahal.
            - The double-dome technique, allowing for a larger outer dome with a smaller inner dome, was an innovation of this period.
            - The Taj Mahal’s chief glory lies in its massive dome, four slender minarets, delicate marble screens, pietra dura work, and formal garden setting.
        - Mosque Architecture Under Shah Jahan
            - Moti Masjid (Agra Fort) – built entirely of marble, similar to the Taj Mahal.
            - Jama Masjid (Delhi) – built in red sandstone, featuring a lofty entrance gate, tall slender minarets, and a series of domes.
        - Mughal Architectural Influence Beyond Shah Jahan
            - Aurangzeb was more economy-minded and built fewer structures.
            - However, Mughal architectural traditions continued into the 18th and 19th centuries.
            - Provincial and local kingdoms adopted Mughal styles for their palaces and forts.
            - The Harmandir Sahib (Golden Temple, Amritsar) was rebuilt multiple times during this period, incorporating the arch-and-dome principle and other Mughal architectural elements.

    - Mughal Painting
        - Distinctive Contributions
            - The Mughals made a significant impact on painting by introducing new themes such as:
                - Court life
                - Battle scenes
                - The chase (hunting expeditions)
            - They added new colors and forms, creating a living artistic tradition that continued even after Mughal power declined.
        - Indian Traditions and Early Influences
            - India had a long tradition of painting, with the Ajanta wall paintings as early evidence of its richness.
            - Although painting declined after the 8th century, it survived through:
                - Palm-leaf manuscripts
                - Illustrated Jain texts (from the 13th century onward)
            - The Jains and some provincial kingdoms (such as Malwa and Gujarat) patronized painting in the 15th century.
            - A vigorous revival began only under Akbar.
        - Development Under Akbar
            - Humayun, while at the court of the Shah of Iran, brought two master painters to India.
            - Under Akbar’s rule:
                - A painting workshop was set up in the imperial palace (havbhanas).
                - A large number of painters, including Hindus and Muslims, were drawn from different regions and castes.
                - Notable painters of Akbar’s court: Daswant and Basawan.
            - Initially, Mughal painters illustrated Persian fables, but later moved to Indian themes by illustrating:
                - The Persian translation of the Mahabharata
                - Akbar Nama (his historical biography)
            - This transition freed Mughal art from Persian dominance.
            - Indian colors (such as peacock blue and Indian red) were introduced.
            - The flat Persian style was replaced with a more three-dimensional effect, achieved through the rounded Indian brush technique.
        - Climax Under Jahangir
            - Jahangir had a keen eye for art and further refined Mughal painting.
            - Special techniques developed under Jahangir:
                - Different artists worked on a single painting (e.g., one painted the face, another the body, another the feet).
                - Jahangir could identify individual artists’ contributions within a painting.
            - Major themes under Jahangir:
                - Portrait painting became highly fashionable.
                - Animals and nature were widely depicted, with Mansur excelling in this genre.
        - European Influence
            - Portuguese priests introduced European painting to the Mughal court under Akbar.
            - The most significant adoption was foreshortening, a technique that enhanced depth and perspective, making images more realistic.
        - Decline and Influence Beyond the Mughals
            - Under Shah Jahan, the tradition of Mughal painting continued but showed less innovation.
            - Aurangzeb’s lack of interest led to the dispersal of Mughal artists to different regions, influencing:
                - Rajasthani painting – blended Western Indian (Jain school) themes with Mughal styles.
                - Pahari painting – continued Mughal artistic traditions.
            - Themes in Rajasthani painting included:
                - Hunting and court scenes
                - Mythological subjects (e.g., Krishna and Radha)
                - Barahmasa (paintings of the seasons)
                - Ragas (melodies depicted in visual form)
            - The Mughal tradition was revived in the 18th century under Aurangzeb’s successors.

    - Language and Literature
        - Persian and Sanskrit as Administrative and Literary Languages
            - Persian and Sanskrit played an important role as languages of thought and administration at the all-India level.
            - The Bhakti Movement and regional rulers’ patronage contributed to the growth of regional languages.
            - By Akbar’s reign, Persian became so widespread in North India that he discontinued the practice of maintaining revenue records in both Persian and Hindavi.
            - However, Deccani states continued to maintain records in local languages until the late 17th century.
        - Persian Literature and Its Growth
            - Persian prose and poetry peaked during Akbar’s reign.
            - Key Persian writers:
