                - Abul Fazl (historian and stylist, set the prose-writing style for generations).
                - Faizi (Akbar’s court poet, supervised the Mahabharata translation).
                - Utbi and Naziri (Persian poets who migrated to India, contributing to Mughal court culture).
            - Many Persian scholars and poets came from Iran to India, making the Mughal court a major cultural hub of the Islamic world.
            - Hindus also contributed to Persian literature, writing historical works and dictionaries.
        - Sanskrit Literature and Its Centers
            - Sanskrit continued to flourish, with most works produced in South and East India under local rulers’ patronage.
            - Banaras remained the main center where scholars from across India gathered.
            - Some Brahmins employed in the Mughal translation department also contributed to Sanskrit literature.
        - Development of Regional Languages
            - Regional languages matured during this period, producing some of the finest lyrical poetry.
            - Popular themes in Bengali, Oriya, Hindi, Rajasthani, and Gujarati poetry:
                - Krishna’s dalliance with Radha.
                - Krishna’s childhood pranks.
                - Stories from the Bhagavata Purana.
            - Translations of Ramayana and Mahabharata into regional languages continued.
            - Hindus and Muslims both contributed to regional literature:
                - Alaol wrote in Bengali and translated works from Persian.
                - Malik Muhammad Jayasi’s Padmavat (written in Hindi) used Alauddin Khalji’s attack on Chittor as an allegory for Sufi philosophy.
        - Hindi Literature and Mughal Patronage
            - Medieval Hindi (Braj dialect), spoken around Agra, received Mughal patronage.
            - From Akbar’s reign onward, Hindi poets became attached to the Mughal court.
            - Prominent Hindi poets:
                - Abdur Rahim Khan-i-Khanan (blended Bhakti poetry with Persian influences).
                - Tulsidas (most influential Hindi poet, venerated Rama as both god and hero).
                    - Used Awadhi dialect (spoken in eastern Uttar Pradesh).
                    - Advocated a modified caste system based on individual qualities, not birth.
                    - Promoted family values and complete devotion to Rama as a path to salvation.
        - Development of Regional Literature in South India
            - Malayalam emerged as an independent literary language.
            - Marathi literature peaked under Eknath and Tukaram.
            - Eknath defended Marathi’s literary value, stating:
                “If Sanskrit was made by God, was Prakrit born of thieves and knaves? Let these errings be of vanity alone. God is no partisan of tongues. To Him, Prakrit and Sanskrit are alike. 
                - My language, Marathi, is worthy of expressing the highest sentiments and is rich, laden with the fruits of divine knowledge.”
            - Punjabi gained new life due to the writings of Sikh Gurus.

    - Music in Mughal period
        - Fusion of Hindu and Muslim Musical Traditions
            - Music was a cultural field where Hindus and Muslims cooperated.
            - Akbar was a great patron of music and supported Tansen of Gwalior, credited with composing many new ragas (melodies).
            - Jahangir and Shah Jahan continued Akbar’s tradition, and many Mughal nobles were also patrons of music.
        - Music Under Aurangzeb
            - Aurangzeb is often said to have banned music, but recent research shows:
                - He banned singing at the court but allowed musical instruments.
                - He was personally skilled in playing the veena.
                - Music thrived under the patronage of Aurangzeb’s queens, princes, and nobles.
                - The largest number of books on classical Indian music in Persian were written during his reign.
        - Music in the 18th Century
            - The greatest developments in Indian music came later during the reign of Muhammad Shah (1719–1748).
            - Muhammad Shah was a major patron of musicians, leading to significant musical advancements.

    - Religious Ideas, Beliefs, and Problems of Integration
        - The Bhakti Movement and Emerging Religious Trends
            - The Bhakti Movement continued to flourish during the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries. Among the significant developments were:
                - The Sikh Movement in Punjab, originating from Guru Nanak’s preachings.
                - Maharashtra Dharma, a movement centered in Maharashtra.
        - The Sikh Movement and Its Evolution
            - The growth of Sikhism was closely linked to the institution of Guruship:
                - The first four gurus followed a tradition of meditation and scholarship.
                - Guru Arjan (5th Guru) played a crucial role in compiling the Adi Granth (Granth Sahib), the Sikh scriptures.
                - Guru Arjan adopted an aristocratic lifestyle to emphasize the combination of spiritual and worldly leadership.
                - He also introduced Dasvandh, a system of collecting one-tenth of Sikh earnings for community welfare.
        - Sikh-Mughal Relations and Conflicts
            - Akbar respected the Sikh gurus and is believed to have visited them.
            - Jahangir’s Conflict with Guru Arjan:
                - Guru Arjan was imprisoned and executed for allegedly aiding Prince Khusrau.
            - Guru Hargobind’s Struggles:
                - Briefly imprisoned by Jahangir, later released.
                - Clashed with Shah Jahan over a hunting incident.
                - Further conflicts arose over:
                    - Building a new city near Jalandhar (objected to by Mughal authorities).
                    - Seizure of two prized horses by royal officials (later retrieved by Bidhi Chand, a Sikh follower).
                - He successfully defended himself in battles but later retired peacefully.
        - Religious Conflicts vs. Political Struggles
            - Historian R.S. Tripathi argues that these conflicts were more political than religious.
            - The rich lifestyle of the gurus and the title Sachcha Padshah (True Sovereign) did not trouble the Mughal rulers.
            - Similar practices were seen among Sufi saints, who also received grand titles from their followers.
        - Mughal Policies and Religious Tolerance
            - There was no systematic persecution of Hindus under Mughal rule.
            - Shah Jahan initially displayed orthodox tendencies, such as demolishing newly built temples, but later softened his stance under the influence of his liberal son Dara Shikoh.
            - Dara Shikoh:
                - Translated the Bhagavad Gita into Persian.
                - Wrote an anthology of the Vedas, emphasizing their harmony with Islam.
        - The Bhakti and Non-Sectarian Movements
            - Dadu (Gujarat-Rajasthan) preached a non-sectarian path (Nirpaksha), rejecting both Hindu and Muslim labels.
            - Tukaram (Maharashtra):
                - Supreme exponent of Bhakti.
                - Lived in Pandharpur, a center for Vithoba (Vishnu worship).
                - Though a Shudra by birth, he personally performed puja, challenging orthodox norms.
        - Orthodox Hindu Reactions
            - Orthodox Hindus opposed these liberal ideas.
            - Raghunandan (Bengal), a leading Dharmashastra writer, asserted:
                - Only Brahmins had the right to read and preach scriptures.
                - In Kali Yuga, only two varnas remained: Brahmins and Shudras.
            - Ramdas (Maharashtra), later Shivaji’s spiritual guru, also upheld Brahminical privileges.
        - Orthodox Muslim Resistance and Shaikh Ahmad Sirhindi
            - Sufism and religious harmony continued among Muslims, but some ulama opposed Akbar’s liberal policies.
            - Shaikh Ahmad Sirhindi, a Naqshbandi Sufi, led a revivalist movement:
                - Opposed pantheistic mysticism (Wahdat al-Wujud), calling it un-Islamic.
                - Denounced Hindu-influenced practices, such as:
                    - Music in religious gatherings (sama).
                    - Excessive meditation.
                    - Visiting saints’ tombs.
                - Advocated for:
                    - Reimposition of jizya (tax on non-Muslims).
                    - Minimal interaction between Hindus and Muslims.
            - Impact:
                - His ideas gained little traction.
                - Jahangir imprisoned him for claiming a higher spiritual status than the Prophet.
                - Aurangzeb also ignored his successors.
        - Liberalism vs. Orthodoxy: A Recurrent Struggle
            - The battle between entrenched privilege and egalitarianism was a recurring theme in Indian history.
            - Orthodox elements resisted reconciliation, obstructing Hindu-Muslim harmony.
            - The struggle culminated during Aurangzeb’s reign.
            - However, Akbar’s liberal policies were revived in the 18th century and became the norm once again.

- Climax and Disintegration of the Mughal Empire—I
    - Problems of Succession
        - Lack of a Clear Succession Tradition
            - The final years of Shah Jahan’s reign were marked by a bitter war of succession among his sons.
            - There was no clear tradition of succession among the Muslims or the Timurids.
            - Some Muslim political thinkers accepted the ruler’s right to nominate a successor, but this was rarely enforced.
            - The Timurid tradition of partitioning the empire had failed and was never implemented in India.
            - Hindu traditions were also unclear. According to Tulsidas, a ruler could choose any of his sons as his successor, but this often led to disputes.
            - For instance, Rajput ruler Sanga had to fight his brothers to secure his throne.
        - Shah Jahan’s Illness and Dara’s Nomination
            - In late 1657, Shah Jahan fell seriously ill in Delhi, leading to rumors of his death.
            - His sons—Shuja in Bengal, Murad in Gujarat, and Aurangzeb in the Deccan—believed or pretended to believe these rumors and prepared for war.
            - Hoping to avoid conflict, Shah Jahan nominated his eldest son, Dara, as his successor (tvadi-ahd).
            - Dara’s mansab was raised to 60,000, and nobles were instructed to obey him.
            - Instead of ensuring a smooth transition, this move confirmed the other princes’ suspicions of Shah Jahan’s bias toward Dara, intensifying their ambitions.
        - Military Campaigns and the Battle of Dharmat (15 April 1658)
            - As the princes advanced toward Agra, Shah Jahan dispatched forces to counter them:
                - Sulaiman Shikoh (Dara’s son) and Mirza Raja Jai Singh led an army to confront Shuja in the east.
                - Raja Jaswant Singh of Jodhpur led another army to Malwa against Aurangzeb and Murad.
            - In Malwa, Aurangzeb and Murad invited Jaswant to step aside, but he chose to fight.
            - The Battle of Dharmat (15 April 1658) ended in Aurangzeb’s victory, boosting his confidence and demoralizing Dara’s allies.
        - Dara’s Strategic Mistakes
            - Overconfident, Dara had sent some of his best troops to fight Shuja, weakening Agra’s defense.
            - Sulaiman Shikoh defeated Shuja near Banaras (February 1658) and pursued him into Bihar.
            - After Dharmat, Dara recalled these troops, but they were too far to return in time.
        - Search for Allies and the Battle of Samugarh (29 May 1658)
            - Dara sought alliances with Rajput rulers Jaswant Singh and the Rana of Udaipur, but Aurangzeb won them over with promises of rank and religious freedom.
            - The Battle of Samugarh (29 May 1658) was a decisive contest of military skill.
            - Both armies had around 50,000 to 60,000 troops, but Aurangzeb’s forces were better trained and led.
            - Dara’s hastily assembled army, dependent on the Hada Rajputs and Saiyids of Barha, could not match Aurangzeb’s seasoned warriors.
        - Aurangzeb’s Capture of Agra and Shah Jahan’s Imprisonment
            - Aurangzeb justified his campaign as an effort to “free” Shah Jahan from the “heretical” Dara.
            - However, the war was not a conflict between religious orthodoxy and liberalism—both Hindu and Muslim nobles were divided in their loyalties.
            - After Dara’s defeat, Shah Jahan was besieged in the Agra Fort. Aurangzeb forced his surrender by cutting off the fort’s water supply.
            - Shah Jahan was confined to the female apartments of the fort but was not mistreated.
            - His daughter Jahanara Begum voluntarily stayed with him and cared for him until his death.
            - After Shah Jahan’s death, Aurangzeb honored Jahanara, increasing her pension from 12 lakh to 17 lakh rupees.
        - Elimination of Rivals
            - Aurangzeb had agreed to divide the empire with Murad, but he betrayed him, imprisoned him, and had him executed two years later.
            - Dara’s Final Struggles and Death
                - After Samugarh, Dara fled to Lahore but abandoned it upon Aurangzeb’s arrival.
                - He fled to Sindh, then Gujarat, and finally Ajmer, where he sought Jaswant Singh’s help.
                - Jaswant betrayed him, leading to the Battle of Deorai (March 1659)—Dara’s last stand.
                - Captured by a treacherous Afghan chief, Dara was handed over to Aurangzeb.
                - A jury of scholars ruled that Dara must be executed “to protect the faith and the state.”
                - Two years later, Dara’s son, Sulaiman Shikoh, was handed over by the ruler of Garhwal and met the same fate.
            - Defeat of Shuja and His Death
                - Aurangzeb defeated Shuja at Khajwah (December 1658) and entrusted the campaign against him to Mir Jumla.
                - Shuja fled to Arakan (April 1660), where he and his family were killed by the Arakanese on charges of rebellion.
        - Aftermath and Aurangzeb’s Rule
            - The civil war, lasting over two years, demonstrated that:
                - Nomination by the ruler or partition of the empire was never accepted.
                - Military strength was the only way to secure the throne.
                - Each successive war of succession became more destructive.
            - After consolidating power, Aurangzeb sought to mend relations with the families of his defeated brothers:
                - 1673: Dara’s son Siphir Shikoh was released, given a mansab, and married to Aurangzeb’s daughter.
                - Murad’s son, Izzat Bakhsh, was also freed and given a mansab.
                - 1669: Dara’s daughter, Jani Begum, was married to Aurangzeb’s son Muhammad Azam.
                - Several marriages between Aurangzeb’s family and the descendants of his defeated brothers helped unite the dynasty in the third generation.

    - Aurangzeb’s Reign and Religious Policy
        - Aurangzeb’s Rule and Governance
            - Aurangzeb ruled for nearly 50 years, during which the Mughal Empire reached its territorial zenith, spanning from Kashmir in the north to Jinji in the south, and from the Hindukush in the west to Chittagong in the east.
            - He was a hardworking ruler, paying close attention to all affairs of the state, as reflected in his letters.
            - A strict disciplinarian, he did not spare even his sons—Prince Muazzam was imprisoned for 12 years for alleged intrigue with the ruler of Golconda.
            - Unlike his predecessors, he lived simply and rejected ostentation. He was considered an orthodox, God-fearing Muslim and came to be regarded as a zinda pir (a living saint).
            - Aurangzeb’s Early Rule and Consolidation Efforts
                - Reassertion of Imperial Authority
                    - Aurangzeb’s primary focus after ascending the throne was consolidating Mughal authority rather than aggressive territorial expansion.
                    - His initial efforts were directed at recovering areas lost during the war of succession and those to which the Mughals had a legal claim.
                - Local Resistance and Rebellions
                    - During the war of succession, many local zamindars and rajas:
                        - Withheld revenue from the Mughal administration.
                        - Plundered neighboring regions, including Mughal territories and royal highways.
                    - To reestablish control, Aurangzeb took military action against several rebellious rulers.
                - Policy on Conquest and Annexation
                    - Non-annexation approach:
                        - Sent an army to Bikaner to enforce obedience but did not annex it.
                        - Bundela chief Champat Rai, who had initially allied with Aurangzeb but later turned to plundering, was relentlessly pursued, but Bundela lands were not annexed.
                    - Selective annexation:
                        - In cases where rulers were accused of disloyalty, such as Palamau in Bihar, their territories were annexed.
                    - Territorial Expansion
                        - While Aurangzeb generally did not follow a forward policy, the imperial frontier expanded in:
                            - The northeast.
                            - The Deccan.
        - Historiographical Debates on Aurangzeb
            - Historians are divided on his rule:
                - Some believe he reversed Akbar’s policy of religious tolerance, leading to Hindu discontent and uprisings that weakened the empire.
                - Others argue that his predecessors’ laxity had already caused Hindu disloyalty, forcing Aurangzeb to adopt harsh measures to rally Muslim support.
            - Modern research places his policies in the context of social, economic, and institutional factors.
        - Aurangzeb’s Religious Beliefs and Policies
            - Aurangzeb followed the Hanafi school of Islamic law but issued zawabit (secular decrees) to adapt to India’s conditions.
            - His religious policies had to account for the fact that the majority of the Indian population was Hindu.
            - His early regulations included:
                - Prohibiting the Kalma from being inscribed on coins to prevent desecration.
                - Discontinuing Nauroz, a festival linked to Zoroastrian traditions.
                - Appointing muhtasibs (moral enforcers) to ensure public adherence to the sharia.
                - Banning public consumption of intoxicants and gambling.
                - Ensuring proper trade practices by regulating weights and measures.
        - Puritanical Measures and Courtly Restrictions
            - In 1669, he imposed further religious restrictions:
                - Banned singing in court, though instrumental music and the royal band (naubat) continued.
                - Court musicians were pensioned off, yet classical Indian music thrived under Persian scholars, and Aurangzeb himself played the veena.
                - Abolished jharokha darshan, considering it a superstitious practice.
                - Prohibited the weighing ceremony of the emperor against gold and silver.
                - Discouraged astrology, but his ban on almanacs was ignored even by his family.
                - Introduced austerity measures, such as:
                    - Simplifying court decor.
                    - Replacing silver inkstands with porcelain ones.
                    - Replacing gold railings in the Diwan-i-Am with lapis lazuli.
                    - Discontinuing the official department of history-writing to cut costs.
        - Economic and Administrative Policies Favoring Muslims
            - Initially exempted Muslim traders from taxes to boost their commerce but reinstated them after finding abuses in the system.
            - Attempted to reserve peshkar and karori posts (petty revenue officials) for Muslims but faced resistance and had to relax the rule.
        - Temple Policy and Jizyah
            - Temple Regulations:
                - At the beginning of his reign, he reaffirmed that old temples should not be demolished, but new ones should not be built.
                - Old temples could be repaired, as stated in farmans issued to temples in Banaras and Vrindavan.
                - However, officials had discretion in interpreting “long-standing temples,” leading to selective enforcement.
            - Destruction of Temples as a Political Tool:
                - As governor of Gujarat, he had ordered temple destruction, and upon becoming emperor, he reiterated these orders in 1665.
                - In 1669, learning that Hindus and Muslims studied together in Banaras, Multan, and Thatta temples, he ordered their destruction.
                - Prominent temples destroyed included:
                    - Keshava Rai Temple (Mathura)
                    - Vishwanath Temple (Banaras)
                    - Somnath Temple
                - Temple destruction was used as punishment for rebellion, such as during wars with the Rathors of Marwar and the Rana of Udaipur (1679–80).
                - Despite this, grants to some Hindu temples and religious institutions continued.
            - Reintroduction of Jizyah (1679)
                - Jizyah was a poll tax on non-Muslims, abolished by Akbar but reinstated by Aurangzeb under pressure from theologians.
                - Contrary to some claims, he did not aim to convert India into Dar-ul-Islam, as Islamic law already recognized the Mughal state as such.
                - His religious convictions and political ambitions often conflicted, leading to contradictory policies.
        - Conclusion
            - Aurangzeb’s religious policies were deeply influenced by his personal beliefs, but political considerations also played a significant role.
            - His strict adherence to Islamic law and intolerance of perceived un-Islamic practices alienated large sections of society.
            - His temple destruction policies and re-imposition of jizyah created resentment among Hindus and weakened the empire’s internal stability.
            - Despite his administrative competence and military achievements, his religious intolerance undermined Mughal unity, contributing to the empire’s eventual decline.

    
    - Mughal Conflicts and Expansion in the Northeast and Bengal
        - Conflict with the Ahoms and Expansion in Assam
            - The Ahoms controlled the Assam Valley and were engaged in conflicts with:
                - The rulers of Kamata (Kamrup)
                - The Afghan rulers of Bengal
            - By the late 15th century, the Kingdom of Kamata declined and was replaced by the Kuch (Cooch Bihar) kingdom, which controlled North Bengal and Western Assam.
            - The Kuch kingdom continued its conflicts with the Ahoms but later split due to internal disputes in the early 17th century, leading to Mughal intervention.
        - Mughal Entry into Assam (1612–1638)
            - The Mughals, at the request of the Kuch ruler, defeated a breakaway faction and occupied Western Assam (up to the Bar Nadi) in 1612.
            - The Kuch ruler became a Mughal vassal, bringing them into direct contact with the Ahoms.
            - A long war followed, ending in a 1638 treaty, which:
                - Fixed Bar Nadi as the boundary between the Ahoms and the Mughals.
                - Brought Guwahati under Mughal control.
        - Aurangzeb’s Assam Campaign (1662–1663)
            - The Ahoms attempted to expel the Mughals from Guwahati and surrounding areas.
            - Mir Jumla, Aurangzeb’s governor of Bengal, launched a campaign to annex Assam and Cooch Bihar:
                - 1662: First, he attacked Cooch Bihar, annexing it to the Mughal Empire.
                - 1663: He invaded the Ahom kingdom, captured Garhgaon, and held it for six months.
                - The Ahoms were forced into a humiliating treaty, which required them to:
                    - Send their princess to the Mughal harem.
                    - Pay a heavy war indemnity.
                    - Provide 20 elephants annually as tribute.
            - The Mughal boundary extended from Bar Nadi to the Bharali River.
        - Ahom Resistance and Mughal Retreat (1667–1680s)
            - Mir Jumla died soon after his victory, and the Mughal hold on Assam weakened.
            - 1667: The Ahoms resumed their struggle, recovering lost territories and recapturing Guwahati.
            - The Mughals also lost control of Cooch Bihar.
            - A prolonged Mughal-Ahom war continued for over 15 years.
            - Raja Ram Singh of Amber, leading the Mughal forces, lacked the necessary resources.
            - Ultimately, the Mughals abandoned Guwahati, retreating further west.
        - Impact of the Assam War
            - Exposed the limits of Mughal power in distant regions.
            - Highlighted Ahom military skill, especially in guerrilla warfare.
            - The prolonged Mughal invasion weakened the Ahom monarchy, eventually leading to internal decline and fragmentation.
        - Mughal Expansion in Bengal and Orissa
            - Shaista Khan’s Campaign in Bengal (1664–1666)
                - Mir Jumla’s aggressive policy was modified by Shaista Khan, who:
                    - Secured a settlement with Cooch Bihar.
                    - Focused on eliminating the Arakanese (Magh) pirates, who had terrorized Bengal from Chittagong.
            - Arakanese (Magh) Pirates and the Chittagong Campaign
                - The Arakanese pirates disrupted trade and depopulated areas up to Dacca.
                - Shaista Khan built a Mughal navy and:
                    - Captured Sondip Island as a base.
                    - Won over Portuguese mercenaries (Firingis) with financial incentives.
                    - Defeated the Arakanese navy near Chittagong, capturing many ships.
                    - 1666: Chittagong was stormed and captured.
            - Effects of the Chittagong Victory
                - Ended Arakanese piracy, allowing:
                    - Expansion of trade and commerce.
                    - Revival of agriculture in Eastern Bengal.
            - Mughal Control Over Orissa
                - The rebellion of Afghan Pathans in Orissa was crushed.
                - Balasore was reopened for commerce.

    - Aurangzeb’s Internal Challenges: Regional and Political Movements
        - Introduction
            - Aurangzeb faced multiple political challenges within the empire, including:
                - The Marathas in the Deccan – fighting for local independence.
                - The Jats and Rajputs in North India – with Rajput conflicts centered on succession and Jat uprisings having a peasant-agrarian background.
                - The Afghans and Sikhs in the Northwest – with the Afghan struggle being tribal and the Sikh movement being deeply influenced by religion.
            - Nature of the Conflicts
                - Rajputs: Primarily a succession dispute.
                - Marathas: Fought for local autonomy.
                - Jats: Uprising had economic and agrarian roots.
                - Sikhs: Religion played a dominant role in their movement.
                - Afghans: Their struggle was tribal in nature but also carried aspirations for a separate Afghan state.
            - Key Factors Behind These Movements
                - Economic and social factors played a significant role.
                - Regional independence remained a powerful sentiment.
                - Religion also had an undeniable influence.
                - While some argue that most of these movements (except the Afghan one) were a Hindu reaction to Aurangzeb’s religious policies, this perspective is debated.
                - Any resistance against the Muslim central government could be perceived as a challenge to Islam.
                - Rebel leaders often used religious slogans and symbols to gain wider support.
            - Thus, these movements were shaped by a mix of economic, social, regional, and religious factors rather than being purely religious conflicts.
        - Jat Rebellions Against the Mughal Government
            - Early Conflicts with the Mughals
                - The Jats of the Agra-Delhi region, primarily peasant cultivators, were the first to come into conflict with the Mughal government.
                - Though mostly peasants, some Jats were zamindars and had a strong sense of brotherhood and justice.
                - Conflicts with the Mughals dated back to Jahangir and Shah Jahan’s reigns, mainly over land revenue collection.
                - The imperial road to the Deccan and western seaports passed through Jat territory, making their revolts a significant concern for the Mughals.
            - Jat Revolt of 1669
                - Led by Gokla, a local zamindar from Mathura, the Jats launched a rebellion.
                - The movement spread quickly among peasants, growing to 20,000 fighters.
                - Aurangzeb personally marched from Delhi to suppress the uprising.
                - Despite their large numbers, the Jats were defeated, Gokla was captured and executed.
                - The revolt was not completely crushed, and discontent continued.
            - Satnami Revolt of 1672
                - Another armed conflict arose in Narnaul, near Mathura, involving the Satnamis, a religious sect.
                - The Satnamis were mostly peasants, artisans, and lower-caste people, rejecting caste distinctions and following a strict code of conduct.
                - What started as a minor clash with a local official escalated into a rebellion.
                - Aurangzeb personally led the campaign against them.
                - Interestingly, local Hindu zamindars, including Rajputs, sided with the Mughals against the Satnamis.
            - Jat Revolt of 1685
                - Under Rajaram, the Jats revolted again, this time with better organization and guerrilla tactics.
                - They combined plundering with warfare, making the Delhi-Agra road unsafe.
                - Aurangzeb enlisted Raja Bishan Singh of the Kachhwaha dynasty to suppress the uprising.
                - Bishan Singh was appointed faujdar of Mathura, and the entire region was granted to him as zamindari.
                - The conflict was further complicated by disputes between Jat primary zamindars (land-owning cultivators) and Rajput intermediary zamindars (revenue collectors).
            - Rise of the Jat State
                - By 1691, Rajaram and his successor Churaman were forced to submit.
                - However, Jat resistance and plundering activities continued.
                - In the 18th century, taking advantage of Mughal civil wars and declining central authority, Churaman carved out a separate Jat principality, ousting the Rajput zamindars.
                - What began as a peasant uprising gradually transformed into an independent Jat-ruled state.
        - Afghan Resistance Against Aurangzeb
            - Historical Background
                - Conflict with the Afghans was not new for the Mughals.
                - Akbar had to fight the Afghan tribes, during which his close friend Raja Birbal lost his life.
                - Shah Jahan also faced conflicts with the Afghan tribesmen.
                - These conflicts were driven by economic, political, and religious factors:
                    - The rugged mountainous terrain between Punjab and Kabul offered little livelihood.
                    - The Afghans relied on plundering caravans or joining the Mughal army.
                    - Their love for freedom made Mughal service difficult.
                    - The Mughals kept them content by paying subsidies, but unrest grew when the population increased or an ambitious leader emerged.
            - First Afghan Revolt (1667-1671)
                - A new stirring among the Pathans began under Bhagu, leader of the Yusufzai tribe.
                - Bhagu proclaimed Muhammad Shah as king and declared himself wazir, signaling the Afghan desire for a separate state.
                - A religious revivalist movement, Raushanai, emphasizing strict ethics and devotion to a pir, gave moral strength to the movement.
                - The rebellion spread, with Hazara, Attock, and Peshawar districts being plundered, bringing traffic through the Khyber Pass to a halt.
                - Aurangzeb sent Amir Khan, the chief bakhshi, with a Rajput contingent to suppress the revolt.
                - After several hard-fought battles, the resistance was broken.
                - To maintain control, Maharaja Jaswant Singh of Marwar was appointed thanedar of Jamrud in 1671.
            - Second Afghan Revolt (1672-1675)
                - In 1672, a second major Afghan uprising took place under Afridi chief Akmal Khan.
                - Akmal Khan proclaimed himself king, read the khutba, and struck coins in his name, calling all Afghans to war against the Mughals.
                - His army, described as “more numerous than ants and locusts,” blocked the Khyber Pass.
                - Amir Khan led an advance to clear the pass but was ambushed and disastrously defeated in a narrow defile.
                    - He barely escaped, but 10,000 Mughal soldiers perished, and goods worth two crores were looted.
                - Encouraged by this, other Afghan leaders, including Khushhal Khan Khattak, a sworn enemy of Aurangzeb, joined the resistance.
                - In 1674, another Mughal noble, Shujaat Khan, was defeated but rescued by a Rathore force sent by Jaswant Singh.
            - Aurangzeb’s Direct Intervention (1674-1675)
                - In mid-1674, Aurangzeb himself marched to Peshawar to restore order.
                - Through a combination of force and diplomacy, the Afghan resistance was broken.
                - By the end of 1675, peace was gradually restored.
            - Significance of the Afghan Uprising
                - The Afghan resistance showed that Mughal opposition was not limited to Hindus like the Jats and Marathas.
                - The uprising diverted Mughal attention from the Deccan, helping Shivaji:
                    - It reduced pressure on Shivaji at a crucial time.
                    - It delayed Mughal expansion in the Deccan until 1676, by which time Shivaji had:
                        - Crowned himself.
                        - Allied with Bijapur and Golconda.
        - Conflict Between the Sikh Gurus and the Mughals under Aurangzeb
            - Early Relations and Initial Clashes
                - Though there were some clashes between the Sikh Gurus and the Mughals under Shah Jahan, no significant conflict occurred between the Sikhs and Aurangzeb until 1675.
                - Aurangzeb recognized the growing influence of the Sikhs and attempted to engage Ram Rai, the elder son of Guru Har Rai, at his court.
                - However, Guru Har Rai disapproved of Ram Rai and instead nominated his younger son, Guru Har Kishan, as his successor, despite his young age.
                - Guru Har Kishan died soon after, and in 1664, Guru Tegh Bahadur became the next Sikh Guru.
            - Intrigues Against Guru Tegh Bahadur
                - Ram Rai contested the succession, both before and after Guru Har Kishan’s death.
                - Aurangzeb did not interfere but granted land in Dehra Dun to Ram Rai for a gurudwara.
                - Ram Rai, however, stayed in Delhi and actively worked against Guru Tegh Bahadur, poisoning the emperor’s mind against him.
                - To escape Ram Rai’s schemes, Guru Tegh Bahadur moved to Bihar and later served alongside Raja Ram Singh of Amber in Assam until 1671.
            - Execution of Guru Tegh Bahadur (1675)
                - In 1675, Guru Tegh Bahadur and five followers were arrested and taken to Delhi.
                - He was accused of various charges and asked to renounce his faith, which he refused.
                - As a result, he was beheaded.
            - Reasons Behind Aurangzeb’s Actions
                - Defense of Hindu Faith: 
                    - According to Guru Gobind Singh’s poetic account, Guru Tegh Bahadur sacrificed his life to protect Kashmiri Brahmins from forced conversion. However, historical records of this event are lacking.
                - Opposition to Mughal Oppression: 
                    - Some traditions claim he protested against the Mughal governor of Kashmir, but evidence of large-scale forced conversions is scarce.
                - Religious Rivalry: 
                    - Another view suggests that Ram Rai influenced Aurangzeb to test the Guru by demanding a miracle, and his refusal led to execution. However, Aurangzeb was away from Delhi at the time.
                - Political Concerns: 
                    - Persian sources allege that the Guru had a large following and collected money from villagers, causing the Mughal administration to perceive him as a threat. However, these accounts are questionable.
            - Impact of Guru Tegh Bahadur’s Execution
                - The event forced the Sikhs to retreat to the Punjab hills and transformed the Sikh movement into a military brotherhood.
                - Guru Gobind Singh played a crucial role in this transformation.
            - Rise of Guru Gobind Singh and the Formation of the Khalsa
                - Early Years and Conflict with Hill Rajas
                    - Guru Gobind Singh established his headquarters at Makhowal (Anandpur) in Punjab’s foothills.
                    - Initially, Hindu hill rajas tried to use the Sikhs in their internal conflicts, but as Guru Gobind Singh’s power grew, tensions escalated.
                    - By 1704, a coalition of hill rajas attacked Anandpur, but they failed to defeat him and sought Mughal intervention.
                - Mughal-Sikh Conflict
                    - The conflict was not purely religious but involved political rivalries between the Sikh movement and the hill rajas.
                    - Aurangzeb, concerned about the Guru’s rising influence, instructed Mughal officials to take action.
                    - Mughal forces, led by Wazir Khan, besieged Anandpur. After prolonged resistance, Guru Gobind Singh negotiated for safe passage, but Wazir Khan’s troops betrayed him and attacked while crossing a river.
                    - Two of the Guru’s sons were captured and executed after refusing to convert to Islam, while two more died in battle.
                - Later Years of Guru Gobind Singh
                    - Guru Gobind Singh withdrew to Talwandi and was left largely undisturbed.
                    - It remains unclear if Wazir Khan acted on Aurangzeb’s direct orders in executing the Guru’s sons.
                    - Aurangzeb later wrote to the governor of Lahore, urging reconciliation with the Guru.
                    - In 1706, Guru Gobind Singh wrote to Aurangzeb, explaining the events, and the emperor invited him to meet in the Deccan.
                    - The Guru set out, but Aurangzeb died before their meeting.
                - Legacy of Guru Gobind Singh
                    - Though he could not establish a Sikh state, he laid the foundation for its future realization.
                    - In 1699, he founded the Khalsa, a militarized Sikh order, demonstrating how a religious movement could evolve into a political and military force.

        - Mughal-Rajput Relations and Rebellions
            - Mughal-Rajput Relations Under Jahangir and Shah Jahan
                - Jahangir’s Policy Towards Mewar
                    - In 1613, Jahangir settled the long-standing conflict with Mewar.
                    - He continued Akbar’s policy of granting favors to leading Rajput rajas and forging matrimonial alliances with them.
                - Shah Jahan’s Rajput Alliance
                    - Shah Jahan maintained close ties with the Rajputs, and their contingents served with distinction in the Deccan, Balkh (Central Asia), and Qandhar.
                    - However, no Rajput raja was appointed governor of a province, and no further matrimonial alliances were made with leading Rajput rulers, despite Shah Jahan being the son of a Rathor princess.
                    - The consolidation of the Rajput alliance likely led to the belief that further matrimonial ties were unnecessary.
                    - Shah Jahan continued to honor the heads of the two leading Rajput houses, Jodhpur and Amber.
                    - Raja Jaswant Singh of Marwar was highly favored and, along with Jai Singh, held a mansab of 7000/7000 at the time of Aurangzeb’s accession.
            - Aurangzeb’s Early Rajput Policy
                - Efforts to Maintain Rajput Loyalty
                    - Aurangzeb valued the Rajput alliance and sought to secure Mewar’s support by raising the Maharana’s mansab from 5000/5000 to 6000/6000.
                    - Despite Jaswant Singh’s earlier opposition—fighting against Aurangzeb at Dharmat, defecting during the campaign against Shuja, and inviting Dara to Marwar—Aurangzeb pardoned him, restored his previous mansab, and entrusted him with important commands, including the governorship of Gujarat.
                    - Jai Singh remained a close ally and confidant of Aurangzeb until his death in 1667.
            - Crisis in Marwar (1678-1679)
                - Jaswant Singh’s Death and Succession Dispute
                    - Jaswant Singh died in 1678 while handling Afghan affairs in the northwest, leaving no surviving male heir at the time.
                    - The Mughal tradition dictated that in cases of disputed succession, the state would be placed under khalisa (Mughal administration) until a successor was determined.
                    - A similar precedent had been set in 1650, when Shah Jahan took Jaisalmer under khalisa before appointing a successor.
                - Mughal Justification for Annexing Marwar
                    - Jaswant Singh, like many Mughal nobles, owed large sums to the Mughal treasury.
                    - Several Rajputs, whom he had offended or whose territories had been granted to him as jagirs, saw his death as an opportunity to create disturbances.
                    - Anticipating resistance, Aurangzeb allotted two parganas in Marwar for Jaswant Singh’s family and supporters.
                    - He assembled a strong army and marched to Ajmer to enforce his authority.
                - Mughal Occupation and Resistance
                    - Rani Hadi, Jaswant Singh’s chief queen, objected to handing over Jodhpur to the Mughals, as it was the watan (homeland) of the Rathors, but she was forced to submit.
                    - Mughal officials were deployed throughout Marwar, and a thorough search was conducted for Jaswant Singh’s hidden treasures.
                    - Large numbers of temples, including old ones, were demolished or sealed, and the Mughals treated Marwar as hostile territory.
            - Aurangzeb’s Decision and Rathor Rebellion
                - Aurangzeb’s Choice of Inder Singh
                    - In Lahore, two of Jaswant Singh’s queens gave birth to sons after his death, strengthening their claim to the throne.
                    - However, Aurangzeb awarded the sikka (throne) of Jodhpur to Inder Singh, grandson of Jaswant Singh’s elder brother Amar Singh, in exchange for a succession fee of 36 lakh rupees.
                    - Aurangzeb may have believed that Shah Jahan had been unjust in passing over Amar Singh in favor of Jaswant Singh.
                    - Some historians claim that Aurangzeb offered the throne to Ajit Singh (Jaswant Singh’s posthumous son) on the condition that he convert to Islam, but contemporary sources provide no evidence for this.
                    - The Hukumat-ni-Bahi, a contemporary Rajasthani record, states that Aurangzeb offered Ajit Singh a mansab and allotted him two parganas in Marwar as a jagir.
                - Rejection of the Mughal Decision
                    - The Rathor sardars, led by Durgadas, rejected Aurangzeb’s compromise as detrimental to Marwar’s interests.
                    - When Aurangzeb ordered the confinement of the Rathor princes and their mothers in the fort of Nurgarh, the Rathors staged a daring escape.
                    - One of the princes was successfully smuggled out of Agra and crowned Ajit Singh at Jodhpur amidst great celebration.
                - Aurangzeb’s Response
                    - Recognizing that Inder Singh had little support, Aurangzeb set him aside for incompetence but refused to recognize Ajit Singh.
                    - Branding Ajit Singh a “pretender,” he summoned forces from across the empire and marched to Ajmer once again.
                    - The Rathor resistance was crushed, and Jodhpur was occupied.
                    - Durgadas fled with Ajit Singh to Mewar, where Rana Raj Singh provided him with a secret hideout.
            - Mewar’s Involvement in the War (1679-1681)
                - Mewar Joins the Conflict
                    - Rana Raj Singh, initially an ally of Aurangzeb, turned against him due to:
                        - Mughal interference in Rajput successions
                        - Mughal efforts to detach vassal states like Dungarpur and Banswara from Mewar
                        - The military occupation of Marwar and rejection of Ajit Singh’s claim
                    - He sent 5,000 troops under a trusted commander to support Rani Hadi’s resistance in Jodhpur.
                - Aurangzeb’s Campaign Against Mewar
                    - In November 1679, Aurangzeb launched an invasion of Mewar.
                    - His forces raided Udaipur and attacked the Rana’s camp.
                    - The Rana retreated into the hills, adopting guerrilla tactics, which the Mughals struggled to counter.
                    - The war became highly unpopular, and Aurangzeb’s repeated admonitions had little effect on his commanders.
            - Prince Akbar’s Rebellion (1681) and Aftermath
                - Rebellion of Prince Akbar
                    - Aurangzeb’s son, Prince Akbar, attempted to exploit Rajput discontent and rebelled against his father.
                    - In January 1681, he allied with Rathor chief Durgadas and marched on Ajmer, where Aurangzeb was vulnerable.
                    - However, delays and Aurangzeb’s counter-tactics caused dissension in Akbar’s camp, forcing him to flee to Maharashtra.
                - Settlement with Mewar and Marwar
                    - With Akbar’s rebellion defeated, Mewar became a secondary concern for Aurangzeb.
                    - In a treaty with Rana Jagat Singh (Rana Raj Singh’s successor):
                        - Mewar surrendered some parganas in lieu of jizyah.
                        - The Rana received a mansab of 5000 and pledged loyalty to the Mughals.
                        - Aurangzeb promised that Ajit Singh would receive a mansab and Marwar upon reaching adulthood.
                    - This agreement satisfied neither the Rajputs nor the Mughals.
                - Final Resolution
                    - The Mughals retained control over Marwar, and sporadic conflict continued.
                    - In 1698, Ajit Singh was finally recognized as Marwar’s ruler, but the Mughals refused to relinquish Jodhpur.
                    - Tensions persisted until Aurangzeb’s death in 1707.
            - Conclusion: The Failure of Aurangzeb’s Rajput Policy
                - Aurangzeb’s handling of Marwar and Mewar was rigid and counterproductive.
                - It weakened the Mughal-Rajput alliance, damaged Mughal military prestige, and exposed his inflexible nature.
                - While it did not indicate a broader anti-Hindu agenda—given the rise of Maratha nobility—it contributed to the empire’s decline.
                - However, the real struggle for the Mughal Empire lay in the Deccan campaigns.


- Climax and Disintegration of the Mughal Empire—II
    - Rise of the Marathas in the Deccan
        - Marathas in the Administrative and Military Systems
            - The Marathas held important positions in the administrative and military systems of Ahmadnagar and Bijapur.
            - Their power and influence in governmental affairs increased as the Mughals advanced into the Deccan.
            - Both the Deccani sultans and the Mughals sought their support.
            - Malik Ambar extensively used Marathas as loose auxiliaries in his army.
        - Influence of Maratha Families
            - Several influential Maratha families exercised local authority in certain areas, including:
                - The Mores
                - The Ghatages
                - The Nimbalkars
            - Unlike the Rajputs, the Marathas did not have large, well-established states.
        - Shahji Bhonsale and Early Maratha State-Building
            - The credit for establishing a large Maratha state goes to Shahji Bhonsale and his son, Shivaji.
            - Shahji acted as a kingmaker in Ahmadnagar for some time and defied the Mughals.
            - However, after the Treaty of 1636, Shahji:
                - Yielded the territories he controlled.
                - Joined the service of Bijapur.
                - Shifted his focus to Karnataka.
        - Attempts to Establish Independent Principalities
            - Taking advantage of unsettled conditions, Shahji attempted to establish a semi-independent principality at Bangalore.
            - This was similar to how:
                - Mir Jumla, the leading noble of Golconda, sought to carve out a principality on the Coromandel coast.
                - The Abyssinian chiefs (Sidis) on the western coast acted in a similar manner.
        - Prelude to Shivaji’s Rise
            - These developments set the stage for Shivaji’s attempt to carve out a large principality around Poona.

    - Shivaji’s Rise and Early Conquests
        - Inheritance and Early Military Exploits (1645–1647)
            - Shahji left the Poona jagir to his neglected senior wife, Jija Bai, and his minor son, Shivaji.
            - At just 18 years old, Shivaji demonstrated his military prowess by capturing several hill forts near Poona:
                - Rajgarh
                - Kondana
                - Torna
            - In 1647, after the death of his guardian, Dadaji Kondadeo, Shivaji became his own master, gaining full control of his father’s jagir.
        - Conquest of Javli and Expansion in the Mavala Region (1656)
            - Shivaji’s true career of conquest began in 1656 when he defeated the Maratha chief Chandra Rao More and conquered Javli.
            - The Javli kingdom and the accumulated treasure of the Mores were significant, and Shivaji acquired them through treachery.
            - With the conquest of Javli:
                - Shivaji became the undisputed master of the Mavala region (highlands).
                - His path to Satara and the Konkan coastal strip was cleared.
                - The Mavali foot soldiers became a crucial component of his army.
                - He further strengthened his position by acquiring more hill forts near Poona.
        - Conflict with the Mughals and the Bijapur Sultanate (1657–1659)
            - In 1657, the Mughal invasion of Bijapur saved Shivaji from immediate conflict with Aurangzeb.
            - Taking advantage of the situation, Shivaji switched sides and made deep inroads into Mughal territory, looting wealth.
            - However, when Aurangzeb reached an agreement with Bijapur, he:
                - Pardoned Shivaji but remained distrustful of him.
                - Advised the Bijapur ruler to expel Shivaji from his conquered territories.
                - Suggested that if Bijapur employed Shivaji, he should be stationed in Karnataka, far from Mughal frontiers.
            - With Aurangzeb preoccupied in the north, Shivaji resumed conquests at Bijapur’s expense, expanding into:
                - Konkan (coastal strip between the Ghats and the sea).
                - Several hill forts in the region.
        - Confrontation with Afzal Khan (1659)
            - Alarmed by Shivaji’s rise, Bijapur sent Afzal Khan, a premier noble, with 10,000 troops to capture him.
            - Treachery was common in those days, and both Afzal Khan and Shivaji had used it before.
            - Shivaji’s forces, unaccustomed to open battle, hesitated to engage Afzal Khan’s superior army.
            - Afzal Khan invited Shivaji for a personal meeting, promising to secure his pardon from Bijapur’s court.
            - Aware of the trap, Shivaji arrived prepared and, in a cunning but daring act, murdered Afzal Khan (1659).
            - His forces then routed the leaderless Bijapuri army, capturing:
                - Goods
                - Equipment
                - Artillery
            - Flushed with victory, Shivaji’s troops:
                - Overran the fort of Panhala.
                - Advanced into South Konkan and Kolhapur, making extensive conquests.
        - Shivaji’s Growing Reputation and Aurangzeb’s Response
            - Shivaji’s daring exploits made him a legendary figure.
            - His reputation soared, and he was believed to have magical powers.
            - Many, including Maratha warriors and Afghan mercenaries, joined his army.
            - Aurangzeb, alarmed by Shivaji’s rise near the Mughal frontiers, ordered his governor of the Deccan, Shaista Khan, to launch an invasion.
        - War with Shaista Khan and Mughal Retaliation (1660–1663)
            - The conflict initially went badly for Shivaji:
                - In 1660, Shaista Khan occupied Poona and made it his headquarters.
                - Mughal forces seized North Konkan, despite facing stiff resistance from Maratha troops.
            - Shivaji, pushed into a corner, devised a bold counterattack:
                - In 1663, he infiltrated Shaista Khan’s camp in Poona under the cover of night.
                - He attacked Shaista Khan in his harem, killing:
                    - His son
                    - One of his captains
                - Shaista Khan was wounded but escaped.
            - This daring raid disgraced Shaista Khan, leading to:
                - Aurangzeb transferring him to Bengal.
                - Aurangzeb refusing him an audience, a significant humiliation at the time.
        - Attack on Surat (1664)
            - Shivaji followed up with another audacious move:
                - In 1664, he attacked Surat, the premier Mughal port.
                - His forces looted it extensively, returning with immense treasure.

    - Treaty of Purandar and Shivaji’s Visit to Agra
        - Appointment of Raja Jai Singh and the Siege of Purandar (1665)
            - After Shaista Khan’s failure, Aurangzeb assigned Raja Jai Singh of Amber, one of his most trusted advisers, to handle Shivaji.
            - Jai Singh was given full military and administrative authority, allowing him to bypass the Mughal viceroy in the Deccan and report directly to Aurangzeb.
            - Unlike his predecessors, Jai Singh:
                - Did not underestimate the Marathas.
                - Made careful diplomatic and military preparations.
                - Appealed to Shivaji’s rivals and opponents, including the Sultan of Bijapur, to isolate him.
            - Jai Singh marched to Poona and decided to strike at the heart of Shivaji’s domain—Fort Purandar, where Shivaji had lodged his family and treasure.
            - In 1665, Jai Singh besieged Purandar, successfully blocking all Maratha relief attempts.
            - With defeat imminent, Shivaji was forced to open negotiations, leading to the Treaty of Purandar.
        - Terms of the Treaty of Purandar (1665)
            - After intense negotiations, Shivaji and Jai Singh agreed to the following terms:
                - Surrender of Forts and Territory:
                    - Out of 35 forts, 23 forts (along with surrounding lands yielding 4 lakh huns annually) were to be surrendered to the Mughals.
                    - 12 forts (yielding 1 lakh huns annually) would remain under Shivaji’s control, provided he served and remained loyal to the Mughal throne.
                - Grant of Bijapuri Territory:
                    - Shivaji retained 4 lakh huns worth of Bijapuri Konkan territory, which he already controlled.
                    - The Mughals granted him additional 5 lakh huns worth of uplands (Balaghat), which Shivaji was expected to conquer himself.
                    - In return, Shivaji was to pay 40 lakh huns to the Mughals in installments.
                - Mansab for Sambhaji and Shivaji’s Military Role:
                    - Shivaji requested exemption from personal service.
                    - Instead, a mansab of 5000 was granted to his minor son, Sambhaji.
                    - Shivaji promised to join Mughal campaigns in the Deccan when required.
        - Mughal-Maratha Relations and the Failure of Jai Singh’s Strategy
            - Jai Singh’s strategy aimed to create a rift between Shivaji and Bijapur’s ruler, ensuring Shivaji remained loyal to the Mughals.
            - However, the success of this plan depended on Mughal support for Shivaji in reclaiming the lands surrendered to them from Bijapur.
            - This proved to be a fatal flaw:
                - Aurangzeb distrusted Shivaji and doubted the value of a joint Mughal-Maratha attack on Bijapur.
                - Jai Singh, on the other hand, had grander ambitions:
                    - He envisioned the conquest of Bijapur and the entire Deccan.
                    - He believed that once this was achieved, Shivaji would have no choice but to remain a Mughal ally.
                    - He wrote to Aurangzeb: “We shall hem Shivaji in like the center of a circle.”
            - However, the Mughal-Maratha expedition against Bijapur failed.
            - Shivaji, who had been assigned to capture Fort Panhala, was also unsuccessful.
            - Seeing his plan collapse, Jai Singh persuaded Shivaji to visit Aurangzeb in Agra, hoping:
                - A reconciliation between Shivaji and Aurangzeb.
                - Increased Mughal support for a renewed attack on Bijapur.
        - Shivaji’s Visit to Agra and His Dramatic Escape (1666)
            - Shivaji’s visit to Agra turned out to be a disaster:
                - He felt insulted when he was ranked among mansabdars of 3000—a rank previously granted to his minor son, Sambhaji.
                - During Aurangzeb’s birthday celebrations, the emperor did not grant Shivaji an audience, further humiliating him.
                - Angered by this treatment, Shivaji:
                    - Stormed out in protest.
                    - Refused imperial service—a shocking act never before seen at the Mughal court.
            - A strong faction at court urged Aurangzeb to punish Shivaji severely to maintain imperial dignity.
        - Aurangzeb’s Dilemma and Shivaji’s Escape
            - Since Shivaji had come to Agra on Jai Singh’s assurance, Aurangzeb sought Jai Singh’s advice.
            - Jai Singh advocated leniency, but before a decision could be made:
                - Shivaji dramatically escaped from detention in 1666.
                - The details of his escape are well known and need not be repeated here.
                - Aurangzeb blamed himself for his carelessness in allowing Shivaji to escape.
        - Impact of Shivaji’s Visit to Agra
            - The Agra incident marked a turning point in Mughal-Maratha relations.
            - Though Shivaji remained inactive for two years after his escape, it was now clear that:
                - Unlike Jai Singh, Aurangzeb placed little value on an alliance with Shivaji.
                - To Aurangzeb, Shivaji was merely a petty zamindar (landholder).
            - As subsequent events proved, Aurangzeb’s failure to recognize Shivaji’s importance and refusal to forge an alliance with him were among the greatest political mistakes of his reign.

    - Shivaji: Conquests, Administration, and Legacy
        - Renewed Conflict with the Mughals and Bijapur
            - Aurangzeb’s rigid interpretation of the Treaty of Purandar, despite its practical failure following the unsuccessful expedition against Bijapur, provoked Shivaji into resuming his conquests. 
            - The loss of 23 forts and territory worth four lakh hons annually to the Mughals, without compensation from Bijapur, was unacceptable to Shivaji.
                - In 1670, he sacked Surat for the second time.
                - Over the next four years, he reclaimed several forts, including Purandar, and made deep inroads into Mughal territories (especially Berar and Khandesh).
                - The Mughal preoccupation with the Afghan uprising in the northwest gave Shivaji an advantage.
                - He also resumed conflict with Bijapur, securing Panhala and Satara through bribes and raiding the Kanara region.
        - Coronation at Raigarh (1674)
            - Shivaji’s formal coronation at Raigarh in 1674 marked his transformation from a petty jagirdar of Poona to the most powerful Maratha chief. Given the extent of his dominions and the size of his army, he could claim status equal to that of the weakened Deccani sultans.
            - The coronation served multiple purposes:
                - Political Legitimacy: Elevated him above other Maratha chiefs, some of whom saw him as an upstart.
                - Social Status: Strengthened his position by marrying into powerful Maratha families like the Mohites and Shirkes.
                - Religious Validation: Gaga Bhatta, the presiding priest, declared him a high-class Kshatriya.
                - Diplomatic Recognition: Enabled him to sign treaties with Deccani sultans as an independent ruler rather than a rebel.
                - Maratha Nationalism: Strengthened Maratha identity and aspirations.
        - Karnataka Expedition (1676-1679)
            - Shivaji embarked on an ambitious Karnataka campaign in 1676, with the support of Madanna and Akhanna, ministers at Hyderabad.
                - Alliance with the Qutb Shah:
                    - Shivaji was welcomed at Golconda, and a treaty was signed.
                    - The Qutb Shah agreed to pay one lakh hons (₹5 lakh) annually and host a Maratha ambassador.
                    - Joint sharing of territory and booty.
                    - The Qutb Shah supplied troops, artillery, and financial aid.
                - Military Success:
                    - Captured Jinji and Vellore from Bijapuri officials.
                    - Conquered lands controlled by his half-brother Ekoji.
                - Breach of Trust:
                    - Despite claiming the title of Haindava-Dharmoddharak (Protector of Hindu Faith), Shivaji plundered the local Hindu population.
                    - Refused to share the loot with the Qutb Shah, leading to strained relations.
            - The Karnataka expedition was Shivaji’s last major campaign. The fort at Jinji, which he fortified, later served as a crucial refuge for his son Rajaram during Aurangzeb’s war on the Marathas.
        - Administrative System            
            - Shivaji established a sound administrative system, largely inspired by the Deccani states.
            - Ashtapradhan Council (Ministers)
                - Although he appointed eight ministers (Ashtapradhan), they were directly responsible to the ruler, not functioning as a collective council.
                    - Peshwa – Finance and general administration.
                    - Sar-i-Naubat (Senapati) – Military commander (mostly honorary).
                    - Majumdar – Accountant.
                    - Waqia Navis – Intelligence, postal services, and household affairs.
                    - Surunavis (Chitnis) – Official correspondence.
                    - Dabir – Foreign affairs and ceremonial duties.
                    - Nyayadhish – Judiciary.
                    - Panditrao – Religious and charitable affairs.
        - Army Organization
            - Cash salaries were preferred, though revenue grants (saranjam) were sometimes awarded.
            - Strict discipline:
                - Women and dancing girls were not allowed in the army.
                - Plunder was strictly accounted for.
            - Regular cavalry (Paga): 30,000–40,000 troops, commanded by Havaldars (fixed salaries).
            - Forts were strategically supervised, guarded by Mavali foot soldiers and gunners.
            - Anti-treachery measures: Three officers of equal rank managed each fort.
        - Revenue System
            - Modeled after Malik Ambar’s reforms.
            - A new revenue assessment was completed by Annaji Datto in 1679.
            - Contrary to some beliefs, Shivaji did not abolish the zamindari (deshmukhi) system nor completely eliminate jagirs (mokasa).
            - Mirasdars (hereditary landowners) were strictly supervised:
                - Sabhasad, an 18th-century chronicler, noted that mirasdars paid the government only a fraction of their revenue.
                - Many had built forts, enlisted musketeers, and defied authority.
                - Shivaji demolished their strongholds and forced submission.
            - Additional Revenue: Chauth System
                - Imposed a one-fourth (chauth) tax on Mughal territories as a tribute.
        - Legacy and Final Years
            - Shivaji passed away in 1680, shortly after returning from the Karnataka campaign. However, his legacy was far-reaching:
                - Military Genius:
                    - Master of guerrilla warfare and rapid troop movement.
                    - His army was highly mobile and efficient.
                    - Relied heavily on plunder for military funding.
                - State-Building:
                    - Though war-driven, his kingdom was not merely a “war-state”.
                    - His rule had a popular base and represented local resistance against Mughal encroachments.
                    - By curbing the deshmukhs’ power, he laid the foundation for a strong Maratha state.
            - Shivaji’s leadership, diplomacy, and military acumen ensured that the Marathas remained a dominant force even after his death, influencing Indian history for centuries to come.

    - Aurangzeb and the Deccan States: Phases of Relations
        - The relations between Aurangzeb and the Deccan states can be divided into three distinct phases:
            1.	First Phase (Until 1668):
                - The primary objective was to recover from Bijapur the territories of the former Ahmadnagar state, which had been surrendered to it by the treaty of 1636.
            2.	Second Phase (1668–1684):
                - The main threat in the Deccan during this period was the Marathas.
                - The Mughals pressured Bijapur and Golconda to align with them against Shivaji and later his son, Sambhaji.
                - While doing so, the Mughals gradually encroached upon the territories of the Deccan states, aiming to bring them under complete Mughal control.
            3.	Final Phase (1684 Onwards):
                - Aurangzeb abandoned hopes of securing the cooperation of Bijapur and Golconda against the Marathas.
                - Convinced that the destruction of the Marathas required the conquest of these states first, he decided to subjugate Bijapur and Golconda entirely.
        - Mughal Expansion and Treaty Violations
            - The Treaty of 1636, under which Shah Jahan had ceded one-third of the Ahmadnagar territories to Bijapur and Golconda as a bribe to keep them from supporting the Marathas, included a Mughal promise “never ever” to conquer these states. However, Shah Jahan himself disregarded this agreement.
                - In 1657-58, both Golconda and Bijapur faced Mughal threats:
                - Golconda was forced to pay a hefty indemnity.
                - Bijapur had to surrender the Nizam Shahi territories granted to it in 1636.
            - Justification for Mughal Actions
                - The Mughals justified these aggressive moves by arguing that:
                    - Both states had expanded significantly in Karnataka.
                    - Since they were considered Mughal vassals, the Mughals were entitled to “compensation” for their conquests, which had been possible only due to Mughal neutrality.
                - However, the real reason was financial:
                    - Maintaining Mughal armies in the Deccan was costly.
                    - The revenue from Mughal-controlled territories was insufficient.
                    - For a long time, the shortfall was covered by subsidies from Malwa and Gujarat treasuries.
            - Consequences of Mughal Expansion
                - The policy of limited expansion in the Deccan had far-reaching consequences, which neither Shah Jahan nor Aurangzeb fully grasped:
                    - It permanently eroded trust in Mughal treaties and assurances.
                    - A “union of hearts” against the Marathas became impossible.
                    - Despite Aurangzeb’s relentless efforts for 25 years, the policy brought little success.
        - The First Phase of Aurangzeb’s Deccan Policy (1658–1668)
            - Upon ascending the throne, Aurangzeb faced two major challenges in the Deccan:
                1.	The rising power of Shivaji.
                2.	Persuading Bijapur to relinquish the territories it had acquired under the Treaty of 1636.
            - Initial Gains and the Mughal Strategy
                - Kalyani and Bidar had been secured in 1657.
                - Parenda was obtained through bribery in 1660.
                - However, Sholapur remained under Bijapur’s control.
                - After his accession, Aurangzeb ordered Jai Singh to take action against both Shivaji and Adil Shah of Bijapur.
                    - This directive reflected Aurangzeb’s overconfidence in Mughal military superiority and his underestimation of his opponents.
                    - However, Jai Singh, an astute politician, warned Aurangzeb:
                        “It would be unwise to attack both these foes at the same time.”
                - Jai Singh was the only Mughal statesman of this period who advocated an aggressive forward policy in the Deccan.
                    - He believed that the Maratha problem could not be resolved without Mughal expansion in the region—a conclusion Aurangzeb himself would reach two decades later.
            - The Plan to Invade Bijapur and Aurangzeb’s Reluctance
                - While planning his invasion of Bijapur, Jai Singh wrote to Aurangzeb:
                    “The conquest of Bijapur is the preface to the conquest of all Deccan and Karnataka.”
                - However, Aurangzeb hesitated to commit to this bold strategy. His reluctance was due to:
                    - The threat posed by the ruler of Iran in the northwest.
                    - The long and arduous nature of a Deccan campaign, which would require the emperor’s personal presence.
                    - The risk of leaving a large Mughal army under the command of an ambitious noble or prince, a lesson Shah Jahan had learned to his misfortune.
                    - Shah Jahan’s continued existence, which restricted Aurangzeb from embarking on a distant military campaign.
            - Jai Singh’s Failed Bijapur Campaign (1665)
                - With limited resources at his disposal, Jai Singh’s Bijapur campaign was doomed to fail.
                    - The Deccan states united against the Mughals, as the Qutb Shah of Golconda sent a large force to aid Bijapur.
                    - The Deccanis employed guerrilla tactics:
                    - They lured Jai Singh’s forces towards Bijapur.
                    - They devastated the countryside, cutting off Mughal supplies.
                - Jai Singh’s campaign suffered from logistical shortcomings:
                    - He lacked siege artillery.
                    - A prolonged siege was impossible.
                - His eventual retreat proved costly, yielding neither money nor additional territory.
                    - The failure of this campaign, coupled with Aurangzeb’s criticisms, led to Jai Singh’s death in 1667.
            - End of the First Phase (1668)
                - In 1668, the Mughals secured the surrender of Sholapur through bribery, marking the end of the first phase of Mughal expansion in the Deccan.
        - The Second Phase of Aurangzeb’s Deccan Policy (1668–1684)
            - Mughal Inactivity and the Rise of Golconda’s Leadership (1668–1676)
                - Between 1668 and 1676, the Mughals largely remained inactive in the Deccan. 
                    - During this period, a new political dynamic emerged with the rise of Madanna and Akhanna, two talented brothers who virtually controlled the administration of Golconda from 1672 until its annexation in 1687.
                - Their primary objective was to establish a tripartite alliance between:
                    1.	Golconda
                    2.	Bijapur
                    3.	Shivaji’s Marathas
                - However, this alliance was frequently disrupted due to:
                    - Factional conflicts within Bijapur
                    - Shivaji’s growing ambitions, which led him to alternately loot Bijapur and then support it against the Mughals
            - Mughal Attempts to Influence Bijapur
                - The factions at Bijapur pursued inconsistent policies, aligning either with or against the Mughals based on their immediate interests.
                - Despite his concerns over the growing Maratha power, Aurangzeb was reluctant to expand Mughal territories in the Deccan.
                - Instead, he pursued a policy of manipulating Bijapur’s internal politics to:
                    - Install a pro-Mughal faction
                    - Weaken Shivaji’s influence
                    - Ensure Bijapur remained independent of Golconda’s control
                - To achieve this, the Mughals launched several military interventions, but these efforts only strengthened the unity of the three Deccan states against the Mughals.
            - Diler Khan’s Failed Siege of Bijapur (1679–1680)
                - In a final, desperate attempt, Diler Khan, the Mughal viceroy, led a campaign to capture Bijapur in 1679–1680. However, the Mughals were outmatched by the combined strength of the Deccan states.
                - A new factor in the conflict was the Karnataki matchlock-armed foot soldiers.
                    - 30,000 of these troops, sent by Berar chief Prem Naik, played a decisive role in repelling the Mughal siege of Bijapur.
                    - Additionally, Shivaji dispatched a large force to aid Bijapur and launched raids into Mughal territories.
            - End of the Second Phase
                - Diler Khan’s campaign failed, exposing Mughal territories to repeated Maratha incursions.
                - As a result, he was recalled by Aurangzeb, marking the end of this phase of Mughal expansion in the Deccan.
        - The Final Phase of Aurangzeb’s Deccan Campaign (1681–1707)
            - Aurangzeb’s Renewed Efforts (1681–1684)
                - Between 1676 and 1680, the Mughals achieved little success in the Deccan. When Aurangzeb arrived in the region in 1681 while pursuing his rebel son, Prince Akbar, he initially focused on:
                    - Targeting Sambhaji, the son and successor of Shivaji.
                    - Attempting to detach Bijapur and Golconda from their alliance with the Marathas.
                - However, these efforts failed, much like before. The Deccan states viewed the Marathas as their only shield against Mughal expansion and refused to abandon them.
            - The Fall of Bijapur (1686)
                - Frustrated with diplomatic failures, Aurangzeb decided to force Bijapur into submission. He issued a set of demands to the Adil Shah, treating him as a Mughal vassal:
                    - Supply provisions to the Mughal army.
                    - Allow free passage for Mughal forces through Bijapur’s territory.
                    - Provide 5,000–6,000 cavalry to assist the Mughals against the Marathas.
                    - Expel Sharza Khan, a prominent anti-Mughal noble in Bijapur.
                - These demands led to open conflict. The Adil Shah sought military support from both Golconda and Sambhaji, who responded immediately. 
                    - However, even the combined strength of the Deccan states could not withstand the full might of Aurangzeb’s army, especially with the emperor himself leading the campaign.
                - Despite their resistance, Bijapur fell in 1686 after an 18-month siege, with Aurangzeb personally present in the final stages. 
                    - The length and difficulty of the siege justified the earlier failures of Jai Singh (1665) and Diler Khan (1679–1680) against Bijapur.
            - The Fall of Golconda (1687)
                - With Bijapur defeated, an attack on Golconda became inevitable. Aurangzeb saw multiple “offenses” in the Qutb Shah’s rule:
                    - He had placed supreme power in the hands of two Hindus, Madanna and Akhanna.
                    - He had supported Shivaji on multiple occasions.
                    - He had openly defied Aurangzeb’s warnings.
                - In 1685, despite stiff resistance, the Mughals occupied Golconda, and the Qutb Shah offered peace, agreeing to:
                    - Pay a large indemnity to Aurangzeb.
                    - Cede certain territories.
                    - Dismiss Madanna and Akhanna.
                - The Qutb Shah complied, and Madanna and Akhanna were brutally murdered in 1686. However, even this betrayal failed to save him. 
                    - The Mughals besieged Golconda again in early 1687, and after six months of fierce resistance, the fort fell due to treachery and bribery.
            - The Maratha Resistance (1689–1690)
                - With Bijapur and Golconda destroyed, Aurangzeb turned his full attention to crushing the Marathas.
                - In 1689, a Mughal force captured Sambhaji at his secret hideout in Sangameshwar. He was paraded before Aurangzeb and executed as a rebel and an infidel. 
                    - This decision proved to be a major political mistake. Instead of securing Mughal control, Sambhaji’s execution:
                        - Eliminated a potential negotiating partner who might have submitted.
                        - Turned the Marathas into an even more decentralized and resilient force.
                        - Gave them a renewed cause for resistance.
                - The Maratha sardars, without a single central authority, began launching guerilla warfare—plundering Mughal territories, vanishing at the approach of Mughal forces, and regrouping elsewhere.
                - Rajaram, Sambhaji’s younger brother, was crowned king but had to flee to Jinji on the eastern coast when the Mughals attacked his capital.
